\int_{\Lambda:|x-x_{i}|>a}{1\over|x-x_{i}|^{3-\varepsilon}}dx
is minimal when
x_{i}
is on the boundary of the sphere
\Lambda
and we can bound

\displaystyle\int_{\Lambda:|x-x_{i}|>a}{1\over|x-x_{i}|^{3-\varepsilon}}dx

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi/4}\int_{a}^{L}{r^{2}\over r^{3-\varepsilon}}drd\theta d\phi

\displaystyle={\pi\over 2\varepsilon}\left[L^{\varepsilon}-a^{\varepsilon}\right]

\displaystyle\geq~{\pi\over 4\varepsilon}L^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\geq{|\Lambda|^{\varepsilon\over 3}\over 3\varepsilon}

and thus

U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{N})\geq{N\over 6}{1\over\varepsilon}|\Lambda|^{\varepsilon\over 3}\rho

and so

\displaystyle P_{\rm good}(N)

\displaystyle={1\over Z_{\Lambda}(\beta,N)}\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{N}{1\over N!}e^{-\beta U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{N})}

\displaystyle\leq{1\over Z_{\Lambda}(\beta,N)}|\Lambda|^{N}{1\over N!}e^{-\beta{N\over 6}{1\over\varepsilon}|\Lambda|^{\varepsilon\over 3}\rho}

Therefore the ratio between the probability of bad and good configurations is now

\displaystyle{P_{\rm bad}(N)\over P_{\rm good}(N)}

\displaystyle\geq{\left[{4\over 3}\pi\delta^{3}\right]^{N}e^{-\beta{N\over 2}(1+\eta_{\Delta}){4\pi\over\varepsilon}|\Lambda_{\rm cp}|^{\varepsilon\over 3}\rho_{\rm cp}}\over|\Lambda|^{N}e^{-\beta{N\over 6}{1\over\varepsilon}|\Lambda|^{\varepsilon\over 3}\rho}}

\displaystyle=~\left[{4\pi\delta^{3}\over 3|\Lambda|}\right]^{N}e^{\beta{N^{1+{\varepsilon\over 3}}\over\varepsilon}\left[4\pi(1+\eta_{\Delta})\rho_{\rm cp}^{4\over 3}-{\rho^{4\over 3}\over 6}\right]}

where recall that
|\Lambda|=\rho N
and
|\Lambda_{\rm cp}|=\rho_{\rm cp}N
. Hence we get

\displaystyle{P_{\rm bad}(N)\over P_{\rm good}(N)}

\displaystyle\geq\left[{4\pi\delta^{3}\over 3|\Lambda|}\right]^{N}e^{\beta{N^{1+{\varepsilon\over 3}}\over\varepsilon}\left[4\pi(1+\eta_{\Delta})\rho_{\rm cp}^{4\over 3}-{\rho^{4\over 3}\over 6}\right]}

\displaystyle\geq\left[{4\pi\delta^{3}\over 3|\Lambda|}\right]^{N}e^{\beta{N^{1+{\varepsilon\over 3}}\over\varepsilon}\left[\rho_{\rm cp}^{4\over 3}-{\rho^{4\over 3}}\right]}

which diverges as
N\to\infty
.

Again physics gives us an example of potential such as (2.14). That is, the purely repulsive
Coulomb potential (for which
\varepsilon=2
) between charged particles
with the same
charge
. Indeed electrons in excess inside a conductor tend to
accumulate at the boundary of the conductor forming layers.

2.4
The Ruelle example

In Example 1,
V_{1}^{\rm bad}
was tempered but not stable. It was a potential
strictly negative at the origin. Therefore a necessary condition for a pair potential
V(x)
to be stable is

V(0)\geq 0
. In the two examples of the previous section we have seen that potentials with a hard-core
preventing that particles accumulate in an arbitrarily small region of
\mathbb{R}^{d}

but with a two slow decay at large distances (i.e non absolutely integrable and hence non regular) yield to non thermodynamic behaviors.
Failure of stability however can occur also for regular
potential which are strictly positive in the neighborhood of the origin. We will
consider here a very interesting and surprising example of a potential in
d=3
dimensions which is strongly
positive near the origin and regular according to Definition
2.2
, that nevertheless
is a non stable potential. This subtle example, originally due to
Ruelle, illustrates very well the intuitive fact that the stability condition is there to avoid the collapse
of many particles into a bounded region of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and it also
shows the key role played by the continuum where we have always the
possibility to put an arbitrary number of particles in a small region of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Let
R>0
and let
\delta>0
and let

V^{\rm bad}_{4}({x})~=~\begin{cases}11&{\rm if}~|{x}|<R-\delta\\
-1&{\rm if}~R-\delta\leq|{x}|\leq R+\delta\\
0&{\rm otherwise}\end{cases}

[formula]

This is clearly a regular potential (actually it is bounded and
finite
range
: particles at distances greater than
R+\delta
do
not interact at all). Neverthless, we will show that this is a non-stable
potential by proving that the grand canonical partition function
diverges for such a potential.

Figure 4. A catastrophic potential. The Ruelle potential

Fix an integer
n
and
let
\tilde{x}_{1},\dots,\tilde{x}_{n}
be sites of a face-centered
cubic lattice in three dimensions with nearest neighborhoods at
distance
R
. We recall that a face-centered cubic lattice is a lattice
whose unit cells are cubes, with lattice points
at the center of each face of the cube, as well as at the vertices.

Let
B(\tilde{x}_{1},\dots,\tilde{x}_{n})=\{\{i,j\}:|\tilde{x}_{i}-\tilde{x}_{j}|=R\}
the set of nearest neighborhood
bonds in
\tilde{x}_{1},\dots,\tilde{x}_{n}
and let

B_{n}~=~|B(\tilde{x}_{1},\dots,\tilde{x}_{n})|
the cardinality of this
set. Suppose that
\tilde{x}_{1},\dots,\tilde{x}_{n}
are arranged
in such way to maximize
B_{n}
, hence in a “close-packed”
configuration. We remind that in the face-centered cubic lattice
every site has
12
nearest neighborhoods. If we take

\tilde{x}_{1},\dots,\tilde{x}_{n}
to be close-packed, then if
n

is sufficiently large, the number of nearest neighborhoods bonds
are of the order
B_{n}\sim 6n
. In fact each site is the vertex of
12 nearest neighborhoods bonds, and each nearest neighborhood bond is
shared between two sites. If the fixed integer
n
is chosen sufficiently large then
it is surely possible to find a close-packed configuration

\tilde{x}_{1},\dots,\tilde{x}_{n}
in such way that

B_{n}>{11\over 2}n+\varepsilon

[formula]

for some
\varepsilon>0
. Suppose thus that
n
is chosen so large such that
the close-packed face centered configuration
\tilde{x}_{1},\dots,\tilde{x}_{n}
is such that (2.16) is satisfied. Then,

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}V_{4}^{\rm bad}(|\tilde{x}_{i}-\tilde{x}_{j}|)~=~nV_{4}^{\rm bad}(0)+2B_{n}V_{4}^{\rm bad}(R)~=~11n-2B_{n}<-2\varepsilon<0

Consider now the
function

\Phi:\mathbb{R}^{6n}\to\mathbb{R}:(y_{1},\dots,y_{n},z_{1},\dots,z_{n})\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}V_{4}^{\rm bad}(|y_{i}-z_{j}|)

We have that
\Phi(\tilde{x}_{1},\dots,\tilde{x}_{n},\tilde{x}_{1},\dots,\tilde{x}_{n})<-2\varepsilon
. Moreover, if

|y_{1}-\tilde{x}_{1}|<{\delta\over 2},~~~\dots,~~~|y_{n}-\tilde{x}_{n}|<{\delta\over 2},~~~|z_{1}-\tilde{x}_{1}|<{\delta\over 2},~~~\dots~~~|z_{n}-\tilde{x}_{n}|<{\delta\over 2}

with
\delta
being the constant appearing in
\clubsuit
2.10, then also

\Phi(y_{1},\dots,y_{n},z_{1},\dots,z_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j~=~1}^{n}V_{4}^{\rm bad}(|y_{i}-z_{j}|)<-2\varepsilon

[formula]

Let now
S_{\delta}^{i}~=~\{{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:|{x}-\tilde{x}_{i}|<\delta/2\}
be the open sphere
in

\mathbb{R}^{3}
with radius
\delta/2
and center in
\tilde{x}_{i}
and
define
\Lambda_{\delta}=\cup_{i=1}^{n}S_{\delta}^{i}
(
\Lambda_{\delta}
is of course a subset
of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
).

Let
s
be a positive integer and define
M_{s}
as the following subset of
\mathbb{R}^{3sn}

M_{s}~=~\{({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{sn})\in\mathbb{R}^{3sn}:|{x}_{(i-1)s+p}-\tilde{x}_{i}|<\delta/2,~~p=1,...,s~~i~=1,...,n\}

Namely,
({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{sn})\in M_{s}
means that the
sn
-uple

({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{sn})
is such that the first
s
variables

{x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{s}
of the
sn
-uple are all inside the sphere

S_{\delta}^{1}
, the variables
{x}_{s+1},\dots,{x}_{2s}
are all
inside the sphere
S_{\delta}^{2}
, the variables
{x}_{2s+1},\dots,{x}_{3s}
are all inside the sphere
S_{\delta}^{3}
, and so on until
the last
s
variables of the
sn
-uple, which are

{x}_{(n-1)s+1},\dots,{x}_{sn}
, and are all inside the sphere

S_{\delta}^{n}
.

Now, if
({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{sn})\in M_{s}
, then,

\displaystyle U({x}_{1},...,{x}_{sn})

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq sn}V_{4}^{\rm bad}(|{x}_{i}-{x}_{j}|)

\displaystyle={1\over 2}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{sn}\sum_{j=1}^{sn}V_{4}^{\rm bad}(|{x}_{i}-{x}_{j}|)-snV_{4}^{\rm bad}(0)\right]

\displaystyle={1\over 2}\left[\sum_{p=1}^{s}\sum_{p^{\prime}=1}^{s}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}V_{4}^{\rm bad}(|{x}_{(i-1)s+p}-{x}_{(j-1)s+p^{\prime}}|)-snV_{4}^{\rm bad}(0)\right]

Now, for fixed
p
and
p^{\prime}
call
{x}_{(i-1)s+p}=y_{i}
and

{x}_{(j-1)s+p^{\prime}}~=~z_{j}
. Then, by definition of
M_{s}
we have that

|y_{i}-\tilde{x}_{i}|<\delta/2
and
|z_{j}-\tilde{x}_{j}|<\delta/2
. Hence
by (2.17)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j~=~1}^{n}V_{4}^{\rm bad}(|{x}_{(i-1)s+p}-{x}_{(j-1)s+p^{\prime}}|)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j~=~1}^{n}V_{4}^{\rm bad}(|y_{i}-z_{j}|)

\displaystyle<-2\varepsilon

hence we conclude that

U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{sn})<-\left(s^{2}\varepsilon+{11\over 2}sn\right)~~~~~~~~~~{\rm whenever}~~({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{sn})\in M_{s}

Therefore, if
V_{\delta}
denote the volume of the sphere of radius

\delta/2
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
we have

\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)~=~1+\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}{\lambda^{N}\over N!}\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{N}~e^{-\beta U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{N})}\geq

\geq 1+\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}{\lambda^{sn}\over(sn)!}\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{sn}~e^{-\beta U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{sn})}\geq

\geq 1+\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}{\lambda^{sn}\over(sn)!}\int_{M_{s}}d{x}_{1}\dots d{x}_{sn}~e^{-\beta U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{sn})}~\geq

\geq~\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}{\lambda^{sn}\over(sn)!}V_{\delta}^{sn}~e^{{\beta}(s^{2}\varepsilon+{11\over 2}sn)}~=~\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}{\left[\lambda V_{\delta}e^{{11\beta\over 2}}\right]^{sn}\over(sn)!}\left(e^{\beta\varepsilon}\right)^{s^{2}}~=~\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}a_{s}

This last series is a series with positive terms whose term

s^{\rm th}
term is given by

a_{s}~=~{\left[\lambda V_{\delta}e^{{11\beta\over 2}}\right]^{sn}\over(sn)!}\left(e^{\beta\varepsilon}\right)^{s^{2}}

In this formula recall that
n
is a fixed value while
s
is the variable
integer. It is now easy to show that
\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}a_{s}

diverges. As a matter of fact, by the ratio test for positive term
series, we have that

\lim_{s\to\infty}{a_{s+1}\over a_{s}}~=~\lim_{s\to\infty}{(sn)!\over[(s+1)n)]!}\left[\lambda V_{\delta}e^{{11\beta\over 2}}\right]^{n}\left(e^{\beta\varepsilon}\right)^{2s+1}\geq

\geq\lim_{s\to\infty}\left[{\lambda V_{\delta}e^{{11\beta\over 2}}\over(s+1)n}\right]^{n}\left(e^{2\beta\varepsilon}\right)^{s}~=~\left[{\lambda V_{\delta}e^{{11\beta\over 2}}\over n}\right]^{n}\lim_{s\to\infty}{\left(e^{2\beta\varepsilon}\right)^{s}\over(s+1)^{n}}~=~+\infty

2.5
Admissible potentials

Let us start by discussing first three classes of potentials which are both tempered and
stable.

1 - “Repulsive” temperate potentials

V\geq 0~~~{\rm and}~~~~\int_{|x|\geq r_{0}}V(x)dx<+\infty~~~~~\mbox{for some $r_{0}\geq 0$}

2 - Positive type potentials: absolutely integrable potentials which are the Fourier Transform of a positive function

V({x})~=~\int\tilde{V}({\bf k})e^{i{\bf k}\cdot{x}}d^{3}{\bf k},~~~~~~\tilde{V}({\bf k})\geq 0

3 - Lennard-Jones Potentials: there exist strictly positive constants
a
,
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
and
\varepsilon
such that

V({x})\geq{C_{1}\over|{x}|^{3+\varepsilon}}~~{\rm for}~~x\leq a,~~~~|V({x})|\leq{C_{2}\over|{x}|^{3+\varepsilon}}~~{\rm for}~~x>a

The potentials in the class 1, which are automatically stable are called “repulsive” in a somewhat improper manner: a positive pair potential should be monotonically decreasing in order to be really purely repulsive.
We remark that tempered potentials with hard core (
V({x})~=~+\infty
if
|{x}|\leq a
) can be included
in case 3 or in case 1.

Figure 5. A Lennard-Jones potential

It is immediate to see that a potential of type 1 is admissible. It is tempered
(i.e. it satisfies (2.7)) by definition and it is stable,
since from
V({x})\geq 0
we get

U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})\geq 0~~~~~~\forall n,{x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n}

hence (2.6) is verified. Moreover, since
\inf_{n,{x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n}}U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})=0
, we have that
B_{V}~=~0
.

Let us show now that a potential of type 2 is admissible. A positive type
potential
V
is stable because

\displaystyle U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})

\displaystyle=~{1\over 2}\sum_{i\neq j}V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})

\displaystyle={1\over 2}\sum_{i,j}V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})-{n\over 2}V({0})

\displaystyle={1\over 2}\sum_{i,j}\int e^{{\rm i}{\bf k}\cdot({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})}\tilde{V}({\bf k})d^{3}{\bf k}-{n\over 2}V(0)

\displaystyle=~{1\over 2}\int\left[\sum_{i,j}e^{{\rm i}{\bf k}\cdot({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})}\right]\tilde{V}({\bf k})d^{3}{\bf k}-{n\over 2}V(0)

\displaystyle=~{1\over 2}\int\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}e^{{\rm i}{\bf k}\cdot{x}_{i}}\right|^{2}\tilde{V}({\bf k})d^{3}{\bf k}-{n\over 2}V(0)\geq-{n\over 2}V(0)

where the last inequality follows from the assumption
\tilde{V}({\bf k})\geq 0
.

Hence the stability condition
(2.6) is satisfied by choosing
B~=~{1\over 2}V(0)
. Potential 2
is tempered since it is supposed to admit Fourier transform, hence
it needs to be absolutely integrable.

The example 3, the Lennard Jones type potential, is of major interest in applications, since it is the most popular and used by physicists and chemists
to model the interactions between molecules in real gases.

Let us show that Lennard-Jones type potential is admissible.
Such a potential is indeed regular by definition, thus we have
just to show that it is also stable. In a given configuration

{x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n}
, denote with the number
r_{\rm min}~=~\min_{i\neq j}|{x}_{i}-{x}_{j}|
the minimum distance between
particles.

We distinguish two cases: 1)
r_{\rm min}<{a/2}
; and 2)
r_{\rm min}\geq{a/2}
.

Case 1)
r_{\rm min}<a/2
.
Suppose without loss of generality that
|{x}_{1}-{x}_{2}|=r_{\rm min}
and all other distances are at distances greater or equal
than
r_{\rm min}
. We have

\displaystyle U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})

\displaystyle=V({x}_{1}-{x}_{2})+\sum_{j=3}^{n}V({x}_{1}-{x}_{j})+U({x}_{2},\dots,{x}_{n})

\displaystyle\geq{C_{1}\over r_{\rm min}^{3+\varepsilon}}+\sum_{j\in\{3,\dots,n\}\atop|{x}_{1}-{x}_{j}|\geq a}V({x}_{1}-{x}_{j})+U({x}_{2},\dots,{x}_{n})

Note that the second sum of the last inequality above is over those
particles with index
j\in\{2,3,\dots,n\}
at distance greater or
equal to
a
. The inequality follows from the fact that
V({x}_{1}-{x}_{j})\geq 0
if
|{x}_{1}-{x}_{j}|<a
.

We will now get a lower bound for the term

\sum_{j\in\{3,\dots,n\}:~|{x}_{1}-{x}_{j}|\geq a}V({x}_{1}-{x}_{j})
. First note that, since in this sum
|{x}_{1}-{x}_{j}|\geq a

for all
j
, we can bound

\sum_{j\in\{3,\dots,n\}:\atop|{x}_{1}-{x}_{j}|\geq a}V({x}_{1}-{x}_{j})\geq-\sum_{j\in\{3,\dots,n\}:\atop|{x}_{1}-{x}_{j}|\geq a}{C_{2}\over|{x}_{1}-{x}_{j}|^{3+\varepsilon}}

We then proceed as follows. Draw around each
{x}_{j}
a cube
Q_{j}

with side
r_{\rm min}/{\sqrt{1}2}
(in such way that its maximal
diagonal is
r_{\rm min}/2
) with
{x}_{j}
being the vertex
farthest away from
{x}_{1}
. Since any two points among
{x}_{3},\dots,{x}_{n}
are at mutual distances
\geq r_{\rm min}
the cubes
so constructed do not overlap. Moreover, if we consider the open
sphere
S_{1}~=~\{{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:|{x}-{x}_{1}|<{a\over 2}\}
and
denote by
S_{1}^{c}
its complementar in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, then by
construction (recall that
r_{\rm min}<a/2
and
|x_{1}-x_{j}|\geq a
) all cubes
Q_{j}\subset S_{1}^{c}
, i.e., they lay outside the open sphere with center
{x}_{i}

and radius
a/2
. Furthermore, we have

{1\over|{x}_{1}-{x}_{j}|^{3+\varepsilon}}\leq{(\sqrt{12})^{3}\over{r_{\rm min}^{3}}}\int_{Q_{j}}{1\over|{x}_{1}-{x}|^{3+\varepsilon}}d{x}

recall in fact that the cube
Q_{j}
is chosen in such way that

|{x}-{x}_{1}|\leq|{x}_{1}-{x}_{j}|
for all
{x}\in Q_{j}
.
Therefore

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\{3,\dots,n\}:\atop|{x}_{1}-{x}_{j}|\geq a}^{n}V({x}_{1}-{x}_{j})

\displaystyle\geq-C_{2}{(\sqrt{12})^{3}\over{r_{\rm min}^{3}}}\sum_{j\in\{3,\dots,n\}:\atop|{x}_{1}-{x}_{j}|\geq a}^{n}\int_{Q_{j}}{1\over|{x}_{1}-{x}|^{3+\varepsilon}}d^{3}{x}

\displaystyle=-C_{2}{(\sqrt{12})^{3}\over{r_{\rm min}^{3}}}\int_{\cup_{j}Q_{j}}{1\over|{x}_{1}-{x}|^{3+\varepsilon}}d^{3}{x}

\displaystyle\geq-C_{2}{(\sqrt{12})^{3}\over{r_{\rm min}^{3}}}\int_{|{x}-{x}_{1}|\geq a/2}{1\over|{x}-{x}_{1}|^{3+\varepsilon}}d^{3}{x}

\displaystyle=-C_{2}{(\sqrt{12})^{3}\over{r_{\rm min}^{3}}}\int_{|y|\geq a/2}{1\over|y|^{3+\varepsilon}}d^{3}y

\displaystyle={C_{2}K_{a}(\varepsilon)\over r_{\rm min}^{3}}

where

K_{a}(\varepsilon)~=~{(\sqrt{12})^{3}}{4\pi 2^{\varepsilon}\over\varepsilon a^{\varepsilon}}

So

U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})~\geq~{C_{1}\over r_{\rm min}^{3+\varepsilon}}-{C_{2}K_{a}(\varepsilon)\over r_{\rm min}^{3}}+U({x}_{2},\dots,{x}_{n})

I.e.

U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})\geq-C_{\varepsilon}+U({x}_{2},\dots,{x}_{n})

where
-C_{\varepsilon}
is the minimum of the
function
F(x)={C_{1}\over x^{3+\varepsilon}}-{C_{2}K_{a}(\varepsilon)\over x^{3}}
for
x>0
.
Therefore, iterating this formula we
get

U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})\geq-C_{\varepsilon}n

Case 2)
r_{\rm min}\geq a/2
. In this case we write

U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})~=~{1\over 2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j\in\{1,2,\dots,n\}:\,\,j\neq i}V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})

and we will get an estimate
\sum_{j\in\{1,2,\dots,n\},\,j\neq i}V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})
. First we bound

\sum_{j\in\{1,2,\dots,n\}\atop j\neq i}V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})\geq-\sum_{j\in\{1,2,\dots,n\}\atop|x_{i}-x_{j}|\geq a}{C_{2}\over|{x}_{i}-{x}_{j}|^{3+\varepsilon}}

We then proceed analogously as before. This time, for fixed
i
,
we can draw around each
{x}_{j}
a cube
Q_{j}
with side
a/{\sqrt{4}8}
(such that the maximal diagonal of
Q_{j}
is
a/4
) with

{x}_{j}
being the vertex farthest away from
{x}_{i}
. Since any two
points among
{x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n}
are at mutual distances
\geq a/2
the cubes so constructed do not overlap. Moreover if we
consider the open sphere
S_{i}~=~\{{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:|{x}-{x}_{i}|<{a\over 2}\}
and denote by
S_{i}^{c}
its complementar
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, then by construction
\cup_{j\neq i}Q_{j}\subset S_{i}^{c}
. In other words all cubes
Q_{j}
lay outside the
open sphere with center
{x}_{i}
and radius
a/2
. Furthermore we
have

{1\over|{x}_{i}-{x}_{j}|^{3+\varepsilon}}\leq{(\sqrt{48})^{3}\over a^{3}}\int_{Q_{j}}{1\over|{x}_{i}-{x}|^{3+\varepsilon}}d^{3}{x}

recall in fact that the cube
Q_{j}
is chosen in such way that

|{x}-{x}_{i}|\leq|{x}_{i}-{x}_{j}|
for all
{x}\in Q_{j}
.

Therefore

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\{1,2,\dots,n\}\atop|x_{1}-x_{j}|\geq a}V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})

\displaystyle\geq~-C_{2}{(\sqrt{48})^{3}\over a^{3}}\sum_{j\in\{1,2,\dots,n\}\atop x_{1}-x_{j}|\geq a}\int_{Q_{j}}{1\over|{x}_{i}-{x}|^{3+\varepsilon}}d^{3}{x}

\displaystyle=-C_{2}{(\sqrt{48})^{3}\over a^{3}}\int_{\cup_{j\neq i}Q_{j}}{1\over|{x}_{i}-{x}|^{3+\varepsilon}}d^{3}{x}

\displaystyle\geq-C_{2}{(\sqrt{48})^{3}\over a^{3}}\int_{|{x}-x_{i}|\geq{a\over 2}}{1\over|{x}_{i}-{x}|^{3+\varepsilon}}d^{3}{x}

\displaystyle\geq~-{\bar{K}_{a}}(\varepsilon)

where

\bar{K}_{a}(\varepsilon)~=~C_{2}{(\sqrt{48})^{3}\over a^{3}}\int_{|{x}-x_{i}|\geq a/2}{1\over|{x}_{i}-{x}|^{3+\varepsilon}}d^{3}{x}

Hence

\sum_{j\in\{1,2,\dots,n\}\atop j\neq i}V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})~\geq~-{\bar{K}_{a}}(\varepsilon)

[formula]

and

U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})~\geq~-{\bar{K}_{a}(\varepsilon)\over 2}n

2.5.1
Basuev Criteria

We now present two very efficient criteria for stability of tempered potentials first proposed by Basuev
[
2
]
. To do this we need to itroduce the following notation.
Given a potential
V(x)
let

V(a)=\inf_{|x|=a}V(x)

[formula]

Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let us consider the set of configurations defined by

\mathcal{S}_{n}(a)=\{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{dn}:~|x_{i}-x_{j}|>a,~~~{\rm for}~1\leq i<j\leq n\}

I.e.
{\cal S}_{n}(a)
is formed by all configurations of
n
particles in
\mathbb{R}^{dn}
such that any two particles are at mutual
distance greater than
a
.

Definition 2.4

A potential
V(x)
is called Basuev if there exists
a>0
such that
V(a)>0
,

V(x)\geq V(a)
for all
|x|\leq a
and

V(a)\geq\mu(a)

[formula]

where

\mu(a)=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N},\atop{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathcal{S}_{n}(a)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}V^{-}(x_{i})

[formula]

with

V^{-}(x)=\max\{-V(x),0\}.

V(x) is called strongly Basuev if

V(a)\geq 2\mu(a)

[formula]

We now prove the following theorem.

Theorem 2.1

Let
V(x)
be a Basuev pair potential with Basuev constants
a
,
V(a)
and
\mu(a)
as in definition
2.4
.
Then
V(x)
is stable with stability constant
B_{V}
such that

B_{V}\leq\mu(a)

Remark
. Since
V(|x|)
is translational invariant, observe that for any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
it holds that

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N},\atop{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{dn}\atop|x_{i}-x_{j}|>a}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}V^{-}(x_{0}-x_{i})=\mu(a)

[formula]

Proof.
Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
be fixed and let us first consider the case in which we have a configuration of
n
particles in positions

x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
such that all
n
particles are mutually at distance greater than
a
, i.e let us consider first the set of configurations
in
{\cal S}_{n}(a)
.
We have that, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\inf_{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in{\cal S}_{n}(a)}{1\over n}U(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})

\displaystyle=\inf_{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in{\cal S}_{n}(a)}{1\over n}\sum_{1\leq k<s\leq n}V(x_{k}-x_{s})

\displaystyle=\inf_{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in{\cal S}_{n}(a)}{1\over 2n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{s\neq k}V(x_{k}-x_{s})

\displaystyle\geq-{1\over 2n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sup_{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in{\cal S}_{n}(a)}\sum_{s\neq k}V^{-}(x_{k}-x_{s})

\displaystyle\geq-{1\over 2n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sup_{s\in\mathbb{N}\atop{(x_{1},\dots,x_{s})\in{\cal S}_{n}(a)}}\sum_{j=1}^{s}V^{-}(x_{j})=-{\mu(a)\over 2}

where in the last inequality we have used (2.23) and (2.21).
We can therefore limit ourselves to configurations in which there are particles at distance
a
or smaller than
a
.
Consider thus a configuration
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
such that there exists
\{i,j\}\subset[n]
such that

|x_{i}-x_{j}|\leq a
. Thus there is a particle, which, without loss of generality, we can assume to be the particle indexed by 1 at position
x_{1}=0
(i.e. at the origin),
which has the maximum number of particles among
x_{2},\dots,x_{n}
at distance less than or equal
a
.
We have to estimate, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}

U(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=E_{1}+U(x_{2},\dots,x_{n})

where

E_{1}=\sum_{j=2}^{n}V(x_{j})

Say that the number of these particles close to
x_{1}
less or equal to
a
is
l
(clearly
l\geq 1
by assumption). The energy
E_{1}
of the particle at position
x_{1}

is thus

E_{1}\geq lV(a)-\sum_{k\in[n]\atop|x_{k}|>a}V^{-}(x_{k})

[formula]

To bound the sum

\sum_{k\in[n]\atop|x_{k}-x_{1}|>a}V^{-}(x_{k}))

observe that we are supposing that each particle has at most
l
other particles at distance less or equal than
a
.
Thus take the
k\in[n]
such that
V^{-}(x_{k})
is maximum. Again, without loss of generality we can suppose
k=2
. In the sphere with center
x_{2}
and radius
a
there are at most

l+1
particles (the particle at position
x_{2}
, for which the value
V^{-}(|x_{2}|)
is maximum plus at most
l
other particles, say
x_{i_{1}},\dots,x_{i_{l}}
, for which
V^{-}(|x_{i_{s}}|)\leq V^{-}(x_{2})
with
s\in[l]
).
Hence

\sum_{k\in[n]\atop|x_{k}-x_{1}|>a}V^{-}(|x_{k}|)\leq(l+1)V^{-}(x_{2})+\sum_{k\in[n]\atop|x_{k}-x_{1}|>a,|x_{k}-x_{2}|>a}V^{-}(x_{k})

Now we have to control the sum

\sum_{k\in[n]\atop|x_{k}-x_{1}|>a,|x_{k}-x_{2}|>a}V^{-}(|x_{k}|)

Note that in this sum all particles are at distance greater than
a
for the particle in
x_{1}

and also from particle at position
x_{2}
moreover each of the particle in the sum
has at most
l
particles at distance less or equal than
a
. Suppose without loss of generality
that the particle at position
x_{3}
is such that

V^{-}(x_{3})=\max_{k\in[n]\atop|x_{k}-x_{1}|>a,|x_{k}-x_{2}|>a}V^{-}(x_{k})

and this particle at
x_{3}
has at most
l
particles at distance less or equal than
a
. Therefore

\sum_{k\in[n]\atop|x_{k}-x_{1}|>a,|x_{k}-x_{2}|>a}V^{-}(x_{k})\leq(l+1)V^{-}(x_{3})+\sum_{k\in[n]\atop|x_{k}-x_{1}|>a,|x_{k}-x_{2}|>a,\,|x_{k}-x_{3}|>a}V^{-}(x_{k})

Iterating we get

\sum_{k\in[n]\atop|x_{k}-x_{1}|>a}V^{-}(x_{k})\leq(l+1)\sum_{k\in[n]\atop|x_{i}-x_{j}|>a}V^{-}(x_{k})

[formula]

where now in the sum in the r.h.s of
\clubsuit
lpu all pairs of particles are at distance greater than
a
to each other. Therefore, recalling definition (2.21)
we have that

\sum_{k\in[n]\atop|x_{i}-x_{j}|>a}V^{-}(x_{k})\leq\mu(a)

so that

\sum_{k\in[n]\atop|x_{k}-x_{1}|>a}V^{-}(x_{k})\leq(l+1)\mu(a)

Therefore, recalling (2.24), we have that

E_{1}\geq lV(a)-(l+1)\mu(a)=l(V(a)-\mu(a))-\mu(a)\geq-\mu(a)

So we have obtained

U(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\geq-\mu(a)+U(x_{2},\dots,x_{n})

Iterating we get

U(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\geq-n\mu(a)

\Box

Example
. Let us consider the potential

V({x})~=~\begin{cases}A&{\rm if}~|{x}|\leq R\\
-1&{\rm if}~R<|{x}|\leq R+\delta\\
0&{\rm otherwise}\end{cases}

[formula]

Let us prove that
V(x)
is stable if
A\geq 12
and
\delta
sufficiently small.

It is known that the maximum number of points that can be put on the surface of the sphere
S(R)
of radius

R
in such way that the distance between any pair of such points
is greater or equal
R
is 12. Let us now show that if
\delta
is sufficiently small 12 is also the maximum number of points that can be fitted in the layer
S_{\delta}(R)=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:R\leq|x|\leq R+\delta\}
.
Indeed, suppose by absurd that for any
\delta>0
one can find 13 points on
S_{\delta}
such that the distance between any pair of these points is greater or equal than
R
. Then by choosing
\delta={1\over n}
, for
n\in\mathbb{N}

one can
construct 13 sequences
x_{n}^{1},\dots,x_{n}^{13}
all contained in
S_{\delta}
such that, for any
n
and for any
i,j\in\{1,2,\dots,13\}
it holds that
|x_{n}^{i}-x_{n}^{j}|\geq R
since
S_{\delta}
is compact
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}S_{\delta}(R)=S(R)
, for each of the sequences

x^{i}_{n}
there exists a subsequence convergent to some point
y^{i}\in S(R)
such that for any pair
|y^{i}-y^{j}|\geq R
. So we have found 13 points of the sphere
S(R)

of radius
R
all at distance from one to another greater that
R
, contradicting the statement that the maximal number of such points is 12.
This means that the maximum number of points we can put on the layer
S_{\delta}
in such way that the distance between any pair of such points
is greater or equal
R
is 12 and therefore the potential of Figure 4 with 11 is replaced by any number
A\geq 12
and
\delta
is sufficiently small satisfies the hypothesis of Theorem
2.1
and hence is stable.

Theorem 2.2

Let
V(x)
be a strongly Basuev and tempered pair potential, with costants
a
,
V(a)
and
\mu(a)
as in definition
2.4
.
Then
V(x)
is stable with stability constant
B_{V}\leq{1\over 2}\mu(a)
. Moreover the representation

V(x)=V_{a}(x)+K_{a}(x)

with

V_{a}(x)=\begin{cases}V(a)&{\rm if}~|x|\leq a\\
V(x)&{\rm if}~|x|>a\end{cases}

[formula]

and

K_{a}(x)=\begin{cases}V(x)-V(a)&{\text{i}f}~|x|\leq a\\
0&{\text{i}f}~|x|>a\end{cases}

[formula]

is such that
the potential

V_{a}(x)
defined in (2.27) is also stable and it has the same stability constant
B_{V}
of the full potential
V(x)
and
the potential
K_{a}(x)
is positive
and supported in
[0,a]
.

Proof.
The thesis is trivial if
V^{-}(x)=0
(i.e. if
V(x)
is purely repulsive). So we may assume that
V^{-}(x)\neq 0
.
For any
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{nd}
and any
i\in[n]
, let

E_{i}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\sum_{j\in[n]:\,j\neq i}V(x_{i}-x_{j})

so that
U(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})={1\over 2}\sum_{i\in[n]}E_{i}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
.
Note that when
V^{-}\neq 0
then surely
B>0
and thus configurations
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
which minimize the energy must be searched
between those such that
U(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})<0
.
Let now

(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{nd}
be a configuration such that
U(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})<0

but there is a particle in position say
x_{1}
(without loss of generality) such that
E_{1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\geq 0
. Then

U(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=E_{1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})+U(x_{2},\dots,x_{n})

and, since we are assuming
E_{1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\geq 0
and
U(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})<0
, we have that
U(x_{2},\dots,x_{n})<0
and

U(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\geq U(x_{2},\dots,x_{n})

i.e.

-U(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\leq-U(x_{2},\dots,x_{n})

with both sides of the last inequality positive.
Therefore, since
{1\over n}<{1\over n-1}
,

-{1\over n}U(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})<-{1\over n-1}U(x_{2},\dots,x_{n})

Thus the configuration
(x_{2},\dots,x_{n})
produces a value
-{1\over n-1}U(x_{2},\dots,x_{n})
which is nearer to
B_{V}
than

-U(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})/n
. Whence we can look for minimal energy configurations
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
limiting ourselves to those configurations in which
the energy per particle
E_{i}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
is negative for all
i\in[n]
.

Now let us consider the system of
particles interacting via the pair potential
V_{a}(x)
defined in (2.27) and let us
assume that conditions (2.20) and (2.22) hold. Note first that, recalling definition (2.27),

V_{a}^{-}(|x|)=\max\{0,-V_{a}(x)\}=V^{-}(x)
.
Consider then a configuration
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
such that there exists
\{i,j\}\subset[n]
such that

|x_{i}-x_{j}|\leq a
. Thus there is at least a particle, (which, without loss of generality, we can assume to be the particle indexed by 1 at position
x_{1}
),
which has the maximum number of particles among
x_{2},\dots,x_{n}
at distance less than or equal than
a
.
Say that the number of these particles close to
x_{1}
less or equal to
a
is
l
(clearly
l\geq 1
by assumption). The energy
E_{1}
of the particle at position
x_{1}

can thus be estimated as follows.

E_{1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\geq lV(a)-\sum_{k\in[n]\atop|x_{k}-x_{1}|>a}V^{-}(x_{k})

To control the sum

\sum_{k\in[n]\atop|x_{k}-x_{1}|>a}V^{-}(x_{k}))

observe that we are supposing that each particle has at most
l
other particles at distance less or equal than
a
.
Thus take the
k\in[n]
such that
V^{-}(x_{k})
is maximum. Again, without loss of generality we can suppose
k=2
. In the sphere with center
x_{2}
and radius
a
there are at most

l+1
particles (the particle at position
x_{2}
, for which the value
V^{-}(|x_{2}|)
is maximum plus at most
l
other particles).
Hence

\sum_{k\in[n]\atop|x_{k}-x_{1}|>a}V^{-}(x_{k}|)\leq(l+1)V^{-}(x_{2})+\sum_{k\in[n]\atop|x_{k}-x_{1}|>a,|x_{k}-x_{2}|>a}V^{-}(x_{k})

Now we have to control the sum

\sum_{k\in[n]\atop|x_{k}-x_{1}|>a,|x_{k}-x_{2}|>a}V^{-}(x_{k})

Note that in this sum all particles are at distance greater than
a
for the particle in
x_{1}

and also from the particle at position
x_{2}
. Moreover,s each of the particle in the sum
has at most
l
particles at distance less or equal than
a
. Suppose without loss of generality
that the particle at position
x_{3}
is such that

V^{-}(x_{3})=\max_{k\in[n]\atop|x_{k}-x_{1}|>a,|x_{k}-x_{2}|>a}V^{-}(x_{k})

and this particle at
x_{3}
has at most
l
particles at distance less or equal than
a
. Therefore

\sum_{k\in[n]\atop|x_{k}-x_{1}|>a,|x_{k}-x_{2}|>a}V^{-}(x_{k})\leq(l+1)V^{-}(x_{3})+\sum_{k\in[n]\atop|x_{k}-x_{1}|>a,|x_{k}-x_{2}|>a,\,|x_{k}-x_{3}|>a}V^{-}(x_{k})

Iterating we get

\sum_{k\in[n]\atop|x_{k}-x_{1}|>a}V^{-}(x_{k})\leq(l+1)\sum_{k\in[n]\atop|x_{i}-x_{j}|>a}V^{-}(x_{k})

[formula]

where now in the sum in the r.h.s of (2.29) all pairs of particles are at distance greater than
a
to each other. Therefore, recalling definition (2.21)
we have that

\sum_{k\in[n]\atop|x_{i}-x_{j}|>a}V^{-}(x_{k})\leq\mu(a)

and hence

E_{1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\geq lV(a)-(l+1)\mu(a)

I.e. we have that
E_{1}>0
whenever

V(a)>{l+1\over l}\mu(a)

Using now assumption (2.22) and since
{l+1\over l}<2
we get

E_{1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})>0

The conclusion is that if a configuration
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
is such that some particles are at distance less or equal than
a

then there is at least a particle whose energy is positive. Hence the minimal energy configurations for
V_{a}(x)
must be searched among
those configurations in which all particles are at distance greater than
a
from each other. But for these configurations
V_{a}(|x|)=V(|x|)
which
implies that
V_{a}
and
V
, if stable, have the same stability constant
B
(and hence also the same
\bar{B}
). It is now easy to see that

V_{a}(|x|)
is stable.
Indeed observe that, for any configuration
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
for which particles are at distance greater than
a
from each other we have

U_{a}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})={1\over 2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j\in[n]\atop j\neq i}V(x_{i}-x_{j})\geq-{1\over 2}n\mu(a)

which implies that
V_{a}(x)
is stable with stability constant
B\leq{\mu(a)\over 2}
.
\Box

We have shown above that a Lennard-Jones type potential
V(|x|)
is
stable. Here below we prove that a Lennard-Jones
type potential is also Basuev.

Theorem 2.3

Let
V(x)
be a pair potential on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
there exist constants
w,r_{1},r_{2}>0
, with
r_{1}\leq r_{2}
, and non-negative monotonic decreasing functions

\xi(|x|)
,
\eta(|x|)
such that

V(x)\begin{cases}\geq\xi(|x|)&{\rm if}~|x|\leq r_{1}\\
\geq-w&{\rm if}~r_{1}<|x|<r_{2}\\
\geq-\eta(|x|)&{\rm if}~|x|\geq r_{2}\end{cases}

[formula]

with

\lim_{x\to 0}\xi(x)x^{d}=+\infty

[formula]

and

\int_{|x|\geq r_{2}}\eta(|x|)dx<+\infty

[formula]

Then
V(x)
is strongly Basuev (i.e. it satisfies Theorem
2.2
).

Proof.
By Theorem
2.2
we just need to show that there exists
a
such that (2.20) and (2.22) are satisfied. Fix
a\in(0,r_{1})
,
let
\bar{w}=\max\{w,\eta(r_{2})\}

\bar{\eta}(|x|)=\begin{cases}\eta(|x|)&{\text{i}f}~|x|>r_{2}\\
\bar{w}&{\text{i}f}~|x|\leq r_{2}\end{cases}

Then, by construction
\bar{\eta}(|x|)
is monotonic decreasing and such that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bar{\eta}(|x|)\leq\infty
.
Moreover by conditions (2.30) we have that

V^{-}(x)\leq\bar{\eta}(|x|)

Hence, recalling (2.21) and considering also that, since we took
a\in(0,r_{1})
, by hypothesis
V^{-}(|x|)=0
for all
|x|\leq a
, we have

\mu(a)\leq\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N},~(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{dn}\atop|x_{i}-x_{j}|>a,~|x_{i}|>a}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{\eta}(|x_{i}|)

To estimate from above
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{\eta}(|x_{i}|)
, having in mind that all particles are at mutual
distances greater than
a
and are at
distance greater than
a
from the origin, we proceed as follows.
We draw for each
x_{j}
a hypercube
Q_{j}
with side
a/2\sqrt{d}

(such that the maximal diagonal of
Q_{j}
is
a/2
) in such a way that

x_{j}
is a vertex of the cube
Q_{j}
and at the same time is the point
x\in Q_{j}
which is the farthest away from the origin
0
. Since any two
points among
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
are at mutual distances
\geq a
the cubes so constructed do not overlap. Furthermore, using the fact that
\bar{\eta}(|x|)
is monotonic decreasing, we
have

\bar{\eta}(|x_{j}|)\leq{(4d)^{d\over 2}\over a^{d}}\int_{Q_{j}}\bar{\eta}(|x|)dx

recall in fact that the cube
Q_{j}
is chosen in such way that

|x|\leq|x_{j}|
for all
x\in Q_{j}
.
Therefore

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{\eta}(|x_{i}|)

\displaystyle\leq{(4d)^{d\over 2}\over a^{d}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{Q_{i}}\bar{\eta}(|x|)dx

\displaystyle={(4d)^{d\over 2}\over a^{d}}\int_{\cup_{i}Q_{i}}\bar{\eta}(|x|)dx

\displaystyle\leq{{(4d)^{d\over 2}\over a^{d}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bar{\eta}(|x|)dx

\displaystyle={C_{d}\over a^{d}}

where

C_{d}=~{(4d)^{d\over 2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bar{\eta}(|x|)dx
.
Hence
we get

\mu(a)\leq{C_{d}\over a^{d}}

Now, in view of condition (2.31), we can always choose
a
such that
\xi(a)a^{d}>2C_{d}
. Thus we get

\xi(a)a^{d}>2C_{d}~~~\Longrightarrow~~\xi(a)>2{C_{d}\over a^{d}}~~~\Longrightarrow~~~V(a)>2{C_{d}\over a^{d}}~~~\Longrightarrow~~~V(a)>2\mu(a)

\Box

Exercise: prove that a Lennard-Jones pair potential satisfies Theorem
2.3
and hence Theorem
2.2
(thus a Lennard-Jones potential can be written as the sum
of a positive potential plus an absolutely integrable and stable potential with stability constant
B_{V}
equal to the one of the full potential).

2.6
The infinite volume limit

We will now start to consider the problem of the existence of the thermodynamic limit

for the pressure of a system of particles in the grand canonical
ensemble interacting via a pair potential stable and tempered. The
mathematical problem is to show the existence of the infinite
volume pressure defined as

\beta p(\beta,\lambda)~=~\lim_{\Lambda\to\infty}{1\over|\Lambda|}\ln\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)

[formula]

where we recall that
|\Lambda|
denotes the volume of
\Lambda
.

First of all we need to give a mathematical meaning to the notation

\Lambda\to\infty
in (2.33). We know that
\Lambda
is a finite region
of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and it can tend to infinity (namely its volume
tends to infinity) in various ways. For instance,
\Lambda
could be a
cylinder of fixed base
A
and height
h
and we could let
h\to\infty
. I.e. like a cigar with increasing length. It is obvious
that such a system (particularly if
A
is very small) is not
expected to have a thermodynamic behavior even if
h\to\infty
.

Thus
\Lambda\to\infty
cannot simply be
|\Lambda|\to\infty
, since we want to exclude
cases like “the cigar”. We need thus that
\Lambda\to\infty

roughly in such way that
\Lambda
is big in every direction, e.g. a
sphere of increasing radius, a cube of increasing size etc. We
will review the following definitions

Definition 2.5

\Lambda
is said to go to infinity in the sense of Van Hove
if the following occurs.

Cover
\Lambda
with small cubes of size
a
and let
N_{+}(\Lambda,a)
the number of cubes with non void
intersection with
\Lambda
and
N_{-}(\Lambda,a)
the number of cubes
strictly included in
\Lambda
. Then
\Lambda\to\infty
in the sense of Van
Hove if

N_{-}(\Lambda,a)\to\infty,~~~~~{\rm and}~~~~{N_{-}(\Lambda,a)\over N_{+}(\Lambda,a)}\to 1~~~~~~~~\forall a

See figure 7.

As an example, it is easy to show that if
\Lambda\to\infty
as in the case of the “cigar” seen before, then

\Lambda
is not tending to infinity in the sense of Van Hove. Consider
thus, for sake of simplicity in the plane, a rectangle
R
with
sizes
l
fixed and
t
variable and going to infinity. Let
consider squares of size
a
to cover the rectangle. Then

N_{-}(R,a)~=~{l\over a}{t\over a},~~~~~~~~N_{+}(R,a)~=~{l\over a}{t\over a}+2{t\over a}+2{l\over a}

thus

\lim_{t\to\infty}{N_{-}(R,a)\over N_{+}(R,a)}~=~{l\over l+2a}\neq 1

On the other hand, let us check that a rectangle of sizes
(\sqrt{t},t)
and
tends to infinity, as
t\to\infty
, in the sense of Van Hove. As a
matter of fact

N_{-}(R,a)~=~{\sqrt{t}\over a}{t\over a},~~~~~~~~N_{+}(R,a)~=~{\sqrt{t}\over a}{t\over a}+2{t\over a}+2{\sqrt{t}\over a}

thus

\lim_{t\to\infty}{N_{-}(R,a)\over N_{+}(R,a)}~=~\lim_{t\to\infty}{t\sqrt{t}\over t\sqrt{t}+2at+2a\sqrt{t}}~=~1

We now give a second and more stringent definition of
\Lambda\to\infty
.
For a given
\Lambda
let
\Lambda_{h}
denotes the
set of points at distance less or equal to
h
from the boundary of
\Lambda
and let
|\Lambda_{h}|
denotes its volume. Let finally

d(\Lambda)
denote the diameter of
\Lambda
(i.e.
d(\Lambda)~=~\sup_{x,y\in\Lambda}\{|x-y|\}
) .

Figure 6. A box
\Lambda
going to infinity in a “non-thermodynamic”
way

Definition 2.6

We say that
\Lambda
tends to infinity in the sense of Fischer if

|\Lambda|\to\infty
and it exists a positive function
\pi(\alpha)
such
that
\lim_{\alpha\to 0}\pi(\alpha)~=~0
and for
\alpha
sufficiently small and for
all
\Lambda

{|\Lambda_{\alpha d(\Lambda)}|\over|\Lambda|}\leq\pi(\alpha)

Figure 7. A set
\Lambda
with
N_{+}~=~44
and
N_{-}~=~20

A rectangle
R
of sizes
f(t),t

such that
\lim_{t\to\infty}{f(t)\over t}~=~0
does not go to
infinity in the sense of Fischer. As a matter of fact

d(R)~=~[t^{2}+f^{2}(t)]^{1/2}

|\Lambda_{\alpha d(R)}|~=~2\alpha[t^{2}+f^{2}(t)]^{1/2}[t-2\alpha[t^{2}+f^{2}(t)]^{1/2}+f(t)]

{|\Lambda_{\alpha d(R)}|\over|R|}~=~{2\alpha[t^{2}+f^{2}(t)]^{1/2}[t-2\alpha[t^{2}+f^{2}(t)]^{1/2}+f(t)]\over tf(t)}

~=~{2\alpha t^{2}\sqrt{1+{f^{2}(t)\over t^{2}}}[1-2\alpha\sqrt{1+{f^{2}(t)\over t^{2}}}+{f(t)\over t}]\over tf(t)}

For any fixed
\alpha
the quantity above can be make large at will,
as
t\to\infty
. Thus it is not possible to find any
\pi(\alpha)
such
that
{V_{\alpha d(R)}(R)/V(R)}\leq\pi(\alpha)
for all
R
(i.e. for all

t
).

On the other hand, the square
S
of size
(t,t)
goes to infinity in the sense of Fischer

d(S)~=~\sqrt{2}t,~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~|\Lambda_{\alpha d(S)}|~=~4\alpha\sqrt{2}t[t-\alpha\sqrt{2}t]

{|\Lambda_{\alpha d(S)}|\over|S|}~=~{4\alpha\sqrt{2}\,t[t-\alpha\sqrt{2}t]\over t^{2}}~=~4\alpha\sqrt{2}[1-\alpha\sqrt{2}]\leq 4\alpha\sqrt{2}

Hence one can choose
\pi(\alpha)~=~4\alpha\sqrt{2}
.

2.7
Example: finite range potentials

We will prove in this section the existence of the thermodynamic limit for the function

\beta p(\beta,\lambda)
defined in (2.33), but we will not treat the
general case, namely particles interacting via a pair potential
stable and tempered and
\Lambda
going to infinity as e.g. Van Hove.
We will rather give our proof in a simpler case. Namely, we will put ourselves in
d=3
dimensions and we will
assume that particles interact through a stable and tempered pair
potential
V({x})
with the further property that it exists
{\bar{r}}>0
such that
V({x})\leq 0
if
|{x}|\geq{\bar{r}}
, i.e. the potential
is negative for distances greater than
{\bar{r}}
.

In order to make things even simpler we will also suppose that
\Lambda
is a cube of size

L
and
\Lambda\to\infty~\Leftrightarrow~L\to\infty
. We now choose
two particular sequences
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2},\dots,\Lambda_{n},\dots
and

\tilde{\Lambda}_{1},\tilde{\Lambda}_{2},\dots,\tilde{\Lambda}_{n},\dots
of cubes. Let

\Lambda_{1}
be a cube of size
L_{1}
with volume
V_{1}
while

\tilde{\Lambda}_{1}
is a new cube of size
\tilde{L}_{1}~=~L_{1}+{\bar{r}}
which
consists of
\Lambda_{1}
plus a frame of thickness
{{\bar{r}}\over 2}
. We
denote
\tilde{V}_{1}
it volume (of course
\tilde{V}_{1}>V_{1}
)
\Lambda_{2}

is a cube of size
L_{2}~=~2L_{1}+{\bar{r}}
, thus in
\Lambda_{2}
we can arrange

2^{3}~=~8
cubes
\Lambda_{1}
with frames of thickness
{\bar{r}}/2
in such
way that any point in a given cube
\Lambda_{1}
inside
\Lambda_{2}
is at
distance greater than
{\bar{r}}
from any point in any other cube

\Lambda_{1}
inside
\Lambda_{2}
. Of course
\tilde{\Lambda}_{2}
is a cube of size

2\tilde{L}_{1}
, i.e. is the cube
\Lambda_{2}
plus a frame of thickness

{\bar{r}}/2
. See Figure 8.

In general
\Lambda_{n+1}
is a cube of size
L_{n+1}=2L_{n}+{\bar{r}}

and
\tilde{\Lambda}_{n+1}
is a cube of size
\tilde{L}_{n+1}=2\tilde{L}_{n}~=~2L_{n}+2{\bar{r}}
.
Note that
|\Lambda_{n+1}|>8|\Lambda_{n}|
and
|\tilde{\Lambda}_{n+1}|=8|\tilde{\Lambda}_{n}|
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}{|\tilde{\Lambda}_{n}|/|\Lambda_{n}|}~=~1
.

We will now show that the sequence of functions

P_{n}(\beta,\lambda)~=~{1\over|\Lambda_{n}|}\ln\Xi_{\Lambda_{n}}(\beta,\lambda)

[formula]

tends to a limit.

Figure 8. The cubes
\Lambda_{n+1}
,
\tilde{\Lambda}_{n+1}
and the cubes

\Lambda_{n}
,
\tilde{\Lambda}_{n}

Define the sequence

\tilde{P}_{n}~=~{1\over|\tilde{\Lambda}_{n}|}\ln\Xi_{\Lambda_{n}}(\beta,\lambda)

[formula]

We will first show that this sequence converges to a limit.
Consider the sequence
\Xi_{n}~=~\Xi_{\Lambda_{n}}(\beta,\lambda)
. We have

\Xi_{n+1}~=~\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{N}\over N!}\int_{\Lambda_{n+1}^{N}}d{x}_{1}\dots d{x}_{N}e^{-\beta U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{N})}

We now think
\Lambda_{n+1}
as the union of
8
cubes
\Lambda^{j}_{n}

(
j=1,2,\dots,8
) plus the (internal) frames. Obviously, if we calculate

\Xi_{n+1}
eliminating the configurations in which particles can
stay in the frames we are underestimating
\Xi_{n+1}
. I.e.

\Xi_{n+1}>\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{N}\over N!}\int_{\cup_{j}\Lambda^{j}_{n}}d{x}_{1}\dots d{x}_{N}e^{-\beta U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{N})}

Thus the
N
particles are arranged in such way that
N_{1}
are in
the box
\Lambda_{n}^{1}
,
N_{2}
are in the box
\Lambda_{n}^{2}
…, and
N_{8}

are in the box
\Lambda_{n}^{8}
. Thus we can rename coordinates
{x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{N}
as
{x}_{1}^{1},\dots{x}_{N_{1}}^{1},\dots,{x}_{1}^{8},\dots{x}_{N_{8}}^{8}
. The interaction between particles
in different boxes is surely non positive (since they are at
distances greater or equal to
{\bar{r}}
) then

U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{N})~=~U({x}_{1}^{1},\dots{x}_{N_{1}}^{1},\dots,{x}_{1}^{8},\dots{x}_{N_{8}}^{8})\leq

~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\leq U({x}_{1}^{1},\dots{x}_{N_{1}}^{1})~+~\dots~+~U({x}_{1}^{8}\dots{x}_{N_{8}}^{8})

and hence

e^{-\beta U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{N})}\geq e^{-\beta\left[U({x}_{1}^{1},\dots{x}_{N_{1}}^{1})~+~\dots~+~U({x}_{1}^{8}\dots{x}_{N_{8}}^{8})\right]}

The number of ways in which such arrangement can occur is

{N\choose N_{1}}{N-N_{1}\choose N_{2}}\dots{N-N_{1}-N_{2}-\dots-N_{7}\choose N_{8}}~=~{N!\over N_{1}!\dots N_{8}!}

Hence

\displaystyle\Xi_{n+1}

\displaystyle>\sum_{N_{1},\dots,N_{8}}{\lambda^{N_{1}+\dots+N_{8}}\over N!}{N!\over N_{1}!\dots N_{8}!}\int_{\Lambda^{1}_{n}}d{x}^{1}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda^{1}_{n}}d{x}^{1}_{N_{1}}\cdots

\displaystyle\cdots\int_{\Lambda^{8}_{n}}d{x}^{8}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda^{8}_{n}}d{x}^{8}_{N_{8}}e^{-\beta U({x}^{1}_{1},\dots,{x}^{1}_{N_{1}})}e^{-\beta U({x}^{2}_{1},\dots,{x}^{2}_{N_{2}})}\dots e^{-\beta U({x}^{8}_{1},\dots,{x}^{8}_{N_{8}})}

\displaystyle=(\Xi_{n})^{8}

So we have shown that

\Xi_{n+1}~>~(\Xi_{n})^{8}

Hence, since
f(x)=\ln x
is a monotonic increasing function, we
also get

\ln\Xi_{n+1}>8\ln(\Xi_{n})

and

{1\over\tilde{V}(\Lambda_{n+1})}\ln\Xi_{n+1}>{8\over\tilde{V}(\Lambda_{n+1})}\ln(\Xi_{n})~=~{1\over\tilde{V}(\Lambda_{n})}\ln\Xi_{n}

So the sequence
\tilde{P}_{n}
is monotonic increasing with
n
. On
the other hand by stability we have that.

\Xi_{n}~=~\sum_{N~=~0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{N}\over N!}\int_{\Lambda_{n}^{N}}d^{3}{x}_{1}\dots d^{3}{x}_{N}e^{-\beta U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{N})}~\leq

~~~~~~~~\leq~\sum_{N~=~0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{N}\over N!}\int_{\Lambda_{n}^{N}}d^{3}{x}_{1}\dots d^{3}{x}_{N}e^{+\beta BN}~=~\exp\{\lambda V_{n}e^{\beta B}\}

therefore,

\ln\Xi_{n}~\leq~{\lambda V_{n}e^{\beta B}}

and

\tilde{P}_{n}~=~{1\over\tilde{V}_{n}}\ln\Xi_{n}\leq{1\over\tilde{V}_{n}}{\lambda V_{n}e^{\beta B}}\leq{\lambda e^{\beta B}}

This means that the sequence
\tilde{P}_{n}
is monotonic increasing
and bounded above. Hence
\lim_{n\to\infty}\tilde{P}_{n}~=~P
exists.
But now

P_{n}~=~{1\over V_{n}}\ln\Xi_{n}~=~{\tilde{V}_{n}\over V_{n}}\tilde{P}_{n}

whence

\lim_{n\to\infty}P_{n}~=~\lim_{n\to\infty}{\tilde{V}_{n}\over V_{n}}\tilde{P}_{n}~=~\lim_{n\to\infty}{\tilde{V}_{n}\over V_{n}}\lim_{n\to\infty}\tilde{P}_{n}~=~P

Therefore we show the existence of the thermodynamic limit for a
class of systems interacting via a potential which, beyond being
tempered and stable, has the further property to be non positive
for
|{x}|\geq{\bar{r}}
, when
\Lambda
goes to infinity along the sequence
of cubes
\Lambda_{n}
. It is not difficult from here to show that the
existence of such limit implies also that the limit exists if

\Lambda
is a cube which goes at infinity in sense homothetic (i.e.
the size
\Lambda\to\infty
).

Actually, the existence of limits can be proved for much more general cases, see e.g.
Theorem 3.3.12 in
[
44
]

2.8
Properties the pressure

Let us now show some general properties of the limit for the pressure
\beta p(\beta,\lambda)

in (2.33). The pressure
p(\beta,\lambda)
is a function of two
variables
\lambda
and
\beta
which are the two independent
thermodynamic parameters describing the macroscopic equilibrium
state in the Grand canonical ensemble.

We are interested to study the function
\beta p(\beta,\lambda)
only
for the “physically” admissible values of
\lambda
and
\beta
. These
physical values are:
\lambda
real number in the interval
(0,+\infty)
and
\beta
real number in the interval
(0,+\infty)
(recall definition (1.31)). The Grand canonical
partition function
\Xi(\beta,\Lambda,\lambda)
defined in (2.2) where we
are supposing of course that
U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{N})
is derived
from a stable and tempered pair potential has the following
structure

\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)~=~1+Z_{1}(\Lambda,\beta)\lambda+{Z_{2}(\Lambda,\beta)}\lambda^{2}+{Z_{3}(\Lambda,\beta)}\lambda^{3}+\dots~=

=~\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{Z_{n}(\Lambda,\beta)}\lambda^{n}~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~

I.e.
is a power series in
\lambda
with convergence radius
R=\infty
(this
is true for any
\Lambda
such that
V(\Lambda)<\infty
), i.e.,

\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
is analytic as a function of
\lambda
in the whole
complex plane. Hence
a fortiori

\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
is analytic
for all
\lambda\in(0,+\infty)
. This is true for all
\Lambda
such that

V(\Lambda)<\infty
.

The coefficients
Z_{n}(\Lambda,\beta)
are explicitly given by

Z_{n}(\Lambda,\beta)~=~{1\over n!}\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{n}~e^{-\beta U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})}

[formula]

They are clearly all positive numbers and due to stability (recall
proposition 1) they admit the upper bound
Z_{n}(\Lambda,\beta)\leq[V(\Lambda)]^{n}\,e^{nB\beta}/n!
. Moreover as functions of
\beta
the
coefficients
Z_{n}(\Lambda,\beta)
are analytic in
\beta
in the whole
complex plane and hence
a fortiori
for all
\beta\in(0,\infty)
.

So,
\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
, for all
\Lambda
such that
|\Lambda|<\infty
, and for all

\lambda\in(0,+\infty)
is also analytic as a function of
\beta
in the
whole complex plane. Hence
a fortiori

\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
is
analytic for all
\beta\in(0,+\infty)
.

Now the function
\ln\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
has no reason to continue
analytic in
\lambda
and
\beta
in the whole complex plane, but it is
indeed analytic in
\lambda
for any
\lambda\in(0,+\infty)
and it is
analytic in
\beta
for all
\beta\in(0,+\infty)
. This is due to the
fact that coefficients
Z_{n}(\Lambda,\beta)
are positive numbers. Hence
\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
has no zeroes in the intervals
\lambda\in(0,+\infty)

and
\beta\in(0,+\infty)
. This means that its logarithm is
analytic for such intervals.

In conclusion, we can state that the function

\beta p_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)~=~{1\over|\Lambda|}\ln\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)~\doteq~f_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)

[formula]

is analytic in
\lambda
for all
\lambda\in(0,+\infty)
and it is also
analytic in
\beta
for all
\beta\in(0,+\infty)
for any finite
box
\Lambda
.

This fact of course does not imply that also in the limit
\Lambda\to\infty
the function

\beta p(\beta,\lambda)
will stay analytic in the whole physical intervals

\lambda\in(0,+\infty)
and
\beta\in(0,+\infty)
.

Let us now list some properties of the function
f_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
defined by (2.37).

Property 0a
.
f_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
defined in (2.37) is continuous as a function of
\lambda

and all its derivatives are continuous as functions of
\lambda
in the
whole interval
\lambda\in(0,+\infty)
and for all
\beta\in(0,+\infty)
and
for all
\Lambda
such that
V(\Lambda)<\infty
.

Property 0b
.
f_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
defined in (2.37) is continuous as a function of
\beta

and all its derivatives are continuous as functions of
\beta
in the
whole interval
\beta\in(0,+\infty)
and for all
\lambda\in(0,+\infty)
and
for all
\Lambda
such that
V(\Lambda)<\infty
.

These properties follow trivially from the fact that
f_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
is analytic in
\lambda

and
\beta
when they vary in the interval
(0,+\infty)
.

Property 1
.
f_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
defined in (2.37)
is monotonic increasing as a function of
\lambda
in the interval

\lambda\in(0,+\infty)
, for all
\beta\in(0,+\infty)
and for all
\Lambda
such
that
V(\Lambda)<\infty

In order to show the property 1 it is sufficient
to show that
{\partial f_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)\over\partial\lambda}\geq 0
. But

{\partial f_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)\over\partial\lambda}~=~{1\over|\Lambda|}{{\partial\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)/\partial\lambda}\over\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)}~=~{\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)\over\lambda}

[formula]

where
\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)={\langle N\rangle\over|\Lambda|}
is the density and

\langle N\rangle
is the mean number of particles in the grand
canonical ensemble at fixed values of
\lambda
,
\beta
and
\Lambda
.
Explicitly
\langle N\rangle
is given by

\langle N\rangle~=~{\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{N}}NZ_{N}(\Lambda,\beta)\over\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{N}}Z_{N}(\Lambda,\beta)}

Hence, since
\langle N\rangle
is surely a positive number for
\lambda>0
, we
get

{\partial f_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)\over\partial\lambda}>0

Property 2
.
f_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
defined in (2.37) is convex as a function of

\ln\lambda

2
2

2

Reminding (1.31) observe that
\ln\lambda
is proportional to the chemical potential.

in the interval
\lambda\in(0,+\infty)
, for all
\beta\in(0,+\infty)

and for all
\Lambda
such that
V(\Lambda)<\infty
. Moreover the finite volume
density
\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)={\partial\over\partial(\ln\lambda)}f_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)

is a monotonic increasing function of
\ln\lambda
.

As a matter of fact

{\partial\over\partial(\ln\lambda)}f_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)~=~\lambda{\partial\over\partial\lambda}f_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)~=~\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)

Last line follows by (2.38). Moreover

{\partial^{2}\over\partial(\ln\lambda)^{2}}f_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)~=~\lambda{\partial\over\partial\lambda}\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)~=~{\lambda\over|\Lambda|}{\partial\over\partial\lambda}\langle N\rangle~=

=~{\lambda\over|\Lambda|}{\partial\over\partial\lambda}\left[{\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{N}}NZ_{N}(\Lambda,\beta)\over\sum_{N~=~0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{N}}Z_{N}(\Lambda,\beta)}\right]~=~{1\over|\Lambda|}\left(\langle N^{2}\rangle-\langle N\rangle^{2}\right)~=

=~{\langle(N-\langle N\rangle)^{2}\rangle\over|\Lambda|}~~~~~~~~~~~~~

[formula]

where

\langle N^{2}\rangle~=~{\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{N}}N^{2}Z_{N}(\Lambda,\beta)\over\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{N}}Z_{N}(\Lambda,\beta)}

thus, since the factor
\langle(N-\langle N\rangle)^{2}\rangle
is always positive we get

{\partial^{2}\over\partial(\ln\lambda)^{2}}f_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)~=~{\partial\over\partial(\ln\lambda)}\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)>0

This prove that
f_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
is convex in the variable
\ln\lambda

and that
\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
is monotonic increasing in
\ln\lambda
.

Property 3
.
f_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
defined in (2.37) is convex as a function of

\beta
in the interval
\beta\in(0,+\infty)
, for all
\lambda\in(0,+\infty)
and
for all
\Lambda
such that
V(\Lambda)<\infty
.

As a matter of fact

{\partial\over\partial\beta}f_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)~=~{1\over|\Lambda|}{\partial\over\partial\beta}\ln\Xi(\Lambda,\beta,\lambda)~=~{-\langle U\rangle\over|\Lambda|}

where

\langle U\rangle~=~{1\over\Xi(\Lambda,\beta,\lambda)}\sum_{N~=~0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{N}\over N!}\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{N}U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{N})e^{-\beta U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{N})}

Deriving one more time respect to
\beta

{\partial^{2}\over\partial\beta^{2}}f_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)~=~{\langle U^{2}\rangle-\langle U\rangle^{2}\over|\Lambda|}

where

\langle U^{2}\rangle~=~{1\over\Xi(\Lambda,\beta,\lambda)}\sum_{N~=~0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{N}\over N!}\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{N}[U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{N})]^{2}\,e^{-\beta U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{N})}

and since
\langle U^{2}\rangle-\langle U\rangle^{2}~=~\langle(U-\langle U\rangle)^{2}\rangle\geq 0
we obtain

{\partial^{2}\over\partial\beta^{2}}f_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)\geq 0

and
f_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
is a convex function of
\beta
for all
\Lambda
such
that
V(\Lambda)
is finite.

Property 4
.
\beta p(\beta,\lambda)~=~\lim_{\Lambda\to\infty}\beta p_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
is convex
as a function of
\beta
and
\ln\lambda
in the interval
\beta,\lambda\in(0,+\infty)
.

This very important property of the pressure in the thermodynamic
limit follows trivially from the fact that the limit of a pointwise
converging sequence of convex functions is also a convex function.

Property 5
.
It is possible to express, for any
\Lambda
finite, the pressure

p_{\Lambda}
as a function of
\rho_{\Lambda}
and
\beta
, i.e.

p_{\Lambda}~=~g_{\Lambda}(\rho_{\Lambda},\beta)

moreover the function
g_{\Lambda}(\rho_{\Lambda},\beta)
is monotonic increasing as
a function of
\rho_{\Lambda}
.

The finite volume density is

\rho_{\Lambda}~=~\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)~=~\rho_{\Lambda}(e^{\ln\lambda},\beta)~=~F_{\Lambda}(\ln\lambda,\beta)

Since, by Property 2 the function
F_{\Lambda}(x,\beta)
is strictly
increasing as a function of
x
for any
\Lambda
finite and any
\beta\in(0,+\infty)
, then it admits, as function of
x
, an inverse, say
x~=~G_{\Lambda}(\rho_{\Lambda},\beta)
. Hence

\ln\lambda~=~G_{\Lambda}(\rho_{\Lambda},\beta),~~~~~\lambda~=~e^{G_{\Lambda}(\rho_{\Lambda},\beta)}

Thus the function
g_{\Lambda}(\rho_{\Lambda},\beta)
can be indeed constructed and is
given explicitly by

p_{\Lambda}~=~{1\over\beta}f_{\Lambda}(e^{G_{\Lambda}(\rho_{\Lambda},\beta)},\beta)~=~g_{\Lambda}(\rho_{\Lambda},\beta)

It is now easy to check that this function is monotonic
increasing.
As a matter of fact

\displaystyle{\partial\over\partial\rho_{\Lambda}}p_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle={1\over\beta}{\partial\over\partial\rho_{\Lambda}}(\beta p_{\Lambda})

\displaystyle={1\over\beta}{\partial(\ln\lambda)\over\partial\rho_{\Lambda}}{\partial\over\partial(\ln\lambda)}(\beta p_{\Lambda})

\displaystyle={1\over\beta}\left[{\partial\rho_{\Lambda}\over\partial(\ln\lambda)}\right]^{-1}~\lambda{\partial\over\partial\lambda}f_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)

\displaystyle={1\over\beta}\left[{\partial\rho_{\Lambda}\over\partial(\ln\lambda)}\right]^{-1}{\rho_{\Lambda}}

\displaystyle={1\over\beta}{\rho_{\Lambda}}\left[\lambda{\partial\rho_{\Lambda}\over\partial\lambda}\right]^{-1}

recalling now that
\rho_{\Lambda}~=~\langle N\rangle/V(\Lambda)
and (2.39) we obtain

{\partial\over\partial\rho_{\Lambda}}p_{\Lambda}~=~{1\over\beta}{\rho_{\Lambda}}\left[\lambda{\partial\rho_{\Lambda}\over\partial\lambda}\right]^{-1}~=~{1\over\beta}{\langle N\rangle\over\langle N^{2}\rangle-\langle N\rangle^{2}}

[formula]

Formula (2.40) shows that
{(\partial/\partial\rho_{\Lambda})}p_{\Lambda}
is
always positive. Actually (2.40) tells us also that the value
of
(\partial/\partial\rho_{\Lambda})p_{\Lambda}
is
{\langle N\rangle/\beta(\langle N^{2}\rangle-\langle N\rangle^{2})}
.
Thus if we are able to prove that
{\langle N\rangle(\langle N^{2}\rangle-\langle N\rangle^{2})^{-1}}

stay bounded away from
+\infty
for any
\Lambda
we can conclude that

p(\rho,\beta)=\lim_{\Lambda\to\infty}p_{\Lambda}(\rho_{\Lambda},\beta)
is continuous a function
of the density
\rho~=\lim_{\Lambda\to\infty}\rho_{\Lambda}
.

Property 6
.

p=g(\rho,\beta)
is monotonic increasing as a function of
\rho

(hence monotonic decreasing as a function of
\rho^{-1}
)

The monotonicity follows trivially from the fact that
p_{\Lambda}(\rho_{\Lambda},\beta)
is monotonic increasing
for any
\Lambda
.

Note now that

{\partial\over\partial\rho_{\Lambda}}p_{\Lambda}~=~{1\over\beta}{\langle N\rangle\over\langle N^{2}\rangle-\langle N\rangle^{2}}~=~C_{\Lambda}\geq 0

[formula]

C_{\Lambda}
is a constant in general depending on
\Lambda
.

2.9
Continuity of the pressure

Experimentally the thermodynamic pressure (i.e. the infinite volume limit
of the finite volume pressure
p_{\Lambda}
) is not only increasing as a function
of the density
\rho
but it appears furthermore to have no (jump)
discontinuities. A general proof of this fact is still lacking. It
has been proven that under suitable conditions on the potential
(super-stability) the pressure is indeed continuous as a function
of the density
[
46
]
. We prove here this fact in a much simpler case, namely
we assume that the pair potential is either hard core, or non
negative (i.e. purely repulsive). Our strategy will consist in
proving that the constant
C_{\Lambda}
in equation (2.41) is bounded
uniformly in
\Lambda
. This will allow us to conclude that

p_{\Lambda}(\rho_{\Lambda},\beta)
has a bounded derivative in
\rho_{\Lambda}
uniformly in

\Lambda
, so the limit
p(\rho,\beta)=\lim_{\Lambda\to\infty}p_{\Lambda}(\rho_{\Lambda},\beta)
, cannot
have vertical jumps as a function of
\rho
, i.e.
p(\rho,\beta)
is
continuous as a function of the density.

Let us thus assume that the pair potential between particles is hard core or
purely repulsive. Under these conditions we will then prove the following theorem.

Theorem 2.4

Let
V
be a tempered and stable pair potential.
If
V
is either positive (
V\geq 0
) or has an hard core (i.e.
\exists\;a
such that
V({x})=+\infty
whenever
|{x}|\leq a
), then

{\langle N\rangle\over\langle N^{2}\rangle-\langle N\rangle^{2}}\leq(1+D\lambda)

[formula]

where
D
is uniform in
\Lambda
.

Proof
. We will use the following short notations

X_{N}~=~{x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{N},~~~~~~~~~~~~dX_{N}~=~d{x}_{1}\dots d{x}_{N}~,~

U(X_{N})~=~U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{N}),~~~~~~~~~~~~~~W({x},X_{N})~=~\sum_{j=1}^{N}V({x}_{j}-{x})

Z_{N}~=~\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{N}\,e^{-\beta U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{N})}~=~\int_{\Lambda^{N}}dX_{N}\,e^{-\beta U(X_{N})}

The partition function
\Xi_{\Lambda}(\lambda,\beta)
(denoted shortly by
\Xi
)
can thus be rewritten as

\Xi~=~\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{N}\over N!}Z_{N}

[formula]

With these definitions we have,

Z^{2}_{N+1}~=~\left[\int_{\Lambda^{N}}dX_{N}\,e^{-\beta U(X_{N})}\int_{\Lambda}d{x}e^{-\beta W({x},X_{N})}\right]^{2}~~=

~=~\left[\int_{\Lambda^{N}}dX_{N}\,e^{-{\beta\over 2}U(X_{N})}\int_{\Lambda}d{x}e^{-{\beta\over 2}U(X_{N})}\,e^{-\beta W({x},X_{N})}\right]^{2}~=~

=~\left[\int_{\Lambda^{N}}dX_{N}\,F(X_{N})G(X_{N})\right]^{2}

where

F(X_{N})~=~e^{-{\beta\over 2}U(X_{N})}~~~~~~~~~~G(X_{N})~=~\int_{\Lambda}d{x}e^{-{\beta\over 2}U(X_{N})}\,e^{-\beta W({x},X_{N})}

Using Schwartz inequality we get

\left[\int_{\Lambda^{N}}dX_{N}\,F(X_{N})G(X_{N})\right]^{2}\leq\int_{\Lambda^{N}}dX_{N}\,F^{2}(X_{N})\int_{\Lambda^{N}}dX_{N}\,G^{2}(X_{N})

thus

\displaystyle Z^{2}_{N+1}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Lambda^{N}}\!\!dX_{N}\,e^{-\beta U(X_{N})}\!\!\int_{\Lambda^{N}}\!\!dX_{N}\int_{\Lambda^{2}}d{x}dye^{-{\beta}U(X_{N})}\,e^{-\beta W({x},X_{N})}e^{-\beta W(y,X_{N})}

\displaystyle=Z_{N}\int_{\Lambda^{N}}dX_{N}\,\int_{\Lambda^{2}}d{x}dye^{-{\beta}U(X_{N},{x},y)}\,e^{+\beta V({x}-y)}

\displaystyle=Z_{N}\int_{\Lambda^{N}}dX_{N}\,\int_{\Lambda^{2}}d{x}dye^{-{\beta}U(X_{N},{x},y)}\,\left(e^{+\beta V({x}-y)}-1+1\right)

\displaystyle=Z_{N}Z_{N+2}+Z_{N}\int_{\Lambda^{N}}dX_{N}\,\int_{\Lambda}d{x}\int_{\Lambda}dye^{-{\beta}U(X_{N},{x},y)}\,\left(e^{+\beta V({x}-y)}-1\right)

\displaystyle=Z_{N}Z_{N+2}+Z_{N}\!\!\!\int_{\Lambda^{N}}\!\!dX_{N}\!\!\int_{\Lambda}\!\!d{x}e^{-{\beta}U(X_{N},{x})}\!\!\int_{\Lambda}\!\!dye^{-{\beta}W(X_{N},y)}[1-e^{-\beta V({x}-y)}]

Now, since we are assuming
V({x})
hard core or positive we have

e^{-{\beta}W(X_{N},y)}\leq K(\beta)~=~\begin{cases}1&{\text{i}f}~V\geq 0\\
e^{\beta\mu(a)}&{\text{i}f}~V~{\rm has~an~hard~core}~a\end{cases}

where
\mu(a)
is a constant defined in (2.21).

Thus

Z_{N+1}^{2}\leq Z_{N}Z_{N+2}~+~K(\beta)Z_{N}\!\!\!\int_{\Lambda^{N}}dX_{N}\int_{\Lambda}\!\!d{x}e^{-{\beta}U(X_{N},{x})}\!\!\int_{\Lambda}\!\!dy\,\left(1-e^{-\beta V({x}-y)}\right)\leq

\leq Z_{N}\,Z_{N+2}+K(\beta)Z_{N}Z_{N+1}\int_{\Lambda}dy\,\left|1-e^{-\beta V({x}-y)}\right|\

Note now that in the case in which
V
is positive or have hard
core, and also recalling that
V
must also be tempered, we have

\int_{\Lambda}dy\,\left|1-e^{-\beta V({x}-y)}\right|\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}d{x}|1-e^{-\beta V({x})}|\leq C(\beta)<+\infty

and calling
D(\beta)~=~K(\beta)C(\beta)
we get finally

Z_{N+1}^{2}\leq Z_{N}\,Z_{N+2}+D(\beta)Z_{N}Z_{N+1}~\Longleftrightarrow~{Z^{2}_{N+1}\over Z_{N}}\leq{Z_{N+2}}+Z_{N+1}D(\beta)

[formula]

Now consider

\left(\langle N\rangle[1+\lambda D(\beta)]\right)^{2}~=~\left(\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{N}\over N!}N{Z_{N}\over\Xi}[1+\lambda D(\beta)]\right)^{2}~=~

=~\left(\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{N}\over N!}{Z_{N}\over\Xi}\left[{\lambda^{N+1}Z_{N+1}\over\lambda^{N}Z_{N}}+N\lambda D(\beta)\right]\right)^{2}~=

=~~\left(\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{N}\over N!}{Z_{N}\over\Xi}\left[{\lambda\,Z_{N+1}\over Z_{N}}+N\lambda D(\beta)\right]\right)^{2}

where in the second line we have used

\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{N}\over N!}NZ_{N}~=~\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}{\lambda^{N}\over(N-1)!}Z_{N}~=~\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{N+1}\over N!}Z_{N+1}

and we will use below that, by definition (2.43)

\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{N}\over N!}{Z_{N}\over\Xi}~=~1

Now

\left(\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{N}\over N!}{Z_{N}\over\Xi}\left[{\lambda Z_{N+1}\over Z_{N}}+N\lambda D(\beta)\right]\right)^{2}~=~\left(\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}F_{N}\cdot G_{N}\right)^{2}

where

F_{N}~=~\left({\lambda^{N}\over N!}{Z_{N}\over\Xi}\right)^{1/2}~~~~~~~~~G_{N}~=~\left({\lambda^{N}\over N!}{Z_{N}\over\Xi}\right)^{1/2}\left[{\lambda Z_{N+1}\over Z_{N}}+N\lambda D(\beta)\right]

using thus again Schwartz inequality we get

\left(\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}F_{N}\cdot G_{N}\right)^{2}\leq(\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}F_{N}^{2})(\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}G_{N}^{2})~=~

=~1\times\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}\left({\lambda^{N}\over N!}{Z_{N}\over\Xi}\right)\left[\lambda{Z_{N+1}\over Z_{N}}+N\lambda D(\beta)\right]^{2}~=~

~=~\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}\left({\lambda^{N}\over N!}{1\over\Xi}\right)\left[\lambda^{2}{Z^{2}_{N+1}\over Z_{N}}+2N\lambda^{2}D(\beta){Z_{N+1}}+Z_{N}N^{2}\lambda^{2}D^{2}(\beta)\right]

hence using also (2.44)

\left(\langle N\rangle[1+\lambda D(\beta)]\right)^{2}\leq\sum_{N~=~0}^{\infty}\left({\lambda^{N}\over N!}{1\over\Xi}\right)\lambda^{2}\Bigg[{Z_{N+2}}~+~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~

~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~+~Z_{N+1}D(\beta)+2ND(\beta){Z_{N+1}}+Z_{N}N^{2}D^{2}(\beta)\Bigg]

Now observe that

\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}\left({\lambda^{N}\over N!}{1\over\Xi}\right)\lambda^{2}{Z_{N+2}}~=~\sum_{N=2}^{\infty}\left({\lambda^{N}{Z_{N}}\over(N-2)!}{1\over\Xi}\right)~=~\langle N(N-1)\rangle

\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}\left({\lambda^{N}\over N!}{1\over\Xi}\right)\lambda^{2}Z_{N+1}D(\beta)~=~\lambda D(\beta)\langle N\rangle

\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}\left({\lambda^{N}\over N!}{1\over\Xi}\right)2N\lambda^{2}D(\beta){Z_{N+1}}~=~2D(\beta)\lambda\langle N(N-1)\rangle

\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}\left({\lambda^{N}\over N!}{1\over\Xi}\right)Z_{N}N^{2}\lambda^{2}D^{2}(\beta)~=~D^{2}(\beta)\lambda^{2}\langle N^{2}\rangle

thus

\left(\langle N\rangle[1+\lambda D(\beta)]\right)^{2}\leq

\leq~\langle N(N-1)\rangle+\lambda D(\beta)\langle N\rangle~+~2D(\beta)\lambda\langle N(N-1)\rangle~+~D^{2}(\beta)\lambda^{2}\langle N^{2}\rangle~=~

~=~\langle N^{2}\rangle-\langle N\rangle+\lambda D(\beta)\langle N\rangle~+~2D(\beta)\lambda(\langle N^{2}\rangle-\langle N\rangle)~+~D^{2}(\beta)\lambda^{2}\langle N^{2}\rangle~=~

~=~\langle N^{2}\rangle(1+D(\beta)\lambda)^{2}-\langle N\rangle(1+\lambda D(\beta))

Thus we are arrived at the inequality

\langle N\rangle^{2}[1+\lambda D(\beta)]^{2}\leq~\langle N^{2}\rangle(1+D(\beta)\lambda)^{2}-\langle N\rangle(1+\lambda D(\beta))

i.e.

\langle N\rangle~~\leq~(\langle N^{2}\rangle-\langle N\rangle^{2})(1+\lambda D(\beta))

which is as to say

{\langle N\rangle\over\langle N^{2}\rangle-\langle N\rangle^{2}}\leq 1+\lambda D(\beta)

and the proof is completed.
\Box

2.10
Analiticity of the pressure

We have seen by property 0a that the pressure at finite volume
p_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
is analytic as a
function of its parameters
\beta
and
\lambda
in the whole physical
domain
\lambda>0
,
\beta>0
. We can now ask if the infinite volume
pressure
p(\beta,\lambda)
is also analytic in its parameters. If this
were the case then we would be really in trouble and we should
conclude that statistical mechanics is not sufficient to describe
the macroscopic behaviour of a system with a large number of
particles.

As a matter of fact experiments tells us that the physical
pressure can indeed be non analytic. For example the graphic of
the pressure versus the density at constant temperature (if the
temperature is not too high, i.e. not above the critical point)
for a real gas is drawn below.

When
\rho
reaches the value
\rho_{0}
the gas starts to condensate to its liquid phase
and during the whole interval
[\rho_{0},\rho_{1}]
the gas performs a
phase transitions. i.e. from its gas phase to its liquid phase, at
the same pressure
p_{0}
. Above
\rho_{1}
the system is totally in its
liquid phase. The change occurs abruptly and is usually
characterized by singular behaviour in thermodynamic functions.

Hence, in spite of the fact that pressure is continuous even in
the limit, its derivatives may be not continuous in the infinite
volume limit. Note that this fact substantially justifies the
necessity to take the thermodynamic limit. Until we stay at finite
volume, all thermodynamic functions are analytic, hence in order
to describe a phenomenon like phase transition we are forced to
consider the infinite volume limit. We can thus give the following mathematical definition for phase transition

Definition 2.7

Any non analytic point of the grand
canonical pressure defined in (2.33) occurring for real
positive
\beta
or
\lambda
is called a phase transition point.

People believe in general that the pressure
p(\beta,\lambda)
is a piecewise analytic function of
its parameters in the physical intervals
\lambda>0

\beta>0
.

Hence it is very important to see which are the values of parameter
\lambda
and
\beta
for which
the pressure is analytic. Guided by the physical intuition, for low
values of
\lambda
and
\beta
one expects that the
pressure is indeed analytic. In fact
\lambda
low means that the
system is at low density, while
\beta
low means that the system is
at high temperature (e.g. above the critical point thus so high
that the system is always a gas and never condensates). For such
values the system is indeed a gas and in general very near to a
perfect gas. I.e. for temperature sufficiently high and/or density
sufficiently low the system should be in the gas phase and no phase
transition occurs.

Hence it should exist a theorem stating that the pressure
p(\beta,\lambda)
is analytic for

\beta
and/or
\lambda
sufficiently small. We would see later the such a
theorem can effectively be proved.

Figure 9. Pressure versus density for a physical gas. The
gas-liquid phase transition

We may ask the following question. We know that
p(\beta,\rho)
is continuous while e.g.

{\partial p\over\partial\rho}
may be not. But if
{\partial p\over\partial\rho}

is not continuous in some point it means that at that point
\rho_{0}

the function can take two values. So what is the thermodynamic
limit when
\rho~=~\rho_{0}
? Or, in other words which of the possible
values of
{\partial p\over\partial\rho}
the system chooses at the
thermodynamic limit?

The answer to this question resides in the concept of boundary conditions.
Up to now boundary conditions were “open”, i.e. we were studying
a system of particles enclosed in a box
\Lambda
supposing that
outside
\Lambda
there was nothing, just empty space.

However, we could also
have done things in a different way, or in more proper words we
could have put a different boundary condition. For instance, we
can put
n
particles outside the box
\Lambda
at fixed point
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
.

In this case the grand canonical partition function looks as

\Xi^{y}_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)~=~1~+\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}{\lambda^{N}\over N!}\int_{\Lambda}\!\!d{x}_{1}\dots\!\!\int_{\Lambda}\!\!d{x}_{N}~e^{-\beta U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{N})}e^{-\beta W({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{N},y_{1},\dots,y_{n})}

[formula]

where

W({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{N},y_{1},\dots,y_{n})~=~\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{n}V({x}_{i}-y_{j})

Hence the function
\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
may depend also on boundary
conditions. This means that also
p_{\Lambda}
the pressure at finite
volume depends on boundary conditions.

Does the infinite volume pressure depend on boundary conditions?

The answer to this question must be no (always guided by physical intuition), at least
for not too strange systems and/or not too strange boundary
conditions.

It is possible to show this quite easily for a finite range (with
range
\bar{r}
) hard core (with hard core
a
) potential. I.e.,
particles must stay at distances
a
or higher and they do not
interact if the distance is greater than
\bar{r}
.

In this case, the particles outside
\Lambda
that give contribution to the
partition function (2.45) are those which are contained in a layer of thickness

\bar{r}
outside
\Lambda
. Supposing that
\Lambda
is a cube in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
of size
L
, the
volume of this layer is of the order
L^{2}\bar{r}
.

Figure 10.

Any particle inside
\Lambda
can interact just with particles inside a
sphere of radius
\bar{r}
centered at the particles position, and
due to the hard core condition in this sphere one can arrange at
most of the order of
(\bar{r}/a)^{3}
. This means that a particle
inside can interact at most with
(\bar{r}/a)^{3}
particles
outside
\Lambda
.

On the other hand particles inside
\Lambda
that can interact with particles
outside
\Lambda
are also contained in a (internal) layer of size

\bar{r}
, and the maximum number of particles inside
\Lambda
(hence
contributing to
W
in (2.45)) is of the order

L^{2}\bar{r}\over a^{3}

With these observations it is not difficult to see that

|W({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{N},y_{1},\dots,y_{n})|\leq{\rm Const.}~{L^{2}\bar{r}\over a^{3}}\left({\bar{r}\over a}\right)^{3}

and therefore

e^{-{\rm Const.}~{L^{2}\bar{r}\over a^{3}}\left({\bar{r}\over a}\right)^{3}}\Xi^{\rm open}_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)~\leq~\Xi^{y}_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)~\leq~e^{{\rm Const.}~{L^{2}\bar{r}\over a^{3}}\left({\bar{r}\over a}\right)^{3}}\Xi^{\rm open}_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)

Thus

{1\over L^{3}}\ln\Xi^{y}_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)~=~{1\over L^{3}}\ln\Xi^{\rm open}_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)\pm{1\over L^{3}}{\rm Const.}~{L^{2}\bar{r}\over a^{3}}\left({\bar{r}\over a}\right)^{3}

and the factor

{1\over L^{3}}{\rm Const.}~{L^{2}\bar{r}\over a^{3}}\left({\bar{r}\over a}\right)^{3}

goes to zero as
L\to\infty
. I.e. the pressure does not depend on boundary conditions.

But now in general the derivative of the pressure may depend on boundary condition.
This happens precisely when the derivative are not continuous. In
such points different boundary condition may force different
values of derivatives. Changing boundary condition one can thus
change the value on a derivative in a discontinuity point. This
can be interpreted as an alternative definition of phase
transition.

Definition 2.8

A phase transition point is a point in which the value of
some derivative of the infinite volume pressure depends on
boundary conditions even at the thermodynamic limit.

Thus when the system is sensible to change of boundary conditions we say that there is a phase transition.

By this rough discussion, we see that lack of analyticity of
the pressure or sensitivity of the system to change in boundary
conditions are two ways to characterize a phase transition.

Chapter 3
High temperature low density expansion

3.1
The Mayer series

3.1.1
Some Notations about graphs

We first give some definitions about abstract graphs which will be useful below.

Let
A
be any finite set, we denote by
|A|
the number of
elements of
A
. We denote by
P(A)
the power set of
A
(i.e.
the set of all subsets of
A
). We denote by
P^{k}(A)~=~\{U\subset A:|U|~=~k\}
(i.e. the set of all subsets of
A
with cardinality
k
). If

A~=~\{1,2,\dots,n\}
, we shortly put
P(\{1,2,\dots,n\})\equiv P_{n}
and
P^{k}(\{1,2,\dots,n\})\equiv P^{k}_{n}
.

Definition 3.1

A graph
g
is a pair
g~=~(V_{g},E_{g})
where

V_{g}
is a countable set and
E_{g}\subset P^{2}(V_{g})
. The set
V_{g}

is called the vertex set of
g
and the elements of
V_{g}

are called vertices of the graph
g
, while
E_{g}
is called
the edge set of
g
and the elements of
E_{g}
are
called edges of the graph
g
.

Given two graphs
g~=~(V_{g},E_{g})
and
f~=~(V_{f},E_{f})
we say that

f\subset g
if
V_{f}\subset V_{g}
and
E_{f}\subset E_{g}
.

Definition 3.2

A graph
g~=~(V_{g},E_{g})

is said to be connected if for any pair
B,C
of subsets
of
V_{g}
such that
B\cup C~=~V_{g}
and
B\cap C~=~\emptyset
, there is
a
e\in E_{g}
such that
e\cap B\neq\emptyset
and
e\cap C\neq\emptyset
.

We denote by
{\cal G}_{V}
the set of all graphs with vertex set
V
and
by
G_{V}
the set of all connected graphs with vertex set
V
. We
will shortly denote
{\cal G}_{n}
the set
{\cal G}_{\{1,2,\dots,n\}}
of
all graphs with vertex set
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
and by
G_{n}
the set

G_{\{1,2,\dots,n\}}
of all connected graphs with vertex set

\{1,2,\dots,n\}
. We will also denote shortly

[n]=\{1,2,\dots,n\}

[formula]

{\rm E}_{n}=\{\{i,j\}\subset[n]\}

[formula]

In particular
{\rm E}_{n}
is the set of all unordered pairs
\{i,j\}
of the set
[n]
.

Definition 3.3

A graph
\tau\in G_{V}
such
that
|E_{\tau}|~=~|V|-1
is called a tree graph.

We denote
T_{V}
the set of all tree graphs with vertex set
V
. Note that

T_{V}\subset G_{V}
. The set of all the tree graph over
[n]

will be denoted by
T_{n}
.

Definition 3.4

Let
g~=~(V_{g},E_{g})
be a graph and let
x\in V_{g}
. Then
the degree (or number of incidence)
d_{x}
of the vertex
x\in V_{g}

in
g
is the number of edges
e\in E_{g}
such that
x\in e
.

Observe that in a tree
\tau\in T_{n}
the numbers of incidence

d_{1},\dots,d_{n}
at vertices
1,\dots,n
satisfies the identity

d_{1}+d_{2}+\dots+d_{n}~=~2n-2

[formula]

This identity holds because any tree
\tau
has
n-1
edges and each
edge has two vertices and so each edge is counted twice in the sum of the l.h.s. of (3.3).
Moreover, we have clearly the following
bound for the number of incidence
d_{i}
in a vertex of tree
\tau
.

1\leq d_{i}\leq n-1

[formula]

This is again because any tree
\tau\in T_{n}
is connected (hence
d_{i}\geq 1
for all
i\in[n]
) and has
n-1
edges (hence
d_{i}\leq n-1
for all
i\in[n]
).

The number of trees in
T_{n}
is explicitly computable using the
Cayley formulas. These formulas can be presented by the
following lemma.

Lemma 3.1

The number of trees in
T_{n}
with
numbers of incidence in vertices
1,2,\dots,n
fixed at the values

d_{1},d_{2},\dots,d_{n}
is given by

\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}\atop d_{1},d_{2},\dots,d_{n}~{\rm fixed}}1~=~{(n-2)!\over\prod_{i~=~1}^{n}(d_{i}-1)!}

[formula]

Moreover

|T_{n}|~=~\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}1~=~n^{n-2}

[formula]

Proof.

We first show (3.5) by induction. Formula (3.5) is
trivially true if
n=2
. We suppose now that (3.5) is true
for all trees with
n
vertices and with any incidence numbers

d_{1},\dots,d_{n}
and we will prove that (3.5) holds also for
all trees with
n+1
vertices and with any incidence numbers

d_{1},\dots,d_{n},d_{n+1}
.

Take a tree
\tau
with
n+1
vertices and numbers of incidence in vertices fixed at
the values
d_{1},\dots,d_{n+1}
. Such tree has at least two
vertices with number of incidence equal to 1. Without lost in
generality let us suppose that
d_{n+1}=1
(otherwise we can
always rename the vertices of
\tau
). Now if
d_{n+1}=1
, then
there is an edge in
\tau
which links the vertex
n+1
with some
vertex
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. This vertex
j
has surely
d_{j}\geq 2
, i.e. there is at least another edge starting from
j
which
ends in some other vertex
k\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, since
\tau
is
connected. So we can count trees with
n+1
vertices and fixed
number of incidence proceeding as follows. For each time we fix
the edge joining
n+1
to some vertex
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, we
count all trees in
\{1,\dots,n\}
with number of incidence
fixed at the values
d_{1},\dots,d_{j-1},d_{j}-1,d_{j+1},\dots d_{n}
, by using the induction hypothesis. Namely,

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\in T_{n+1}\atop d_{1},\dots,d_{n+1}~{\rm fixed}}1

\displaystyle=\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{j\in\{1,2,\dots,n\}:\atop d_{j}\geq 2}\!\!\!\!{(n-2)!\over(d_{1}-1)!\cdots(d_{j-1}-1)!(d_{j}-2)!(d_{j+1}-1)!\cdots(d_{n}-1)!}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\{1,2,\dots,n\}:\atop d_{j}\geq 2}{(n-2)!(d_{j}-1)\over(d_{1}-1)!\cdots(d_{j}-1)!\cdots(d_{n}-1)!(d_{n+1}-1)!}

\displaystyle={(n-2)!\over\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}(d_{i}-1)!}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(d_{j}-1)

where in the second line we just rewrite

{1\over(d_{j}-2)!}~=~{(d_{j}-1)\over(d_{j}-1)!(d_{n+1}-1)!}

using the assumption that
d_{n+1}=1
and hence
(d_{n+1}-1)!=0!=1
. Now using again that
d_{n+1}-1=0
and by (3.3) with
n+1
in place of
n
, we get

\sum_{j=1}^{n}(d_{j}-1)=\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}(d_{j}-1)~=~\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}d_{j}-(n+1)~=~2(n+1)-2-(n+1)~=~n-1

hence

\sum_{\tau\in T_{n+1}\atop d_{1},d_{2},\dots,d_{n+1}~{\rm fixed}}1~=~{(n-2)!\over\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}(d_{i}-1)!}(n-1)~=~{(n-1)!\over\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}(d_{i}-1)!}
