and (3.5) is proved. Now (3.6) is an easy consequence of (3.5). As a matter of fact, just observe, using
(3.3), (3.4), and (3.5), that

\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}1~=~\sum_{d_{1},\dots,d_{n}:~1\leq d_{i}\leq n-1\atop d_{1}+\dots+d_{n}=2n-2}{(n-2)!\over\prod_{i=1}^{n}(d_{i}-1)!}~=~\sum_{s_{1},\dots,s_{n}:~0\leq s_{i}\leq n-2\atop s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}~=~n-2}{(n-2)!\over\prod_{i=1}^{n}s_{i}!}~=~n^{n-2}

\Box

3.1.2
Mayer Series: definition

We now come back to the claim that a system of particles,
interacting via a reasonable potential energy (e.g. defined via a
stable and tempered pair potential) should be in the a gas phase at
sufficiently low density and/or sufficiently high temperature,
hence the pressure of such system should be analytic in the
thermodynamic parameters in this region. Since we are considering
just the Grand Canonical ensemble, the region of high temperature
and low density will be in the case the region of
\lambda
small and

\beta
small.

Let us thus consider again the grand Canonical partition function

\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)~=~1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\lambda^{n}\over n!}\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{n}~e^{-\beta U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})}

[formula]

with

U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})~=~\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})

[formula]

where
V({x})
is a stable and tempered pair potential. We denote shortly
{x}_{[n]}
the set of coordinates

{x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n}
and if
I\subset[n]
then
{x}_{I}
will denote the set of coordinates
{x}_{i}
with
i\in I
. Hence
for
I\subset[n]

U({x}_{I})=\sum_{\{i,j\}\subset I}V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})

We now look for an expansion of the log of such function valid for
\lambda
small.
This is clearly possible, due to the structure of
\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
. In fact, as a power series of
\lambda
the partition
function has the form

\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)~=~1+c_{1}\lambda+c_{2}\lambda^{2}+c_{3}\lambda^{3}+\dots~=~1+O(\lambda)

and
\ln(1+O(\lambda))
can indeed be expanded also in power series
of
\lambda
. Let us show that there exists a formal expansion of
\ln\Xi
in powers of
\lambda
called the Mayer series of the
pressure. We thus prove now the following theorem.

Theorem 3.1

Let
\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
be defined as in (3.7) and (3.8).
Then

{1\over|\Lambda|}\ln\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)~=~\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)\lambda^{n}

[formula]

where

C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)~=~{1\over n!}{1\over|\Lambda|}\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{n}\Phi^{T}({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})

[formula]

with
\Phi^{T}({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})
given by the following expressions:

A)

\Phi^{T}({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})~=~\begin{cases}\sum\limits_{g\in G_{n}}~\prod\limits_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}\left[e^{-\beta V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})}-1\right]&{\rm if}~n\geq 2\\
1&{\rm if}~n=1\end{cases}

[formula]

where recall that
G_{n}
is the set of all connected graphs in

\{1,2,\dots,n\}
and if
g\in G_{n}
then its edge set is denoted by
E_{g}
.

B)

Given
[n]
and
1\leq k\leq n
, let
\Pi^{k}_{n}
is the set of all partitions of
[n]
in
k
blocks. Then

\Phi^{T}({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})~=~\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k-1}(k-1)!\sum_{\{I_{1},I_{2},\dots,I_{k}\}\in\Pi^{k}_{n}}e^{-\beta\sum_{\alpha=1}^{k}U({x}_{I_{\alpha}})}

[formula]

where
I=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{|I|}\}\subset[n]
and
x_{I}=(x_{i_{1}},\dots,x_{i_{|I|}})
with
U({x}_{I})=U(x_{i_{1}},\dots,x_{i_{|I|}})
.

The series in the r.h.s. of (3.9) is called the Mayer series and the coefficient

C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)
(
n\geq 1
) is called Mayer (or Ursell) coeffcient of order
n
. For the moment this series is merely
a formal series, since Theorem
3.1
does not say if the series
converges or not.

Proof of A
.

\displaystyle\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)

\displaystyle=~1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\lambda^{n}\over n!}\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{n}~e^{-\beta\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})}

\displaystyle=~1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\lambda^{n}\over n!}\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{n}~\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}e^{-\beta V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})}

\displaystyle=~1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\lambda^{n}\over n!}\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{n}~\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\left[\left(e^{-\beta V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})}-1\right)+1\right]

Develop now the product in the factor

\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\left[\left(e^{-\beta V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})}-1\right)+1\right]

Then it is not difficult to see that it
can be rewritten as

\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\left[\left(e^{-\beta V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})}-1\right)+1\right]~=~\sum_{g\in{\cal G}_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}\left[e^{-\beta V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})}-1\right]

where recall that
{\cal G}_{n}
is the set of all graphs (connected or
not connected) in the set
\{1,2\dots,n\}
. In
{\cal G}_{n}
we also
include the empty graph, i.e the graph
g
such that

E_{g}~=~\emptyset
, and its contribution is the factor
1
in the
development of the product.

We now reorganize the sum over graphs in
{\cal G}_{n}
.

For
1\leq k\leq n
,
let
\{I_{1},I_{2},\dots,I_{k}\}
denote a partition of the set

[n]
. Namely, for any
i,j~=~1,2\dots,k
we have that

I_{i}\neq\emptyset
,
I_{i}\cap I_{j}~=~\emptyset
and

\cup_{j~=~1}^{k}I_{j}~=~[n]
. We denote by
{x}_{I_{j}}
the
set of coordinates
{x}_{i}
with
i\in I_{j}
. We also denote with

\Pi_{n}
the set of all partitions of the set
[n]
.

Then it is not difficult to see that

\sum_{g\in{\cal G}_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}\left[e^{-\beta V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})}-1\right]~=~\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{\{I_{1},I_{2},\dots,I_{k}\}\in\Pi^{k}_{n}}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\Phi^{T}({x}_{I_{j}})

[formula]

where

\Phi^{T}({x}_{I_{j}})~=~\begin{cases}\sum\limits_{g\in G_{I_{j}}}\prod\limits_{\{l,s\}\in E_{g}}\left[e^{-\beta V({x}_{l}-{x}_{s})}-1\right]&{\rm if}~|I_{j}|\geq 2\\
1&{\rm if}~|I_{j}|~=~1\end{cases}

Note now that
\sum_{g\in G_{I_{j}}}
runs over all
connected

graphs in the set
I_{j}
.

Hence

\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)~=~1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\lambda^{n}\over n!}\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}~\sum_{\{I_{1},\dots,I_{k}\}\in\Pi^{k}_{n}}~\prod_{j=1}^{k}\Phi^{T}({x}_{I_{j}})

Now observe that each
\Phi^{T}({x}_{I_{j}})
depends only on the
set of coordinates
{x}_{i}
with
i\in I_{j}
and since
I_{1},\dots I_{k}
is a partition of
\{1,\dots,n\}
we can write

\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{n}~=~\int_{\Lambda^{|I_{1}|}}d{x}_{I_{1}}\dots\int_{\Lambda^{|I_{k}|}}d{x}_{I_{k}}

where of course
d{x}_{I_{i}}~=~\prod_{i\in I_{j}}d{x}_{i}
. Hence

\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)~=~1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\lambda^{n}\over n!}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{\{I_{1},\dots,I_{k}\}\in\Pi_{n}}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\int_{\Lambda^{|I_{j}|}}d{x}_{I_{j}}\Phi^{T}({x}_{I_{j}})

Observe now that the factor

\int_{\Lambda^{|I_{j}|}}d{x}_{I_{j}}\Phi^{T}({x}_{I_{j}})

depends only on
|I_{j}|
and not anymore from
{x}_{i}
(since space
coordinates are integrated) and not even on
I_{j}
since
i\in I_{j}

is just an index attached to a mute variable. I.e.

\int_{\Lambda^{|I_{j}|}}d{x}_{I_{j}}\Phi^{T}({x}_{I_{j}})~=~\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{|I_{j}|}\Phi^{T}({x}_{1},\dots{x}_{|I_{j}|})~=~\phi(|I_{j}|)

Note that the numbers
|I_{j}|
are
positive integers subjected to the condition
\sum_{j=1}^{k}|I_{j}|=n
. Hence

\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)~=~1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\lambda^{n}\over n!}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{\{I_{1},\dots,I_{k}\}\in\Pi^{k}_{n}}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\phi(|I_{j}|)

Now observe that

\displaystyle\sum_{\{I_{1},I_{2},\dots,I_{k}\}\in\Pi^{k}_{n}}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\phi(|I_{j}|)

\displaystyle=~{1\over k!}\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{k}:~\,m_{i}\geq 1\atop m_{i}+\dots+m_{k}=n}\sum_{I_{1},\dots,I_{k}:\atop|I_{1}|=m_{1},\dots,|I_{k}|=m_{k}}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\phi(m_{j})

\displaystyle=\,\,{1\over k!}\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{k}:~\,m_{i}\geq 1\atop m_{1}+\dots+m_{k}=n}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\phi(m_{j})\sum_{I_{1},\dots,I_{k}:\atop|I_{1}|=m_{1},\dots,|I_{k}|=m_{k}}\!\!\!\!1

here above we have also to divide by
k!

because the same partition
I_{1},\dots I_{k}
of
[n]
appears
exactly
k!
times in the sum

\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{k}:\,m_{i}\geq 1\atop m_{1}+\dots m_{k}=n}\sum_{I_{1},\dots,I_{k}:\atop|I_{1}|=m_{1},\dots,|I_{k}|=m_{k}}

Therefore we get

\sum_{\{I_{1},I_{2},\dots,I_{k}\}\in\Pi^{k}_{n}}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\phi(|I_{j}|)={1\over k!}\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{k}:~\,m_{i}\geq 1\atop m_{1}+\dots m_{k}=n}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\phi(m_{j}){n!\over m_{1}!\dots m_{k}!}

[formula]

Indeed,
{n!/(m_{1}!\dots m_{k}!)}
is the number
of partitions of
[n]
in
k
subsets
I_{1},...,I_{k}

such that the numbers
|I_{1}|,...,|I_{k}|
are fixed at the values

m_{1},\dots,m_{k}
respectively.
Hence we can write

\displaystyle\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)

\displaystyle=~1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\lambda^{n}\over n!}\sum_{k=1}^{n}{1\over k!}\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{k}:\,m_{i}\geq 1\atop m_{1}+\dots+m_{k}=n}{n!\over m_{1}!\dots m_{k}!}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\phi(m_{j})

\displaystyle=~1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{n}{1\over k!}\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{k}:\,m_{i}\geq 1\atop m_{1}+\dots+m_{k}=n}{\lambda^{m_{1}}\cdots\lambda^{m_{k}}\over m_{1}!\dots m_{k}!}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\phi(m_{j})

\displaystyle=~1+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{1\over k!}\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{k}\atop\,m_{i}\geq 1}{\lambda^{m_{1}}\cdots\lambda^{m_{k}}\over m_{1}!\dots m_{k}!}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\phi(m_{j})

\displaystyle=~1+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{1\over k!}\left[\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}{\lambda^{m}\over m!}\phi(m)\right]^{k}

i.e we have found

\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)~=~1+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{1\over k!}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\lambda^{n}\over n!}\phi(n)\right]^{k}~=~\exp\left\{\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\lambda^{n}\over n!}\phi(n)\right]\right\}

Hence

\displaystyle\ln\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)

\displaystyle=~\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\lambda^{n}\over n!}\phi(n)

\displaystyle=~\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\lambda^{n}\over n!}\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{n}\Phi^{T}({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})

\displaystyle=~\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\lambda^{n}\over n!}\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{n}\sum_{g\in G_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}\left[e^{-\beta V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})}-1\right]

which concludes the proof of part A) of the theorem.
\Box

Proof of B)
. We start by writing

Z_{n}=\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{n}~e^{-\beta U({x}_{[n]})}

and in general, if
I\subset[n]
,

Z_{I}=\int_{\Lambda^{|I|}}d{x}_{I}~e^{-\beta U({x}_{I})}=Z_{|I|}

so that

\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)~=~1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\lambda^{n}\over n!}Z_{n}

Hence, formally

\displaystyle\ln\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)

\displaystyle=~\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{(-1)^{k-1}\over k}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\lambda^{n}\over n!}Z_{n}\right]^{k}

\displaystyle=~\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{(-1)^{k-1}\over k}\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{k}\atop m_{i}\geq 1}\prod_{j=1}^{k}{\lambda^{m_{j}}Z_{m_{j}}\over m_{j}!}

\displaystyle=~\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{(-1)^{k-1}\over k}\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}{\lambda^{n}\over n!}\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{k}:\;m_{i}\geq 1\atop m_{1}+\dots+m_{k}=n}(\prod_{j=1}^{k}{Z_{m_{j}}}){n!\over m_{1}!\cdots m_{k}!}

Now, by equation (3.14) and denoting shortly

\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{n}=\int_{\Lambda^{n}}d{x}_{[n]}

we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{k}:\;m_{i}\geq 1\atop m_{1}+\dots+\,m_{k}=n}(\prod_{j=1}^{k}{Z_{m_{j}}}){n!\over m_{1}!\cdots m_{k}!}

\displaystyle=k!\sum_{\{I_{1},I_{2},\dots,I_{k}\}\in\Pi^{k}_{n}}\prod_{j=1}^{k}Z_{(|I_{j}|)}

\displaystyle=~k!\int_{\Lambda^{n}}d{x}_{[n]}\sum_{\{I_{1},\dots,I_{k}\}\in\Pi^{k}_{n}}e^{-\sum_{\alpha=1}^{k}\beta U({x}_{I_{\alpha}})}

Thus we get

\ln\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{(-1)^{k-1}(k-1)!}\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}{\lambda^{n}\over n!}\int_{\Lambda^{n}}d{x}_{[n]}\sum_{\{I_{1},\dots,I_{k}\}\in\Pi^{k}_{n}}e^{-\beta\sum_{\alpha=1}^{k}U({x}_{I_{\alpha}})}

Finally, exchanging the sum over
k
with the sum over
n
we get

\ln\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\lambda^{n}\over n!}\int_{\Lambda^{n}}d{x}_{[n]}\sum_{k=1}^{n}{(-1)^{k-1}(k-1)!}\sum_{\{I_{1},\dots,I_{k}\}\in\Pi_{n}}e^{-\beta\sum_{\alpha=1}^{k}U({x}_{I_{\alpha}})}

whence (3.12) follows.

\Box

3.1.3
The combinatorial problem

The very structure of the Mayer series for
\ln\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
hides a very
hard combinatorial problem. To understand this problem let us try to find
a bound uniform in
\Lambda
for the coefficient of order
n
of
the Mayer series, as given by formula (3.10), i.e.

C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)~=~{1\over n!}{1\over|\Lambda|}\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{n}\Phi^{T}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})~=~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~

=~{1\over n!}{1\over|\Lambda|}\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{n}\sum_{g\in G_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}\left[e^{-\beta V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})}-1\right]

[formula]

Note that
C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)
is a function of
\beta
and
\Lambda
.
The simplest way to bound
|C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)|
is as follows

\displaystyle|C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)|

\displaystyle\leq{1\over n!}{1\over|\Lambda|}\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{n}\Big|\sum_{g\in G_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}\left[e^{-\beta V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})}-1\right]\Big|

\displaystyle\leq{1\over n!}{1\over|\Lambda|}\sum_{g\in G_{n}}\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{n}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}\left|e^{-\beta V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})}-1\right|

Now observe that, if
V({x})
is stable then necessarily it is
bounded below by
2B
, i.e.

V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})\geq-2B

which is simply the stability condition
U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})\geq-Bn
for the case in which
n~=~2
. Thus factor

|e^{-\beta V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})}-1|\leq\max\{1,e^{2\beta B}-1\}\leq 1

[formula]

for
\beta
sufficiently small.

Now observe that any connected graph
g\in G_{n}
contains at least
one tree
\tau\in T_{n}
. Let us thus choose for each
g
a tree
\tau
such
that
\tau\subset g
. It is allowed to choose the same tree for
different
g
’s.

So, by (3.16) we have

\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}\left|e^{-\beta V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})}-1\right|\leq\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}\left|e^{-\beta V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})}-1\right|

We now prove the following

Proposition 3.1

For any
\tau\in T_{n}
it holds the following inequality

\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{n}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}\left|e^{-\beta V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})}-1\right|~\leq~|\Lambda|\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|e^{-\beta V({x})}-1|d{x}\right]^{n-1}

[formula]

Proof
.
Let us denote shortly

C_{\tau}(\beta)=\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{n}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}\left|e^{-\beta V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})}-1\right|

and
to fix ideas let
E_{\tau}~=~\{i_{1},j_{1}\},\dots\{i_{n-1},j_{n-1}\}
, so
that

C_{\tau}(\beta)=\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{n}\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}\left|e^{-\beta V({x}_{i_{k}}-{x}_{j_{k}})}-1\right|

Define a change of variables in the integral as follows

y_{k}~=~x_{i_{k}}-x_{j_{k}},~~~~~~\forall k~=~2\dots,n

y_{1}~=~x_{1}

The Jacobian of this transformation is clearly 1, then

\displaystyle C_{\tau}(\beta)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}dy_{2}\dots\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\!\!dy_{n}\prod_{j~=~2}^{n}\left|e^{-\beta V(y_{j})}-1\right|

\displaystyle=~|\Lambda|\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|e^{-\beta V({x})}-1|d{x}\right]^{n-1}

\Box

It is now a simple exercise to see that stability and temperness
imply that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|e^{-\beta V({x})}-1|d{x}~\doteq~C(\beta)<\infty

Actually, it is not even necessary for the potential to be
stable. A sufficient condition for the finiteness of
C(\beta)
is,
if
S(a)
is a sphere of radius
a
and center in
{x}~=~0
,

V({x})
integrable in
\mathbb{R}^{3}\backslash S(a)
and non
negative for
|{x}|\leq a
.

So we get

|C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)|\leq{[C(\beta)]^{n-1}\over n!}\sum_{g\in G_{n}}1~=~{[C(\beta)]^{n-1}\over n!}B_{n}

where
B_{n}
is the number of connected graphs in the set

\{1,2,\dots,n\}
. Hence
|C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)|
has as upper bound a quantity uniform in
\Lambda
, which is good,
hence the
coefficents for the Mayer series of the pressure indeed admit a
bound uniform in
\Lambda
!

Thus looking for the absolute convergence of the Mayer series for the
finite volume pressure we get

|\beta p_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)|\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}|C_{n}((\beta,\Lambda))\lambda^{n}|\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{[C(\beta)]^{n-1}\over n!}B_{n}\lambda^{n}

If we could now get a good bound for
B_{n}
, the number of
connected graphs in a finite set of
n
elements, e.g. a bound at
worst such as
n!B^{n}
, we would have the proof that the infinite
volume pressure is analytic in the region

|\lambda|C(\beta)B<1

with the further restriction (3.16) on
\beta
. But it is not the
case since it is easy to show that
for all
n\geq 2

B_{n}\geq 2^{{(n-1)(n-2)\over 2}}

[formula]

Indeed, first observe that the number of graphs, denoted with
{\cal B}_{n}
, (connected or not connected) in the
set
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
is

{\cal B}_{n}~=~\sum_{g\in{\cal G}_{n}}1~=~2^{{1\over 2}n(n-1)}

[formula]

As a matter of fact, we can construct a one-to-one correspondence
between the set of graphs in
{\cal G}_{n}
and the set
\Omega_{n}
of
sequences
\{\sigma_{\{i,j\}}\}_{\{i,j\}\subset\{1,\dots,n\}}

with
\sigma_{\{i,j\}}~=~0,1
, with the rule that,
\sigma_{\{i,j\}}=1
if

\{i,j\}\in E_{g}
and
\sigma_{\{i,j\}}=0
if
\{i,j\}\notin E_{g}
. Hence a
graph
g\in{\cal G}_{n}
can be viewed as an ordered sequence of

n(n-1)/2
numbers which can be either 0 or 1. Thus the total
number of graphs equals the total number of such sequences which
is clearly
2^{n(n-1)/2}
.

It is now simple to get a bound for
B_{n}=|G_{n}|
. Consider the subset of

\tilde{\Omega}_{n}\subset\Omega_{n}
formed by sequences

\{\sigma_{\{i,j\}}\}_{\{i,j\}\subset\{1,\dots,n\}}

such that

\sigma_{\{1,2\}}=1,\sigma_{\{2,3\}}=1,\dots,\sigma_{\{n-1,n\}}=1
, hence

n-1
links are fixed while
n(n-1)/2-(n-1)=(n-1)(n-2)/2
are
arbitrary. Clearly, any graphs corresponding to a sequence in

\tilde{\Omega}_{n}
is connected by construction, since it contains
the tree
\tau=\left\{\{{1,2\}},\{2,3\}...,\{n-1,n\}\right\}
and

|\tilde{\Omega}_{n}|=2^{(n-1)(n-2)/2}
. Thus

B_{n}\geq 2^{(n-1)(n-2)/2}

Such a bound shows that we have no hope to control the (absolute) convergence of
the Mayer series if we don’t exploit cancellations hidden in the
factor

\left|\sum_{g\in G_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}\left[e^{-\beta V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})}-1\right]\right|

One may think to use the alternative expression (3.12) for the Ursell coefficients
\Phi^{T}({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})
which looks more well behaved as a function of
n
and also looks more suitable to
take advantage of stability. The problem is that
the expression (3.12), if bounded naively, behaves badly in the volume
|\Lambda|
. Using naively (3.12) we get the following bound for the absolute value of

C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)
.

\displaystyle|C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)|

\displaystyle\leq{1\over n!}{1\over|\Lambda|}\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{n}|\Phi^{T}({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})|

\displaystyle\leq{1\over|\Lambda|}\sum_{k=1}^{n}(k-1)!\sum_{\{I_{1},\dots,I_{k}\}\in\Pi_{n}}{1\over n!}\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{n}e^{-\beta\sum_{\alpha=1}^{k}U({x}_{I_{\alpha}})}

and using then stability we get

\displaystyle|C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)|

\displaystyle\leq{1\over|\Lambda|}\sum_{k=1}^{n}(k-1)!\sum_{\{I_{1},\dots,I_{k}\}\in\Pi_{n}}{1\over n!}\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{n}e^{+\beta\sum_{\alpha=1}^{k}B|I_{\alpha}|}

\displaystyle\leq|\Lambda|^{n-1}e^{\beta Bn}\sum_{k=1}^{n}{k!\over n!}\sum_{\{I_{1},I_{2},\dots,I_{k}\}\in\Pi_{n}}1

\displaystyle=e^{+\beta B}(|\Lambda|e^{\beta B})^{n-1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}{k!\over n!}S(n,k)

where the terms
S(n,k)
are the Stirling numbers of the second kind
1
1

1

S(n,k)
is the number of ways to partition a set of
n
objects into
k
non-empty subsets.
.
It holds

\displaystyle S(n,k)

\displaystyle=~\sum_{\{I_{1},I_{2},\dots,I_{k}\}\in\Pi_{n}}1

\displaystyle=~{1\over k!}\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{k}:~\,m_{i}\geq 1\atop m_{1}+\dots m_{k}=n}{n!\over m_{1}!\dots m_{k}!}

\displaystyle=~{n!\over k!(n-k)!}\sum_{s_{1},\dots,s_{k}:~\,s_{i}\geq 0\atop s_{1}+\dots s_{k}=n-k}{(n-k)!\over(s_{1}+1)!\dots(s_{k}+1)!}

\displaystyle\leq~{n\choose k}\sum_{s_{1},\dots,s_{k}:~\,s_{i}\geq 0\atop s_{1}+\dots s_{k}=n-k}{(n-k)!\over s_{1}!\dots s_{k}!}

\displaystyle=~{n\choose k}k^{n-k}

Therefore we obtain

\displaystyle|C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)|

\displaystyle\leq{e^{\beta B}}{(|\Lambda|e^{\beta B})^{n-1}}\sum_{k=1}^{n}{k!\over n!}{n\choose k}k^{n-k}

\displaystyle=~{e^{\beta B}}{(|\Lambda|e^{\beta B})^{n-1}}\sum_{k=1}^{n}{k^{n-k}\over(n-k)!}

\displaystyle=~{e^{\beta B}}{(|\Lambda|e^{\beta B})^{n-1}}\sum_{s=0}^{n-1}{(n-s)^{s}\over s!}

\displaystyle\leq~{e^{\beta B}}{(|\Lambda|e^{\beta B})^{n-1}}\sum_{s=0}^{n-1}{{n}^{s}\over s!}

\displaystyle\leq~{e^{\beta B}}{(|\Lambda|e^{\beta B})^{n-1}}\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}{{n}^{s}\over s!}

\displaystyle=~|\Lambda|^{n-1}{(e^{+\beta B+1})^{n}}

This bound for
|C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)|
has a well combinational behavior in
n
, but it grows as
|\Lambda|^{n}
in the volume
\Lambda
and hence the (lower) bound for the convergence radius of the Mayer series obtained
from this bound shrinks to zero as
\Lambda\to\infty
.

3.2
Convergence of the Mayer series

The best rigorous upper bound on
|C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)|
until recently (and hence the best lower bound on the convergence radius
of the Mayer series) for
stable and tempered pair potentials was that
obtained by Penrose and Ruelle in 1963
[
34
,
45
]
.

Theorem 3.2
(Penrose-Ruelle)

Let
V
be a stable and tempered pair potential with stability constant
B
. Then
the
n
-order Mayer coefficient
C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)
defined in (3.15)
is bounded by

|C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)|\leq e^{2\beta B(n-2)}n^{n-2}{[C(\beta)]^{n-1}\over n!}

[formula]

where

C(\beta)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}dx~|e^{-\beta V(x)}-1|

[formula]

Therefore the Mayer series (3.9) converges absolutely, uniformly in
\Lambda
,
for any complex
\lambda
inside the disk

|\lambda|<{1\over e^{2\beta B+1}C(\beta)}

[formula]

I.e. the convergence radius of the Mayer series (3.9) admits the following lower bound

R_{V}\geq{1\over e^{2\beta B+1}C(\beta)}

[formula]

The estimate (3.20) leading to the lower bound (3.23) was obtained by attacking the problem with a rather
indirect approach. Namely Penrose and Ruelle looked at an infinite class of functions of the systems,
the so called correlation functions, and showed that they can be expressed via an absolute convergent
expansion. The next section is entirely devoted to the proof of Theorem
3.2
.

3.2.1
Kirkwood-Salsburg equations: the original proof of Theorem
3.2
(according to Penrose)

We are considering a system of particles enclosed in a box
\Lambda
and interacting via a pair potential

V(x-y)
stable and tempered.
We thus define the correlation functions of the system as follows. For any
n\geq 1

\rho_{n}({x}_{1},\dots{x}_{n};\lambda)={1\over\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{n+m}\over m!}\int_{\Lambda}dy_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}dy_{m}\,e^{-\beta U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{m})}

[formula]

with the convention that

U({x}_{1})=0

[formula]

and for
n=0

\rho_{0}(\emptyset;\lambda)=1

[formula]

The numbers
\rho_{n}({x}_{1},\dots{x}_{n};\lambda)
represent the probability density of finding
in the system
n
particles at positions
{x}_{1},\dots{x}_{n}
irrespective of where the other particle are.
It is important to observe that the functions
\rho_{n}({x}_{1},\dots{x}_{n};\lambda)
are by construction symmetric
under permutation of positions
{x}_{1},\dots{x}_{n}
. I.e. if
\sigma:\{1,2,\dots,n\}\to\{1,2,\dots,n\}:i\mapsto\sigma(i)

is one-to-one (i.e. a permutation) then

\rho_{n}({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n};\lambda)=\rho_{n}({x}_{\sigma(1)},\dots,{x}_{\sigma(n)};\lambda)

[formula]

Observe also that, due to stability, the numerator and the denominator of (3.24) are holomorphic functions
of
\lambda
(see proposition
2.1
), hence
\rho_{n}({x}_{1},\dots{x}_{n};\lambda)
is a meromorphic function of
\lambda
.
The series expansion
\rho({x}_{1},\dots{x}_{n};\lambda)
in powers of
\lambda
around
\lambda=0
has convergence radius at least
equal to the convergence radius of
\log\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
(i.e. where in the region where the denominator

\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
in l.h.s of (3.24)
is free of zeros). Let us write this series expansion as

\rho_{n}({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n};\lambda)=\sum_{\ell=0}\rho_{n,\ell\,}({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})\lambda^{n+\ell}

[formula]

Observe that if we consider the one-point correlation function
\rho_{1}({x}_{1})
, it is easy to see that
the coefficients
\rho_{1,\ell}(x_{1})
of its series expansion in power of
\lambda
, integrated over the volume
\Lambda
, are related with
the Ursell coefficient of the Mayer expansion for
\log\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
. As a matter of fact,
since

\rho_{1}({x}_{1};\lambda)={1\over\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{1+m}\over m!}\int_{\Lambda}dy_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}dy_{m}\,e^{-\beta U({x}_{1},y_{1},\dots,y_{m})}

[formula]

it is easy to check that

\int_{\Lambda}\rho_{1}({x}_{1};\lambda)d{x}_{1}=\lambda\,\>{d(\log\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda))\over d\lambda}

Thus

\displaystyle{1\over|\Lambda|}\int_{\Lambda}\rho_{1,\ell-1\,}({x}_{1})d{x}_{1}

\displaystyle={1\over(\ell-1)!}{1\over|\Lambda|}\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{\ell}~\Phi^{T}({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{\ell})

[formula]

\displaystyle=\ell C_{l}(\beta,\Lambda)

One can now calculate easily the first term in the expansion (3.28) by division
in (3.24). As a matter of fact, since

\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)=1+O(\lambda)
, then also
\Xi^{-1}_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)=1+O(\lambda)
and the first term
of (3.24) is thus the first term of the numerator. Therefore

\rho_{n,0\,}({x}_{1},\dots{x}_{n})=e^{-\beta U({x}_{1}.\dots,{x}_{n})}

[formula]

Moreover observe that, by the convention (3.26), we also have

\rho_{0,\ell\,}(\emptyset)=\delta_{0,\ell}

[formula]

where
\delta_{ij}
is the Kronecker symbol, i.e
\delta_{ij}=1
if
i=j
and
\delta_{ij}=0
if
i\neq j
. Let us now find recurrence relations for the higher coefficients

\rho_{n,\ell\,}({x}_{1},\dots{x}_{n})
for
n\geq 1
. It is easy to see that the functions
\rho_{n}({x}_{1},\dots{x}_{n};\lambda)

satisfies a set of integral functions named the Kirkwood-Salsburg equations. These equations
are obtained first by decomposing the energy
U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{m})
appearing in l.h.s. of
(3.24) as

U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{m})=W({x}_{1};{x}_{2},\dots,{x}_{n})+~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~

~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~+\sum_{j=1}^{m}V({x}_{1}-y_{j})+U({x}_{2},\dots,{x}_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{m})

[formula]

where

W({x}_{1};{x}_{2},\dots,{x}_{n})=\sum_{i=2}^{n}V({x}_{1}-{x}_{i})

Putting (3.33) into (3.24) one gets

\displaystyle\rho_{n}({x}_{1},\dots{x}_{n};\lambda)

\displaystyle={\lambda e^{-\beta W({x}_{1};{x}_{2},\dots,{x}_{n})}\over\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{n-1+m}\over m!}\int_{\Lambda}dy_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}dy_{m}

\displaystyle\times e^{-\beta(\sum_{j=1}^{m}V({x}_{1}-y_{j})+U({x}_{2},\dots,{x}_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{m}))}

\displaystyle=~{\lambda e^{-\beta W({x}_{1};{x}_{2},\dots,{x}_{n})}\over\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{n-1+m}\over m!}\int_{\Lambda}dy_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}dy_{m}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{j=1}^{m}\Big[\Big(e^{-\beta V({x}_{1}-y_{j})}-1\Big)+1\Big]~e^{-\beta U({x}_{2},\dots,{x}_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{m})}

\displaystyle=~{\lambda e^{-\beta W({x}_{1};{x}_{2},\dots,{x}_{n})}\over\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{n-1+m}\over m!}\int_{\Lambda}dy_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}dy_{m}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{s=0}^{m}\sum_{j_{1},\dots j_{s}\atop 1\leq j_{1}<\dots<j_{s}\leq m}\prod_{k=1}^{s}(e^{-\beta V({x}_{1}-y_{j_{k}})}-1)e^{-\beta U({x}_{2},\dots,{x}_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{m})}

Here above the term with
s=0
correspond to 1.
Since the variables
y
are dummy indices, for any term of the sum over indices

j_{1},\dots j_{s}
we can relabel the variables
y_{1},\dots y_{m}
in such way that

y_{j_{1}}=y_{1},\dots,y_{j_{s}}=y_{s}
and keeping in account that

\sum_{j_{1},\dots j_{s}\atop 1\leq j_{1}<j_{2}<\dots<j_{s}\leq m}1={m\choose s}={m!\over s!(m-s)!}

and the fact that
U(z_{1},\dots z_{p})
is symmetric under permutations of its variables, we obtain

\rho_{n}({x}_{1},\dots{x}_{n};\lambda)={\lambda e^{-\beta W({x}_{1};{x}_{2},\dots,{x}_{n})}\over\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{n-1+m}}\int_{\Lambda}dy_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}dy_{m}\,

~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\sum_{s=0}^{m}\,{1\over s!(m-s)!}\prod_{k=1}^{s}(e^{-\beta V({x}_{1}-y_{k})}-1)e^{-\beta U({x}_{2},\dots,{x}_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{m})}

We can interchange the sum over
m
and
s
by observing that

\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{s=0}^{m}\dots=\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=s}^{\infty}\dots

and calling
t=m-s
. Hence we get, after a suitable renomination of the dummy indices
y

\rho_{n}({x}_{1},\dots{x}_{n};\lambda)={\lambda e^{-\beta W({x}_{1};{x}_{2},\dots,{x}_{n})}\over\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)}\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}{1\over s!}\int_{\Lambda}dy_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}dy_{s}\,\prod_{k=1}^{s}(e^{-\beta V({x}_{1}-y_{k})}-1)

~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\,{{\lambda^{n-1+s+t}}\over t!}\int_{\Lambda}dy^{\prime}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}dy^{\prime}_{t}e^{-\beta U({x}_{2},\dots,{x}_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{s},y^{\prime}_{1},\dots,y^{\prime}_{t})}

i.e.

\rho_{n}({x}_{1},\dots{x}_{n};\lambda)={\lambda e^{-\beta W({x}_{1};{x}_{2},\dots,{x}_{n})}}\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}{1\over s!}\int_{\Lambda}dy_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}dy_{s}\,\prod_{k=1}^{s}(e^{-\beta V({x}_{1}-y_{k})}-1)

\rho_{n-1+s}({x}_{2},\dots{x}_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{s};\lambda)

[formula]

The latter are the Kirkwood-Salsburg equations.
Substituting now (3.28) into (3.34) we get

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\rho_{n,k\,}\!({x}_{1},\dots{x}_{n})\lambda^{k}={e^{-\beta W({x}_{1};{x}_{2},\dots,{x}_{n})}}\!\!\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}{1\over s!}\!\!\int_{\Lambda}\!\!dy_{1}\dots\!\!\!\int_{\Lambda}\!\!dy_{s}\!\prod_{k=1}^{s}(e^{-\beta V({x}_{1}-y_{k})}-1)

\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}\rho_{n-1+s,r\,}({x}_{2},\dots{x}_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{s})\lambda^{s+r}

and equating coefficients with the same power, say
k
, of
\lambda

\rho_{n,k\,}({x}_{1},\dots{x}_{n})={e^{-\beta W({x}_{1};{x}_{2},\dots,{x}_{n})}}\sum_{s=0}^{k}{1\over s!}\int_{\Lambda}dy_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}dy_{s}\,\prod_{k=1}^{s}(e^{-\beta V({x}_{1}-y_{k})}-1)

\rho_{n-1+s,k-s\,}({x}_{2},\dots{x}_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{s})

[formula]

Recalling (3.32), this equation holds for all integers
n\geq 1
and
k\geq 0
. Note that for
n=1
the formula (3.35) degenerates into

\rho_{1,k\,}({x}_{1})=\sum_{s=0}^{k}{1\over s!}\int_{\Lambda}dy_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}dy_{s}\,\prod_{k=1}^{s}(e^{-\beta V({x}_{1}-y_{k})}-1)\rho_{s,k-s\,}(y_{1},\dots,y_{s})

[formula]

To estimate the convergence radius of the correlation functions

\rho_{n}({x}_{1},\dots{x}_{n};\lambda)

and hence of the
\log\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
, we have to calculate efficient upper bounds
on the coefficients
\rho_{n,k\,}({x}_{1},\dots{x}_{n})
of the expansion of the correlations.

Looking to the structure of the equation (3.35) one can observe the following.
Given a pair of indices
(n,k)
, let
M=n+k
, then (3.35) says that

\rho_{n,k}
is a function of the
\rho_{i,j}
such that
i+j=M-1
.
Therefore it is absolutely natural
to look for a bound on
\rho_{n,k}
by induction on
n+k
. Namely, we look for

|\rho_{n,M-n\,}({x}_{1},\dots{x}_{n})|\leq K_{n,M-n}~~~~~~(n=1,2,\dots,M)

[formula]

and make the induction on the integer
M
.
By (3.31) and (3.25)

K_{1,0}=1

[formula]

Therefore inequality (3.37) is satisfied for
M=1
with
|\rho_{1,0}({x}_{1})|\leq K_{1,0}=1
.
Now, for
M>1
, we proceed by induction on
M
. Assume that (3.37) is true
M-1
and for all
n=1,\dots,M-1
, then, by (3.35)

|\rho_{n,M-n\,}({x}_{1},\dots{x}_{n})|\leq e^{-\beta W({x}_{1};{x}_{2},\dots,{x}_{n})}\sum_{s=0}^{M-n}{1\over s!}[C(\beta)]^{s}\,K_{n-1+s,M-n-s\,}

[formula]

where

C(\beta)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Big|e^{-\beta V({x})}-1\Big|\,d{x}

[formula]

In order to bound the factor
\exp\{-\beta W({x}_{1};{x}_{2},\dots,{x}_{n})\}
in (3.39),
we explicitly make use of the symmetry of the function
\rho_{n,M-n\,}({x}_{1},\dots{x}_{n})

under permutation of
{x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n}
.

Let
i\in\{1,2,\dots,n\}
and let
\sigma_{1\leftrightarrows i}
be
a permutation in

\{1,2,\dots,n\}
such that

\sigma_{1\leftrightarrows i}(1)=i
,
\sigma_{1\leftrightarrows i}(i)=1
and
\sigma_{1\leftrightarrows i}(k)=k

for all
k\neq 1,i
(i.e.
\sigma_{1\leftrightarrows i}
exchange only
i
with 1). Then, by stability
for at least one
j\in\{1,2,\dots,n\}
, it holds

W({x}_{\sigma_{1\leftrightarrows j}(1)};{x}_{\sigma_{1\leftrightarrows j}(2)},\dots,{x}_{\sigma_{1\leftrightarrows j}(n)})\geq-2B

[formula]

Indeed, by stability we have that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}W({x}_{\sigma_{1\leftrightarrows i}(1)};{x}_{\sigma_{1\leftrightarrows i}(2)},\dots,{x}_{\sigma_{1\leftrightarrows i}(n)})=2U({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})\geq-2nB

and this immediately implies (3.41) for at least one
j
. Thus, choosing
j\in[n]
such that the permutation

\sigma_{1\leftrightarrows j}
satisfies (3.41), we have

|\rho_{n,M-n\,}({x}_{1},\dots{x}_{n})|=|\rho_{n,M-n\,}({x}_{\sigma_{1\leftrightarrows j}(1)},\dots{x}_{\sigma_{1\leftrightarrows j}(n)})|\leq

\leq~e^{2\beta B}\sum_{s=0}^{M-n}{1\over s!}[C(\beta)]^{s}\,K_{n-1+s,M-n-s\,}

[formula]

Hence (3.37) holds also for
M
provided that, for
n=1,\dots,M

K_{n,M-n}\geq\,e^{2\beta B}\sum_{s=0}^{M-n}{1\over s!}[C(\beta)]^{s}\,K_{n-1+s,M-n-s\,}

[formula]

In conclusion we have proved by induction that (3.37) holds
for all
M\geq 1
if (3.38) holds and (3.43) for
M>1
.
To find the best value for
K_{n,M-n}

let us solve the set of equations

K_{n,M-n}=\,e^{2\beta B}\sum_{s=0}^{M-n}{1\over s!}[C(\beta)]^{s}\,K_{n-1+s,M-n-s\,}

[formula]

These equations are recursive. I.e., for a fixed integer
M
all coefficients
K_{n,M-n}
with
n=1,\dots,M
are functions of
coefficients
K_{n,M-1-n}
with
n=1,\dots M-1
. So the initial condition (3.38) plus the equations (3.44)
determines uniquely all coefficients
K_{n,M-n}
.
It is worth to check that its solution is

K_{n,\ell}=e^{2\beta B(n+\ell-1)}n(n+\ell)^{\ell-1}{[C(\beta)]^{\ell}\over\ell!}

[formula]

In conclusion we have that the coefficients of the power series in
\lambda
given by (3.28) can be bounded as

|\rho_{n,\ell\,}({x}_{1},\dots{x}_{n})|\leq e^{2\beta B(n+\ell-1)}n(n+\ell)^{\ell-1}{[C(\beta)]^{\ell}\over\ell!}

[formula]

This implies that (3.28) has convergence radius at least

R\geq{1\over e^{2\beta B+1}C(\beta)}

As a matter of fact, the n-point correalation
|\rho_{n}({x}_{1},\dots{x}_{n})|
, see (3.28) and
(3.46) is less or equal to

\displaystyle|\rho_{n}({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n};\lambda)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}e^{2\beta B(n+\ell-1)}n(n+\ell)^{\ell-1}{[C(\beta)]^{\ell}\over\ell!}\lambda^{n+\ell}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}e^{2\beta B(n+\ell-1)}(n+\ell)^{\ell}~{[C(\beta)]^{\ell}\over\ell!}\lambda^{n+\ell}

\displaystyle=e^{2\beta B(n-1)}\lambda^{n}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\Big(1+{n\over\ell}\Big)^{\ell}~\,{\ell^{\ell}\over\ell!}~\Big[e^{2\beta B}C(\beta)\lambda\Big]^{\ell}

\displaystyle\leq e^{2\beta B(n-1)}\lambda^{n}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}e^{n}\,e^{\ell}\Big[e^{2\beta B}C(\beta)\lambda\Big]^{\ell}

\displaystyle\leq e^{(2\beta B+1)n}\lambda^{n}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\Big[e^{2\beta B+1}C(\beta)\lambda\Big]^{\ell}

It is also interesting to calculate the bound we obtain for the Ursell coefficients
of the Mayer series. Observe first that in place of (3.39) we have, recalling the special case

n=1
(3.36),

|\rho_{1,M-1}({x}_{1})|\leq\sum_{s=0}^{M-1}{1\over s!}[C(\beta)]^{s}\,K_{s,M-1-s\,}

[formula]

(i.e.the factor
e^{-\beta W({x}_{1};{x}_{2},\dots,{x}_{n})}
is not present in the
n=1
case).
So, using (3.44) and (3.45) we get

|\rho_{1,\ell}({x}_{1})|\leq e^{-2\beta B}K_{1,\ell}=e^{2\beta B(\ell-1)}(1+\ell)^{\ell-1}{[C(\beta)]^{\ell}\over\ell!}

Hence, recalling the definitions (3.9) and (3.10) and using (3.30), we get

\displaystyle n|C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)|

\displaystyle\leq~\left|n{\lambda^{n}\over n!}{1\over|\Lambda|}\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}d{x}_{n}~\Phi^{T}({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})\right|

\displaystyle=~\Big|{1\over|\Lambda|}\int_{\Lambda}\rho_{1,n-1\,}({x}_{1})d{x}_{1}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq~e^{2\beta B(n-2)}(n)^{n-2}{[C(\beta)]^{n-1}\over(n-1)!}

I.e. we obtain the bound (3.20)

|C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)|\leq e^{2\beta B(n-2)}n^{n-2}{[C(\beta)]^{n-1}\over n!}

[formula]

which completes the proof of Theorem
3.2
.

We stress once again that this bound is valid for any stable pair potential
V({x})
such that

C(\beta)
defined in (3.40) is finite. The bound is very efficient, but
it is indeed obtained in a rather involved and indirect way. We will now show
an alternative way to get the same bound (3.48) in a much more direct way,
i.e. obtaining directly a bound for the absolute values of the Ursell coefficients
|C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)|

starting from their explicit expression (3.10).

3.3
The Penrose Tree graph Identity

The
tree-graph identity that we present was proposed by Penrose
[
35
]
in 1967 and
it was based on the existence of a map from the set
T_{n}
of the trees with vertex set
[n]
to the set
G_{n}
of the connected graphs with vertex set
[n]
inducing a so-called
partition scheme
in
G_{n}
.
This tree graph identity allows us to rewrite the Ursell coefficient defined in (3.11), whose expression we recall

\Phi^{T}({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})~=~\sum\limits_{g\in G_{n}}~\prod\limits_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}\left[e^{-\beta V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})}-1\right]

[formula]

in terms of a sum over trees rather than over connected graphs.

Definition 3.5

A map
\mathfrak{M}:T_{n}\to G_{n}
is called a partition scheme in the set of the connected graphs
G_{n}
if, for all
\tau\in T_{n}
,

\tau\subset\mathfrak{M}(\tau)

and
G_{n}=\biguplus_{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}}[\tau,\mathfrak{M}(\tau)]

where
\biguplus
means disjoint union and
[\tau,\mathfrak{M}(\tau)]=\{g\in G_{n}:\tau\subset g\subset\mathfrak{M}(\tau)\}

is a boolean interval (with respect to the set-inclusion).

Once a partition scheme in
G_{n}
has been given, we have the following identity

Theorem 3.3
(General Penrose identity)

Let
n\geq 2
and let
\{V_{ij}\}_{\{i,j\}\in E_{n}}
be
n(n-1)/2
real numbers
(one for each unordered pair
\{i,j\}\subset[n]
) taking values in
\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
and let

\Phi^{T}(n)=\sum\limits_{g\in G_{n}}~\prod\limits_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}\left[e^{-V_{ij}}-1\right]

Let
\mathfrak{M}:T_{n}\to G_{n}
be a partition scheme in
G_{n}
.
Then the following identity holds

\Phi^{T}(n)~=~\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}e^{-\sum\limits_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\mathfrak{M}(\tau)}\backslash E_{\tau}}V_{ij}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}\left(e^{-V_{ij}}-1\right)

[formula]

Proof
. Since

G_{n}
is the disjoint union
G_{n}=\biguplus_{\tau\in{T}_{n}}[\tau,\mathfrak{M}(\tau)]
we can write

\displaystyle\Phi^{T}(n)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}\sum_{g\in[\tau,\mathfrak{M}(\tau)]}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}\left(e^{-V_{ij}}-1\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}\left(e^{-V_{ij}}-1\right)\sum_{g\in[\tau,\mathfrak{M}(\tau)]}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}\setminus E_{\tau}}\left(e^{-V_{ij}}-1\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}\left(e^{-V_{ij}}-1\right)\sum_{g\in G_{n}\atop E_{\tau}\subset E_{g}\subset E_{\mathfrak{M}(\tau)}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}\setminus E_{\tau}}\left(e^{-V_{ij}}-1\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}\left(e^{-V_{ij}}-1\right)\sum_{E\subset E_{\mathfrak{M}(\tau)}\setminus E_{\tau}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E}\left(e^{-V_{ij}}-1\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}\left(e^{-V_{ij}}-1\right)\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\mathfrak{M}(\tau)}\setminus E_{\tau}}\left[\left(e^{-V_{ij}}-1\right)+1\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}\left(e^{-V_{ij}}-1\right)\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\mathfrak{M}(\tau)}\setminus E_{\tau}}e^{-V_{ij}}

which concludes the proof.

\Box

In general it is not so simple to check whether a given map
\mathfrak{M}:T_{n}\to G_{n}
is a partition scheme. The proposition below
can be useful.

Proposition 3.2

The following statements are equivalent.

1.

There are two maps

\textstyle{{\mathcal{G}}_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{T}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{T}}_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{M}}

such that
\mathfrak{T}^{-1}(\tau)=\{g\in{\mathcal{G}}_{n}:\,\tau\subseteq g\subseteq\mathfrak{M}(\tau)\}
for every
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}_{n}
.

2.

\mathfrak{M}
is a partition scheme in
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
.

Proof
.
1\Rightarrow 2
.
Since
g\in\mathfrak{T}^{-1}(\mathfrak{T}(g))
, we have
\mathfrak{T}(g)\subset g
for all
g\in{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
. In particular,
for every tree
\tau
we have
\mathfrak{T}(\tau)\subset\tau
which implies

\mathfrak{T}(\tau)=\tau
because both are trees. I.e.,
\mathfrak{T}
is surjective and thus the intervals
\mathfrak{T}^{-1}(\tau)
are nonempty. This implies that

{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
is the disjoint union of the intervals

{\mathcal{G}}_{n}=\bigcup_{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}_{n}}\mathfrak{T}^{-1}(\tau)=\bigcup_{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}_{n}}[\tau,\mathfrak{M}(\tau)]
.
Hence in view
of Definition
3.5
we conclude that
\mathfrak{M}
is a partition scheme in
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
.

2\Rightarrow 1
. If

\mathfrak{M}
is a partition scheme then for any
g\in{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
there exists a unique tree
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}_{n}

such that
g\in[\tau,\bm{M}(\tau)]
. Therefore we can define the map
\bm{T}
from
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
to
{\mathcal{T}}_{n}

such that, for all
g\in[\tau,\bm{M}(\tau)]
,
\bm{T}(g)=\tau
.

\Box

3.3.1
The original Penrose map

The original partition scheme proposed by Penrose was exclusively based on labels
1,\dots,n
(rather than
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
)
and it involves two explicit maps, say
{\rm\bf t}:G_{n}\to T_{n}
and
{\rm\bf m}:T_{n}\to G_{n}
satisfying
Proposition
3.2
. Let us first construct the map

{\rm\bf t}:G_{n}\to T_{n}
.
To define this map we have first of all to choose a root among vertices
{1,2,\dots,n}
. So we
identify for example a vertex among
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
as the root, e.g., to fix the ideas, let the root
be the vertex
1
(as in the original paper of Penrose).
Once the root
1
has been chosen,
let us denote, for any
g\in G_{n}
,
by
d_{g}(i)
the graph distance of the vertex
i
from the root
1
in
g
.
Given thus
g\in G_{n}
, we construct the tree
{\rm\bf t}(g)

as follows.

1) We first delete all edges
\{i,j\}
in
E_{g}

with
d_{g}(i)=d_{g}(j)
.

After this operation we are left
with a connected graph
g^{\prime}
such that
d_{g^{\prime}}(i)=d_{g}(i)
for all vertices
i=1,\dots,n
.
Moreover,
each edge
\{i,j\}
of
g^{\prime}
is such that
|d_{g^{\prime}}(i)-d_{g^{\prime}}(j)|=1
.

2) For any
i\neq 1
let now delete from the graph
g^{\prime}
all edges
\{i,j\}

in
E_{g^{\prime}}
such that
d_{g^{\prime}}(j)=d_{g^{\prime}}(i)-1
except the one with
j
minimal.

The resulting graph
g^{\prime\prime}\doteq{\rm\bf t}(g)
is by construction a connected graph in
G_{n}
, i.e.

{\rm\bf t}(g)\in G_{n}
, which is a subgraph of
g
, i.e.
{\rm\bf t}(g)\subset g
, and which has
no cycles, i.e.
{\rm\bf t}(g)\in T_{n}
. Observe that the map
{\rm\bf t}
is a surjection from
G_{n}
to
T_{n}
(because
{\rm\bf t}(\tau)=\tau
for all
\tau\in T_{n}
).

In order to define the map
{\rm\bf m}:T_{n}\to G_{n}
, i.e. the Penrose partition scheme, let us introduce some notations regarding rooted trees..
First, observe that if
\tau\in T_{n}

is thought as rooted in
1
,
then vertices of
\tau\in T_{n}
may be thought as
partially ordered
so that
each vertex
i
of
\tau
has a unique parent, which we denote by
i^{\prime}
, and

s_{i}
children, denoted by
i^{1},\dots,i^{s_{i}}
. The number
s_{i}
is also called the branching factor of
i
.
Of course, the root has no parent. For any
\tau\in T_{n}
we also denote by
d_{\tau}(i)

the tree distance of the vertex
i
from the root
1
(
d_{\tau}(i)
is also called the

generation number
of the vertex
i
in
\tau
). If
i
is a vertex of
\tau

such that
s_{i}=0
(i.e.
i
has no children) then
i
is called a
leaf
(or end-point) of
\tau
.

Definition 3.6
(Penrose partition scheme)

The Penrose partition scheme is
the map
{\rm\bf m}:T_{n}\to G_{n}
such that to each tree
\tau\in T_{n}
associates the graph

{\rm\bf m}(\tau)\in G_{n}
formed by adding to
\tau

all edges
\{i,j\}
such that either:

(p1)

d_{\tau}(i)=d_{\tau}(j)
(edges between vertices of the same generation), or

(p2)

d_{\tau}(j)=d_{\tau}(i)-1
and
j>i^{\prime}
(edges between vertices with generations differing by one).

It is clear, by construction, that, for any
\tau\in T_{n}
, we have
{\rm\bf t}({\rm\bf m}(\tau))=\tau
and if
g\in G_{n}
such that
{\rm\bf t}(g)=\tau
then
g\in[\tau,{\rm\bf m}(\tau)]
. In other words,
the maps
{\rm\bf m}
and
{\rm\bf t}
satisfy Proposition
3.2
and the map
{\rm\bf m}
is a partition scheme.

The original
Penrose identity
[
35
]
, involving the explicit map
{\rm\bf m}
, is the following

Theorem 3.4

Let
V
be a pair potential and let
{\rm\bf m}
be the map described in
Definition
3.6
. Then the following identity holds.

\Phi^{T}({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})~=~\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}e^{-\beta\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E_{{\rm\bf m}(\tau)}\backslash E_{\tau}}V(x_{i}-x_{j})}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}\left(e^{-\beta V(x_{i}-x_{j})}-1\right)

[formula]

The problem with this identity is that, supposing stability of the potential
V(x)
, it is in general very hard (if not impossible) to efficiently estimate
the factor
-\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E_{{\rm\bf m}(\tau)}\backslash E_{\tau}}V(x_{i}-x_{j})
using stability. In other words, let us consider the following conjecture

Conjecture 3.1

Let
{\rm\bf m}
be the map described in Definition
3.6
. Let
V(x)
be a stable pair potential with stability constant
B
. Then there exists a constant

\tilde{B}
such that
for any
n\geq 2
, for any
\tau\in T_{n}
and for any
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{dn}
it holds

\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E_{{\rm\bf m}(\tau)}\backslash E_{\tau}}V(x_{i}-x_{j})\geq-\tilde{B}n

If the conjecture above is true, then we would immediately get from (3.51) the inequality

|\Phi^{T}({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})|~\leq~{e^{\beta\tilde{B}n}}\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in\tau}\left|e^{-\beta V(x_{i}-x_{j})}-1\right|

and thus, recalling (3.15), the Mayer coefficients would be bounded as follows

C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)\leq{1\over|\Lambda|}{e^{\beta\tilde{B}n}\over n!}\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}\int_{\Lambda}dx_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}dx_{n}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}\left|e^{-\beta V(x_{i}-x_{j})}-1\right|\leq

\leq{e^{\beta\tilde{B}n}\over n!}\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}dx\left|e^{-\beta V(x)}-1\right|\right]^{n-1}\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}1~\leq~{e^{\beta\tilde{B}n}\over n!}C(\beta)^{n-1}n^{n-2}

The latter estimates yield a lower bound for the convergence radius
R_{V}
of the Mayer series as

R_{V}\geq{1\over e^{\beta\tilde{B}+1}C(\beta)}

which would have been better than the Penrose-Ruelle bound (3.23) provided
\tilde{B}\leq 2B
.

Unfortunately, Conjecture
3.1
, as far as the map
{\rm\bf m}
of Definition
3.6
is concerned, has never been proven to be true. We will see later that using a
partition scheme different from
{\rm\bf m}
, it is possible to prove the Conjecture
3.1
.

Let us conclude this section by showing a first consequence of the tree graph identity (3.51). We show that
when the potential is positive the Mayer series of the pressure has the remarkable property
to be an alternate series. Using the tree graph identity (3.51), it is
very easy to see the following.

Proposition 3.3

If
V({x})\geq 0
then

\Phi^{T}({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})=(-1)^{n-1}|\Phi^{T}({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})|

and therefore the Mayer series of the
finite volume pressure is, for any
\Lambda
, an alternate series, i.e.

(-1)^{n-1}C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)\geq 0

Proof
.
We have

\Phi^{T}({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})~=~\sum\limits_{g\in G_{n}}\prod\limits_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}\left[e^{-\beta V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})}-1\right]

and, using the tree graph identity (3.51), with, for any fixed
{x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n}
, we can write

\displaystyle\Phi^{T}({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}[e^{-\beta V(x_{i}-x_{j})}-1]e^{-\beta\sum\limits_{\{i,j\}\in E_{{\rm\bf m}(\tau)}\backslash E_{\tau}}V(x_{i}-x_{j})}

\displaystyle=~(-1)^{n-1}\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}[1-e^{-\beta V(x_{i}-x_{j})}]e^{-\beta\sum\limits_{\{i,j\}\in E_{{\rm\bf m}(\tau)}\backslash E_{\tau}}\!\!\!\!\!\!V(x_{i}-x_{j})}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n-1}\left|\Phi^{T}({x}_{1},\dots,{x}_{n})\right|

\Box

An interesting consequence of such proposition is that for positive
potential the Mayer series with convergence radius
R
has surely
a singularity at the point
\lambda~=~-R
. This fact is quite
unpleasant, since the singularity occurs in a non physical region.

The Penrose partition scheme can be used to prove the following identity.

Proposition 3.4

Let
G_{n}
be the set of all connected graphs with vertex set
[n]
and let, for any
g\in G_{n}
, be
E_{g}
its edge set with cardinality
|E_{g}|
. Then

\sum_{g\in G_{n}}(-1)^{|E_{g}|}=(-1)^{n-1}(n-1)!

Proof
Recalling that
V_{ij}
can take the value
+\infty
, let us consider the case in which
V_{ij}=+\infty
for all
\{i,j\}\in E_{n}
and let us apply the general Penrose identity
(3.50) with
\mathfrak{M}={\rm\bf m}
. We have in this case

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{g\in G_{n}}~\prod\limits_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}\left[e^{-V_{ij}}-1\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{g\in G_{n}}(-1)^{|E_{g}|}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}e^{-\sum\limits_{\{i,j\}\in E_{{\rm\bf m}(\tau)}\backslash E_{\tau}}V_{ij}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}\left(e^{-V_{ij}}-1\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}\atop{\rm\bf m}(\tau)\backslash E_{\tau}=\emptyset}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}\left(e^{-V_{ij}}-1\right)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n-1}\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}\atop{\rm\bf m}(\tau)\backslash E_{\tau}=\emptyset}1

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n-1}(n-1)!

The last identity follows from the fact that
\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}:~{\rm\bf m}(\tau)\backslash E_{\tau}=\emptyset}1=(n-1)!
. Indeed the trees
\tau\in T_{n}
such that
{\rm\bf m}(\tau)\backslash E_{\tau}=\emptyset
are
the trees with incident numbers
d_{1},\dots,d_{n}
such that
d_{1}=1
, and
d_{j}\leq 2
for a single
j\in[n]\setminus\{1\}
and
d_{k}=2
for all
k\in[n]\setminus\{1,j\}
(i.e. the linear trees rooted at 1), which are clearly
(n-1)!
(i.e the permutations of the set
\{2,3,\dots,n\}
).

3.4
The hard-sphere gas via Penrose identity

The original Penrose identity (3.51), although it has not been proven useful to deal with general stable potentials, has been proven to be useful and very efficient
for purely hard-core pair potentials. Namely for those potentials of the form

V^{a}_{\rm h.c.}(x)=\begin{cases}+\infty&{\rm if}~|x|\leq a\\
0&{\rm otherwise}\end{cases}

[formula]

where
a>0
. We remind that particles interacting via the pair potential
V^{a}_{\rm h.c.}(x)
are in fact a system of
free hard spheres of diameter
a>0
.

Observe that the potential
V^{a}_{\rm h.c.}
is stable with stability constant
B=0
(since
V^{a}_{\rm h.c.}
is non-negative) and tempered (since

\int_{x>a}V^{a}_{\rm h.c.}(|x|)dx=0
).
We can therefore apply Theorem
3.2
which gives us the following lower bound the convergence radius of the Mayer series for such a system

R_{{V^{a}_{\rm h.c.}}}\geq{1\over eS_{d}(a)}

[formula]

where

S_{d}(a)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|e^{-\beta V^{a}_{\rm h.c.}(|x|)}-1|dx=\int_{|x|\leq a}1dx

is the volume of the
d
-dimensional sphere of radius
a
.

Let us now use the Penrose tree graph identity (3.51) to bound directly the Mayer coefficients of a system of free hard spheres
of diameter
a
(i.e particles interacting via the potential (3.52)).

Given
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{dn}
, let us use the short notation
i\sim j
when
|x_{i}-x_{j}|>a
and
i\nsim j
when
|x_{i}-x_{j}|\leq a
.

Definition 3.7

Let
V^{a}_{\rm h.c.}(x)
be a purely hard core pair potential as in formula (3.52).
Then, for any
n\geq 2
and any
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{dn}
,
we define the set of Penrose trees
P_{a}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\subset T_{n}

as follows. A tree
\tau\in T_{n}
belongs to
P_{a}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
if the following conditions are satisfied

(t0)

if
\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}
then
i\nsim j
(i.e.
|x_{i}-x_{j}|\leq a
)

(t1)

if two vertices
i
and
j
are cousins in
\tau
(i.e. such that
d_{\tau}(i)=d_{\tau}(j)
), then
i\sim j
(i.e.
|x_{i}-x_{j}|>a
);

(t2)

if two vertices
i
and
j
are such that
d_{\tau}(j)=d_{\tau}(i)-1
and
j>i^{\prime}
, then
i\sim j
(i.e.
|x_{i}-x_{j}|>a
);

Then we have the following

Theorem 3.5

For any purely hard core pair potential

{V^{a}_{\rm h.c.}}
, for any
n\geq 2
, for any
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dn}
and for any
\beta\in(0,+\infty)

it holds the identity

\sum_{g\in G_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}\left(e^{-\beta{V^{a}_{\rm h.c.}}(x_{i}-x_{j})}-1\right)~=~(-1)^{n-1}\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{\{P_{a}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\}}(\tau)

[formula]

where

\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{\{P_{a}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\}}(\tau)=\begin{cases}1&{\text{i}f}\tau\in P_{a}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\\
0&{\text{o}therwise}\end{cases}

Proof
. Observe that, by definition (3.52) we have that
\beta{V^{a}_{\rm h.c.}}(x)={V^{a}_{\rm h.c.}}(x)
, for any
\beta\in(0,+\infty)
and any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Using now (3.51) we have

\sum_{g\in G_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}\left(e^{-\beta{V^{a}_{\rm h.c.}}(x_{i}-x_{j})}-1\right)=\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}w_{\tau}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})

where

w_{\tau}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=e^{-\sum\limits_{\{i,j\}\in E_{{\rm\bf m}(\tau)}\backslash E_{\tau}}{V^{a}_{\rm h.c.}}(x_{i}-x_{j})}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}\left(e^{-{V^{a}_{\rm h.c.}}(x_{i}-x_{j})}-1\right)

Now observe that

\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}\left(e^{-{V^{a}_{\rm h.c.}}(x_{i}-x_{j})}-1\right)=\begin{cases}(-1)^{n-1}&{\rm if}~|x_{i}-x_{j}|\leq a~{\rm for\;all}~\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}\\
0&{\rm otherwise}\end{cases}

and

e^{-\sum\limits_{\{i,j\}\in E_{{\rm\bf m}(\tau)}\backslash E_{\tau}}{V^{a}_{\rm h.c.}}(x_{i}-x_{j})}=\begin{cases}1&{\rm if}~|x_{i}-x_{j}|>a~{\rm for\;all}~\{i,j\}\in E_{{\rm\bf m}(\tau)}\backslash E_{\tau}\\
0&{\rm otherwise}\end{cases}

Therefore

w_{\tau}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})~=~\begin{cases}(-1)^{n-1}&{\rm if}~|x_{i}-x_{j}|\leq a\;{\rm for\;all\;}\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}~{\rm and}\atop~~~~~|x_{i}-x_{j}|>a\;{\rm for\;all\;}\{i,j\}\in E_{{\rm\bf m}(\tau)}\backslash E_{\tau}\\
\\
0&{\rm otherwise}\end{cases}

Now, recalling the Definition
3.6
of the map
{\rm\bf m}
, we have

\{i,j\}\in E_{{\rm\bf m}(\tau)}\setminus E_{\tau}~~~\Longrightarrow~~~{\rm either}~d_{\tau}(i)=d_{\tau}(j)\;{\rm or}\;d_{\tau}(j)=d_{\tau}(i)-1\;{\rm and}\;j>i^{\prime}

whence

w_{\tau}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=(-1)^{n-1}\begin{cases}1&{\rm if}~{{|x_{i}-x_{j}|\leq a~{\rm for\;all\;}\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}~{\rm and}}\atop~~~|x_{i}-x_{j}|>a\;{{\rm for\;all\;}\;\{i,j\}\in E_{{\rm\bf m}(\tau)}\setminus E_{\tau}}}\\
\\
0&{\rm otherwise}\end{cases}

I.e., recalling the Definition
3.7
concerning the Penrose trees,

w_{\tau}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=(-1)^{n-1}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{\{P_{a}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\}}(\tau)

[formula]

\Box

We now derive a useful inequality from (3.54). To do this we need one more definition.

Definition 3.8

Let
V^{a}_{\rm h.c.}(x)
be a purely hard core pair potential as in formula (3.52).
Then, for any
n\geq 2
and any
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{dn}
,
we define the set of weakly Penrose trees
P^{*}_{a}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\subset T_{n}

as follows. A tree
\tau\in T_{n}
belongs to
P^{*}_{a}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
if the following conditions are satisfied

(t0)

if
\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}
then
i\nsim j
(i.e.
|x_{i}-x_{j}|\leq a
);

(t1)

if two vertices
i
and
j
are siblings (i.e.
d_{\tau}(i)=d_{\tau}(j)
and moreover they have the same parent
i^{\prime}=j^{\prime}
), then
i\sim j
(i.e.
|x_{i}-x_{j}|>a
);

Then we have the following

Theorem 3.6

For any purely hard core pair potential

V^{a}_{\rm h.c.}

it holds the inequality

|\sum_{g\in G_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}\left(e^{-{V^{a}_{\rm h.c.}}(x_{i}-x_{j})}-1\right)|~\leq~\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}w^{*}_{\tau}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})

[formula]

with

w^{*}_{\tau}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{\{P^{*}_{a}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\}}(\tau)

Proof
. The inequality follows immediately from Theorem
3.5
by noting that
P^{*}_{a}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\supset P_{a}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
.
\Box

Observe now that

w^{*}_{\tau}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\begin{cases}1&{\rm if}~{|x_{i}-x_{j}|\leq a\;{\rm for\;all\;}\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}~{\rm and}\atop~~~~~|x_{i}-x_{j}|>a\;{\rm for\;all\;}i,j\;{\rm siblings\;in\;}\tau\\
\\
0&{\rm otherwise}\end{cases}

[formula]

Therefore
n
-order Mayer coefficient for a system interacting via the potential
{V^{a}_{\rm h.c.}}
is bounded by

|C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)|\leq{1\over n!}\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}S_{\Lambda}(\tau)

[formula]

with

S_{\Lambda}(\tau)={1\over|\Lambda|}\int_{\Lambda}dx_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}dx_{n}w^{*}_{\tau}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})

By (3.57) we have

S_{\Lambda}(\tau)\;\leq\;g_{d}(d_{1})\prod_{i=2}^{n}g_{d}(d_{i}-1)

[formula]

where
d_{i}
is the degree of the vertex
i
in
\tau
,

g_{d}(k)\;=\;\int_{|x_{i}|\leq a\atop|x_{i}-x_{j}|>a}dx_{1}\dots dx_{k}\;=\;a^{dk}\int_{|y_{i}|\leq 1\atop|y_{i}-y_{j}|>1}dy_{1}\dots dy_{k}

for
k
positive integer, and
g_{d}(0)=1
by definition. Recalling that
S_{d}(a)
denotes the volume of the
d
-dimensional sphere of radius
a
, it is convenient to write

g_{d}(k)\;=\;[S_{d}(a)]^{k}\,\widetilde{g}_{d}(k)

[formula]

with

\widetilde{g}_{d}(k)\;=\;{1\over[S_{d}(1)]^{k}}\int_{|y_{i}|\leq 1\atop|y_{i}-y_{j}|>1}dy_{1}\dots dy_{k}

[formula]

for
k
positive integer and
\widetilde{g}_{d}(0)=1
. We observe
that
\widetilde{g}_{d}(k)\leq 1
for all values of
k
.
From
(3.59)–(3.61) we conclude that

\displaystyle S_{\Lambda}(\tau)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle[S_{d}(a)]^{d_{1}}\,\widetilde{g}_{d}(d_{1})\prod_{i=2}^{n}[S_{d}(a)]^{d_{i}-1}\,\widetilde{g}_{d}(d_{i}-1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[S_{d}(a)]^{n-1}\,\widetilde{g}_{d}(d_{1})\prod_{i=2}^{n}\widetilde{g}_{d}(d_{i}-1)\;.

The last identity follows from the fact that for every tree of
n
vertices,

d_{1}+\cdots+d_{n}=2n-2
.
The
\tau
-dependence of this last bound is only through the degree of the vertices,
hence it leads, upon insertion in (3.58), to the inequality

|C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)|\;\leq\;{[S_{d}(a)]^{n-1}\over n!}\sum_{d_{1},...,d_{n}:\,d_{i}\geq 1\atop d_{1}+...+d_{n}=2n-2}\widetilde{g}_{d}(d_{1})\,\prod_{i=2}^{n}\widetilde{g}_{d}(d_{i}-1)\,{(n-2)!\over\prod_{i=1}^{n}(d_{i}-1)!}\;\leq

\leq{[S_{d}(a)]^{n-1}\over n}\sum_{d_{1},...,d_{n}:\,d_{i}\geq 1\atop d_{1}+...+d_{n}=2n-2}{\widetilde{g}_{d}(d_{1})\over d_{1}!}\,\prod_{i=2}^{n}{\widetilde{g}_{d}(d_{i}-1)\,\over(d_{i}-1)!}\;

where we recall that the quantity
{(n-2)!/[\prod_{i=1}^{n}(d_{i}-1)!]}
in the first line of inequality above is precisely
the number of trees with
n
vertices and fixed degrees

d_{1},\ldots,d_{n}
, according to Cayley formula and in the second line we have used the bound
d_{1}\leq n-1
.

At this point, following
[
39
]
,
we multiply and divide by
\mu^{n-1}
where
\mu>0
is a parameter to
be chosen in an optimal way. This leads us to the inequality

\displaystyle|C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{1\over n}\left[{[S_{d}(a)]\over\mu}\right]^{n-1}\sum_{d_{1},...,d_{n}:\,d_{i}\geq 1\atop d_{1}+...+d_{n}=2n-2}{{\widetilde{g}}_{d}(d_{1})\,\mu^{d_{1}}\over d_{1}!}\prod_{i=2}^{n}{\widetilde{g}_{d}(d_{i}-1)\,\mu^{d_{i}-1}\over(d_{i}-1)!}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{1\over n}\left[{[S_{d}(a)]\over\mu}\right]^{n-1}{\Bigg(\sum_{s\geq 0}{\widetilde{g}_{d}(s)\mu^{s}\over s!}\Bigg)^{n}}

Therefore the convergence radius of the Mayer series of the of the gas of free hard spheres of diameter
a
admit the following lower bound

R_{{V^{a}_{\rm h.c.}}}\geq{1\over S_{d}(a)}\max_{\mu>0}{\mu\over C_{d}(\mu)}

where

C_{d}(\mu)\;=\;\sum_{s\geq 0}{\widetilde{g}_{d}(s)\over s!}\,\mu^{s}

(attention! This is a polynomial in
\mu
).
Let us show that for
d=2

the quantitative improvement given by this condition
with respect to the classical bound (3.53)
can be substantial.
In the
d=2
case (i. e. the two-dimensional hard sphere gas)

C_{2}(\mu)\;=\;\sum_{s=0}^{5}{\widetilde{g}_{2}(s)\over s!}\mu^{s}

where, by definition,
\widetilde{g}_{2}(0)=\widetilde{g}_{2}(1)=1
.
The factor
\widetilde{g}_{2}(2)
can be explicitly evaluated in terms of
straightforward integrals and we get

\widetilde{g}_{2}(2)={1\over\pi^{2}}\int_{|x|\leq 1}d^{2}x\int_{|x^{\prime}|\leq 1}d^{2}x^{\prime}\Theta(|x-x^{\prime}|>1)

where
\Theta(|x-x^{\prime}|>1)=1
if
|x-x^{\prime}|>1
and zero otherwise.
Using polar coordinates

\widetilde{g}_{2}(2)={2\pi\over\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}A(\rho)\rho d\rho

where
A(\rho)
is the area of the region
S_{0}\backslash S_{\rho}

with
S_{0}=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:x^{2}+y^{2}\leq 1\}
and

S_{\rho}=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:(x-\rho)^{2}+y^{2}\leq 1\}
.
We get

A(\rho)=2\left[\int_{-1}^{\rho/2}\sqrt{1-x^{2}}dx-\int_{-1+\rho}^{\rho/2}\sqrt{1-(x-\rho)^{2}}\,dx\right]=4\int_{0}^{\rho/2}\sqrt{1-x^{2}}dx=

=2\left[\arcsin(\rho/2)+{\rho\over 2}\sqrt{1-{\rho^{2}\over 4}}\right]

Hence

\widetilde{g}_{2}(2)={2\over\pi}\int_{0}^{1}A(\rho)\rho d\rho={4\over\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\left[\arcsin(\rho/2)+{\rho\over 2}\sqrt{1-{\rho^{2}\over 4}}\right]\rho d\rho=

={16\over\pi}\int_{0}^{1/2}\left[u\arcsin(u)+{u^{2}}\sqrt{1-{u^{2}}}\right]du

But, since

\int u\arcsin(u)du={1\over 4}\left[2u^{2}\arcsin(u)-\arcsin(u)+u\sqrt{1-u^{2}}\right]

and

\int u^{2}\sqrt{1-u^{2}}={1\over 8}\left[\arcsin(u)+u\sqrt{1-u^{2}}(1-2u^{2})\right]

We obtain

\widetilde{g}_{2}(2)={3\sqrt{3}\over 4\pi}

The other terms of the sum can be numerically evaluated (e.g. using Mathematica),
obtaining

\widetilde{g}_{2}(3)=0,0589\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\widetilde{g}_{2}(4)=0,0013\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\widetilde{g}_{2}(5)\leq 0,0001

Choosing
\mu=\left[8\pi\over 3\sqrt{3}\right]^{1/2}
(a value for which
{\mu\over C_{d}(\mu)}
is close to its maximum) we get

R_{{V^{a}_{\rm h.c.}}}\geq{1\over S_{d}(a)}\;0.5107

This should be compared with the bound
R_{{V^{a}_{\rm h.c.}}}\geq{1/e\over S_{d}(a)}
obtained
through the classical condition (3.53).

3.5
Stable and tempered potentials

The Penrose map
\bm{m}
described above is not suitable to control the factor

\exp\{-\beta\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\bm{m}(\tau)}\backslash E^{+}_{\tau}}V(x_{i}-x_{j})\}

appearing in (3.51). The key point is thus to find another partition scheme
\bm{M}
in such a way that is possible to find a good bound for such a factor.
We will construct explicitly our new partition scheme
\bm{M}:{\mathcal{T}}_{n}\to{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
by first defining an auxiliary map

\bm{T}:{\mathcal{G}}_{n}\to{\mathcal{T}}_{n}
and then deriving
\bm{M}
from
\bm{T}
according to Proposition
3.2
.

We thus start
by first defining the map
\bm{T}
from
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
to

{\mathcal{T}}_{n}
.

For a given pair potential
V
and a fixed
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{dn}
, call
V_{ij}=\beta V(x_{i}-x_{j})
for all
\{i,j\}\in E_{n}
. Let us establish a total order
\succ
in the set
E_{n}
in such way that

\{i,j\}\succ\{k,l\}
implies
V_{ij}\geq V_{kl}
. Using this total order we can associate to any
g\in{\cal G}_{n}
a unique
minimal spanning tree
\bm{T}(g)
of
g
via the so-called Kruskal algorithm. Namely,
\bm{T}(g)
is the spanning tree
of
g
constructed by choosing its edges one after the other from the set
E_{g}
in such way that at each step the chosen
edge is always the lowest edge (in the order
\succ
) that does not create a cycle.

Definition 3.9

For a given pair potential
V
and a fixed
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{dn}
, the map
\bm{T}:{\cal G}_{n}\to\mathcal{T}_{n}
associates to any
g\in{\cal G}_{n}
its unique minimal spanning tree
\bm{T}(g)

w.r.t. the order
\succ
defined above via the Kruskal algorithm.

We can now define the
map

\bm{M}
from
{\mathcal{T}}_{n}
to
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
as follows.

Definition 3.10

The map
\bm{M}:{\mathcal{T}}_{n}\to{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
is such that
\bm{M}(\tau)
is the graph whose edges are
the
\{i,j\}
such that
\{i,j\}\succ\{k,l\}
for every edge
\{k,l\}\in E_{\tau}
belonging to the path from
i
to
j
through
\tau
.

Thus we have constructed

\textstyle{{\mathcal{G}}_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bm{T}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{T}}_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bm{M}}

Observe that
\tau\subset\bm{M}(\tau)
and
\bm{T}(g)\subset g
.
We now show that these two maps
{\bm{T}}
,
{\bm{M}}
satisfy the hypothesis of Proposition
3.2
.

Lemma 3.2

Let
\bm{T}
be the and
\bm{M}
be the maps given in Definitions

3.9
and
3.10
respectively. Then

\bm{T}^{-1}(\tau)=\{g\in{\mathcal{G}}_{n}:\,\tau\subset g\subset\bm{M}(\tau)\}

and therefore
\bm{M}
is a partition scheme in
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
.

Proof.

Let
g\in\bm{T}^{-1}(\tau)
. We have
\tau=\bm{T}(g)\subset g
. Now take
\{i,j\}\in E_{g}
, and let
e\in E_{\tau}

be any edge belonging to the path from
i
to
j
in
\tau
.
Consider the graph
\tau^{\prime}
obtained from
\tau
after replacing the edge
e
by
\{i,j\}
. Clearly
\tau^{\prime}
is connected and has
n-1
edges,
so it is a tree. By minimality of
\tau
we must have
\{i,j\}\succ e
, whence
\{i,j\}\in E_{\bm{M}(\tau)}
. Therefore
g\subset\bm{M}(\tau)
.

Conversely, let
\tau\subset g\subset\bm{M}(\tau)
. We must show
\bm{T}(g)=\tau
. By cardinality, it suffices to show
\bm{T}(g)\subset\tau
.
Proceeding by contradiction, take
\{i,j\}\in E_{\bm{T}(g)}\setminus E_{\tau}
. Consider the path
p^{\tau}(\{i,j\})
in
\tau
joining
i
with
j
.
Since
\bm{T}(g)\subset\bm{M}(\tau)
,
\{i,j\}
is greater (w.r.t.
\succ
) than any
edge in the path
p^{\tau}(\{i,j\})
. If we remove
\{i,j\}
from
\bm{T}(g)
, the tree
T(g)
splits into two trees.
Necessarily, at least one of the edges, say
f
, in
the path
p^{\tau}(\{i,j\})
joins a vertex of one tree with a vertex of the other. Thus, by adding this edge we obtain a connected
graph with
n-1
edges, a new tree
\tau^{\prime}\subset g
with differs from
\bm{T}(g)
because the edge
\{i,j\}
has been replaced by
f
and
\{i,j\}\succ f
which contradicts the minimality of
\bm{T}(g)
.
\Box

Remark
. The map
\bm{M}:{\mathcal{T}}_{n}\to{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
given in Definition
3.10
is a
partition scheme in
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
. This is our new partition scheme. The advantage to consider this new partition
scheme instead of the one originally proposed
by Penrose is manifestly clear in the following two lemmas. The first of them
follows easily from the general Penrose tree graph identity given in Theorem
3.3
. The second key lemma shows the crucial role played by the new partition scheme
introduced above.

Lemma 3.3

Let
n\geq 2
and let
\{V_{ij}\}_{\{i,j\}\in E_{n}}
be
n(n-1)/2
real numbers
(one for each unordered pair
\{i,j\}\subset[n]
) taking values in
\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
.
Let
\mathfrak{M}:T_{n}\to G_{n}
be any partition scheme in
G_{n}
.
Then the following inequality holds

\left|\sum\limits_{g\in G_{n}}~\prod\limits_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}\left[e^{-V_{ij}}-1\right]\right|~\leq~\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}e^{-\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\mathfrak{M}(\tau)}\backslash E^{+}_{\tau}}V_{ij}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}\left(1-e^{-|V_{ij}|}\right)

[formula]

where

E_{\tau}^{+}=\{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}:~V_{ij}\geq 0\}

[formula]

Proof
. By Theorem
3.3
we have that

\left|\sum_{g\in{\mathcal{G}}_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}\left(e^{-V_{ij}}-1\right)\right|~\leq~\sum_{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}_{n}}e^{-\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\mathfrak{M}(\tau)}\setminus E_{\tau}}V_{ij}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}\left|e^{-V_{ij}}-1\right|

[formula]

Observe now that, for any
\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{n}

\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}|e^{-V_{ij}}-1|=\Big[\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}(1-e^{-|V_{ij}|})\Big]\ e^{-\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}\setminus E^{+}_{\tau}}V_{ij}}

so that

e^{-\sum\limits_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\mathfrak{M}(\tau)}\setminus E_{\tau}}V_{ij}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}\left|e^{-V_{ij}}-1\right|~=~e^{-\sum\limits_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\mathfrak{M}(\tau)}\setminus E^{+}_{\tau}}V_{ij}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}(1-e^{-|V_{ij}|})

[formula]

Inserting now (3.65) into (3.64) we get (3.62).
\Box

Lemma 3.4

Let
n\geq 2
and let
\{V_{ij}\}_{\{i,j\}\in E_{n}}
be
n(n-1)/2
real numbers
(one for each unordered pair
\{i,j\}\subset[n]
) taking values in
\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
.
Suppose that there exist non negative numbers
\{B_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
such that
for each

S\subset[n]
it holds

\sum_{\{i,j\}\subset S}V_{ij}\geq\sum_{i\in S}B_{i}

[formula]

Let
{\bm{M}}:T_{n}\to G_{n}
be the partition scheme in
G_{n}
given in Definition
3.10
.
Then, for any
\tau\in T_{n}
the following inequality holds

\sum_{\{i,j\}\in\bm{E}_{M(\tau)}\setminus E_{\tau}^{+}}V_{ij}\geq-\sum_{i=1}^{n}B_{i}

[formula]

Proof
.
The set of edges
E_{\tau}\setminus E_{\tau}^{+}
forms the forest
\{\tau_{1},...,\tau_{k}\}
.
Let us denote
{\mathcal{V}}_{\tau_{s}}
the vertex set of the tree
\tau_{s}

of the forest.
Assume
i\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\tau_{a}}
,
j\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\tau_{b}}
.
If
a\neq b
, the path from
i
to
j
through
\tau
involves an edge
e
in
E_{\tau}^{+}
. Thus, if in addition
\{i.j\}\in E_{\bm{M}(\tau)}
, we have that
\{i,j\}\succ e
and therefore

V_{ij}\geq V_{e}\geq 0
. If
a=b
, the path from
i
to
j
through
\tau
is contained in
\tau_{a}
. Thus, if in addition

\{i,j\}\notin E_{\bm{M}(\tau)}
, there must be at least an edge
e
in that path such that

\{i,j\}\prec e
and therefore
V_{ij}\leq V_{e}\leq 0
. This allows to bound:

\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\bm{M}(\tau)}\setminus E_{\tau}^{+}}V_{ij}\geq\sum_{s=1}^{k}\sum_{\{i,j\}\subset{\mathcal{V}}_{\tau_{s}}}V_{ij}\geq-\sum_{s=1}^{k}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau_{s}}}B_{i}\geq-\sum_{i=1}^{n}B_{i}~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\Box

From Lemmas
3.3
and
3.4
we immediately get the following theorem.

Theorem 3.7

Let
n\geq 2
and let
\{V_{ij}\}_{\{i,j\}\in E_{n}}
be
n(n-1)/2
real numbers
(one for each unordered pair
\{i,j\}\subset[n]
) taking values in
\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
.
Suppose that there exist non negative numbers
\{B_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
such that
for each

S\subset[n]
it holds

\sum_{\{i,j\}\subset S}V_{ij}\geq\sum_{i\in S}B_{i}

[formula]

Then the following inequality holds.

\left|\sum_{g\in{G}_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}(e^{-V_{ij}}-1)\right|\leq e^{\sum_{i=1}^{n}B_{i}}\sum_{\tau\in{T}_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}(1-e^{-\beta|V_{ij}|})

[formula]

Proof
. By Lemma
3.3
we have that

\big|\sum_{g\in{G}_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}(e^{-V_{ij}}-1)\Big|=\sum_{\tau\in{T}_{n}}e^{-\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E_{{\bm{M}}(\tau)}\setminus E^{+}_{\tau}}V_{ij}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}(1-e^{-|V_{ij}|})

where
\bm{M}
is the map given in Definition
3.10
.
Using then Lemma
3.4
, the inequality (3.69) follows.
\Box

Theorem 3.8

Let
V
be a stable pair potential with stability constant
B
. Then
for any
n\in{}\mathbb{N}
such that
n\geq 2
and any
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dn}
the following inequality holds.

\left|\sum_{g\in{G}_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}(e^{-\beta V(x_{i}-x_{j})}-1)\right|\leq e^{\beta Bn}\sum_{\tau\in{T}_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}(1-e^{-\beta|V(x_{i}-x_{j}|)})

[formula]

Proof.
Just observe that, once
(x_{1},...,x_{n})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dn}
are fixed,
the numbers
V_{ij}=\beta V(x_{i}-x_{j})
satisfy (3.68) with
B_{i}=B
for all
i\in[n]
. So (3.70) follows immedialely
by Theorem
3.7
.
\Box

From Theorem
3.8
we have immediately the following Theorem.

Theorem 3.9

Let
V
be a stable and tempered pair potential with stability constant
B
. Then
the
n
-order Mayer coefficient
C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)
defined in (3.15)
is bounded by

|C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)|\leq e^{\beta Bn}n^{n-2}{[\tilde{C}(\beta)]^{n-1}\over n!}

[formula]

where

\tilde{C}(\beta)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}dx~|e^{-\beta|V(x)|}-1|

[formula]

Therefore the Mayer series (3.9) converges absolutely, uniformly in
\Lambda
,
for any complex
\lambda
inside the disk

|\lambda|<{1\over e^{\beta B+1}\tilde{C}(\beta)}

[formula]

I.e. the convergence radius of the Mayer series (3.9) admits the following lower bound

R_{V}\geq{1\over e^{\beta B+1}\tilde{C}(\beta)}

[formula]

Proof
. From Theorem
3.8
(and also recalling Proposition
3.1
) we have that

\displaystyle|C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)|

\displaystyle\leq{1\over n!}{1\over|\Lambda|}\int_{\Lambda}dx_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}dx_{n}|\Phi^{T}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})|

\displaystyle\leq{1\over n!}{1\over|\Lambda|}\int_{\Lambda}dx_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}dx_{n}e^{\beta Bn}\sum_{\tau\in{T}_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}(1-e^{-\beta|V(x_{i}-x_{j}|)})

\displaystyle\leq{e^{\beta Bn}\over n!}\sum_{\tau\in{T}_{n}}{1\over|\Lambda|}\int_{\Lambda}dx_{1}\dots\int_{\Lambda}dx_{n}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}(1-e^{-\beta|V(x_{i}-x_{j}|)})

\displaystyle\leq~{e^{\beta Bn}}{1\over n!}\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big[1-e^{-\beta|V(x)|}\Big]\,dx\right]^{n-1}\sum_{\tau\in{T}_{n}}1

\displaystyle=~{e^{\beta Bn}}{n^{n-2}\over n!}\left[\tilde{C}(\beta)\right]^{n-1}

This concludes the proof of Theorem
3.9
.
\Box
.

The improvement on the lower bound of the convergence radius of the Mayer series for stable and tempered potentials
given by Theorem
3.9
with respect to Theorem
3.2
is
twofold. First, the factor
e^{\beta B+1}
in (3.74) replaces
the factor
e^{2\beta B+1}
in (3.23). Second, the factor
\tilde{C}(\beta)
in (3.74) replaces the factor
C(\beta)
in
(3.23) and clearly, recalling their definitions (3.72) and (3.21) respectively, we have
\tilde{C}(\beta)\leq C(\beta)
where
the equality only holds if
V
is non-negative (purely repulsive). Moreover observe that while
\tilde{C}(\beta)

grows at most linearly in
\beta
, the factor
C(\beta)
grows exponentially with
\beta
.
So, if we denote by
R_{PR}=[e^{2\beta B+1}C(\beta)]^{-1}
the
Penrose-Ruelle lower bound for the convergence radius given in Theorem
3.2

and by
R^{*}=[e^{\beta B+1}\tilde{C}(\beta)]^{-1}
the lower bound given by Theorem
3.9
for the same convergence radius we get that

{R^{*}/R_{PR}}=e^{\beta B}[{C(\beta)/\tilde{C}(\beta)}]
. This ratio, always greater than one, is the product of two factors,

e^{\beta B}
and
[{\tilde{C}(\beta)/C(\beta)}]
, both
growing exponentially fast
with
\beta
. To give an idea, for a gas of particles interacting via the classical Lennard-Jones potential

V(|x|)={1\over|x|^{12}}-{2\over|x|^{6}}

at inverse temperature
\beta=1
, using the value
B_{\rm LJ}=8.61

for its stability constant (see
[
22
]
), the lower bound (3.74)
is at least
8.5\times 10^{4}
larger than the Penrose-Ruelle lower bound, while for
\beta=10

is at least
7.26\times 10^{43}
larger than the Penrose-Ruelle lower bound.

3.6
Convergence of the Virial expansion

We recall that via the formulas

\beta P_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)~=~{1\over|\Lambda|}\ln\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)\lambda^{n}

[formula]

\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)~=~{1\over|\Lambda|}{\lambda}{\partial\over\partial\lambda}{\ln\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}nC_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)\lambda^{n}

[formula]

it is possible to express the pressure of the system in the grand canonical ensemble in power of the
density
\rho(\beta,\lambda)
. Indeed, by the discussion of the previous section the r.h.s. of (3.76) is a function of
\lambda
which is analytic for
|\lambda|<{1/[e^{\beta B+1}\tilde{C}(\beta)]}

and its derivative respect to
\lambda
calculated at
\lambda=0
is equal to 1.
So one can eliminate
\lambda
in (3.75) and (3.76) to obtain the so-called
Virial expansion of the Pressure
, i.e. the pressure as a (formal) power series of the density
\rho=\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
, in the Grand Canonical Ensemble

\beta P_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)=\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)-\sum_{k\geq 1}{k\over k+1}\beta_{k}(\beta,\Lambda)[\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)]^{k+1}

[formula]

The coefficients
\beta_{k}(\beta,\Lambda)
are combinations of the Mayer coefficients
C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)
with
n\leq k
by algebraic expressions. Namely one can prove that (see e.g. formula (29) p. 319 of
[
33
]
)

\beta_{k}(\beta,\Lambda)=\sum_{n=1}^{k}{(-1)^{n-1}}{(k-1+n)!\over k!}\sum_{{\{m_{2},\dots,m_{k+1}\}\atop m_{i}\in{\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}},~\sum_{i=2}^{k+1}m_{i}=n}\atop\sum_{i=2}^{k+1}(i-1)m_{i}=k}\prod_{i=2}^{k+1}{[{C_{i}(\beta,\Lambda){i}}]^{m_{i}}\over m_{i}!}

[formula]

It is also possible to prove (see e.g. sec. 6.3 of
[
30
]
) that the limit

\beta_{k}(\beta)=\lim_{\Lambda\to\infty}\beta_{k}(\beta,\Lambda)

admits a representation in term of two-connected graphs. Namely,

\beta_{k}(\beta)={1\over n!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}d{x}_{2}\dots\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}d{x}_{n}\sum_{g\in G^{*}_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}\left[e^{-\beta V({x}_{i}-{x}_{j})}-1\right]

[formula]

where here
G^{*}_{n}
is the set of the two-connected graphs with vertex set
[n]
. We recall that a graph
g=(V_{g},E_{g})
is two-connected if, for every vertex
x\in V_{g}
the graph
g\setminus x
is connected.

To study the convergence of the series (3.77) in principle one could try to proceed as we did in the previous section
when we dealed with the convergence of the Mayer series. Namely, we could try to get a bound on the virial coefficients directly from their finite volume expressions
(3.78) or their infinite volume expression (3.79). Unfortunately no method has been found to do this
and the lower bound for the convergence radius of the
virial series has been obtained in 1964 by Lebowitz and Penrose
[
25
]
in a more indirect way. We illustrate here below how to get these bounds. Before doing this we need to introduce some further notations and definitions
which will be useful in order to deduce the lower bound for convergence radius of the Virial series.

Given a pair potential
V
with stability constant
B
we let

B_{n}=\sup_{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{dn}}-{1\over n}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}V(|x_{i}-x_{j}|)

so that

B=\sup_{n\geq 2}B_{n}

We also define

{\bar{B}}_{n}={n\over n-1}B_{n}

and

\bar{B}=\sup_{n\geq 2}\bar{B}_{n}

Then we have clearly, for all
n\geq 2
and all
x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{dn}

\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}V(x_{i}-x_{j})\geq-(n-1)\bar{B}

[formula]

and

\bar{B}\geq B

We call
\bar{B}
the
Basuev stability constant
of the potential
V
, after Basuev who was the first to introduce it in
[
2
]
.
On the other hand, as noted by Basuev,

B
and
\bar{B}
, if not coinciding, should be very close for the large majority of
stable pair potentials.
Indeed, let us first state and prove the following propositions.

Proposition 3.5

Let
V(x)
be a stable and tempered pair potential in
d
dimensions with stability constant
B
and Basuev stability constant
\bar{B}
.

(a)

If

\bar{B}=\limsup\bar{B}_{n}

[formula]

then
B=\bar{B}
.

(b)

If there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\bar{B}=\bar{B}_{m}
, then

\bar{B}\leq{d+1\over d}

[formula]

(c)

If
V(x)=V(|x|)
and
V(|x|)

reaches a negative minimum at
|x|=r_{0}

being negative for
|x|>r_{0}
, then

\bar{B}\leq\begin{cases}{3\over 2}B&{\rm if}~d=1\\
{7\over 6}B&{\rm if}~d=\\
{2d(d-1)+1\over 2d(d-1)}B&{\rm if}~\geq 3\end{cases}

[formula]

Proof.

Let us prove
(a)
.
Suppose by contradiction that
\bar{B}-B=\delta>0
. If this holds
then necessarily there exists a finite
m\geq 2
such that
B=B_{m}
otherwise if
B=\limsup B_{n}
then
B=\bar{B}
.
Now, due to the hypothesis (3.81), for all
\varepsilon>0
there exists
n_{0}
such that
for infinitely many
n>n_{0}

\bar{B}_{n}>\bar{B}-\varepsilon~~~~~~~~~~\Longrightarrow~~~~~~B_{n}>{n-1\over n}(\bar{B}-\varepsilon)={n-1\over n}(B+\delta-\varepsilon)~

choose now
\varepsilon={\delta\over 2}
, then for infinitely many
n
we have that

B_{n}>{n-1\over n}(B+{\delta\over 2})>B~~~~~~~~~~~~\mbox{ as soon as $n>{2B\over\delta}+1$}

in contradiction with the assumption that
B=B_{m}
. Hence we must have
B=\limsup B_{n}=\limsup\bar{B}_{n}=\bar{B}
.

We now prove
(b)
. By hypotheis
\bar{B}=\bar{B}_{m}
.
Since
V
is stable, it is bounded from below and since
V
is tempered
\inf V
cannot be positive.
Let
\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}V(x)=-C
with
C\geq 0
. Then for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
r_{0}
such that

V(r_{0})<-(C-\varepsilon)
.
Take the configuration
(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{d+1})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{d+1}
such that
x_{1}
,
x_{2},\dots,x_{d+1}
are vertices of a
d
-dimensional
hypertetrahedron with sides of length
r_{0}
. Recall that a
d
-dimensional
hypertetrahedron has
d+1
