vertices and
d(d+1)/2
sides.
Then
U(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{d+1})<-{d(d+1)\over 2}(C-\varepsilon)
, which implies that
B>{d\over 2}(C-\varepsilon)
and by the arbitrariness of
\varepsilon
we get
B\geq{d\over 2}C
.
On the other hand, we also have, for any
(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}
that

U(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\geq-{m(m-1)\over 2}C
which implies that
\bar{B}_{m}=\bar{B}\leq{m\over 2}C
. Hence we can write
{d\over 2}C\leq B\leq\bar{B}=\bar{B}_{m}\leq{m\over 2}C
which implies
m\geq d+1
and so
\bar{B}=\bar{B}_{m}={m\over m-1}B_{m}\leq{m\over m-1}B\leq{d+1\over d}B
so that (3.82) holds.

We prove
(c)
.
Let
\inf_{r\geq 0}V(r)=-C
with
C>0
.
First note that
d(d-1)C
is always a lower bound for
B
when
d\geq 3
. Just consider a configuration in which
n
particle
(with
n
as large as we want) are
arranged in close-packed configuration at the sites of a
d
-dimensional face-centered cubic lattice with step
r_{0}
. The energy of such configuration
is (asymptotically
as
n\to\infty
)
less than or equal to
-d(d-1)Cn
since
in a
d
-dimensional face-centered cubic lattice each site has
2d(d-1)
neighbors (see e.g.
[
51
]
) and so there are (asymptotically)

d(d-1)n
pairs of neighbors in the configuration.
On the other hand,
for any
n
-particle configuration
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{dn}
,
it holds that
U(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\geq-n(n-1)C/2
, i.e.
\bar{B}_{n}\leq nC/2
. Now,
if
\bar{B}=\sup_{n}\bar{B}_{n}
is attained at
n\to\infty
then, as previously seen, we have
\bar{B}=B
. So let us suppose
that
\bar{B}=\sup_{n}\bar{B}_{n}
is attained at a certain finite
m
. Then
we must have that
d(d-1)C\leq B\leq\bar{B}\leq mC/2
and so
m\geq 2d(d-1)
. Now if
m=2d(d-1)
then
\bar{B}=B
. So when
\bar{B}>B
then

m>2d(d-1)
and so we have
\bar{B}=\frac{m}{m-1}B_{m}\leq{2d(d-1)+1\over 2d(d-1)}B
. The case
d=1
and
d=2
are treated analogously by just
observing that the close-packed arrangement in
d=1
is simply the cubic lattice with
2
neighbors for each site while for
d=2
is the
triangular lattice with
6
neighbors for each site. So
d(d-1)
must be replaced by
1
for
d=1
and by
3
for
d=2
yielding
m>2
and

m>6
for
d=1
and
d=2
respectively.
\Box

It is also possible to express the bounds on the Mayer coefficients in terms of the the Basuev Stability constant
\bar{B}
.

Proposition 3.6

Let
V
be a stable and regular pair potential with basuev stability constant
\bar{B}
. Then the Mayer coefficients
C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)
defined in (3.10) admit the following bound

|C_{n}(\beta,{\Lambda})|\leq e^{\beta\bar{B}(n-1)}n^{n-2}{[\tilde{C}(\beta)]^{n-1}\over n!}

[formula]

Proof
.
Using the partition scheme
\bm{M}
given in Definition
3.10
let us prove the following inequality.
Given
\tau\in{T}_{n}
abd given
(x_{1},...,x_{n})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dn}
, we have

\sum_{\{i,j\}\in\bm{E}_{M(\tau)}\setminus E_{\tau}^{+}}v(x_{i}-x_{j})\geq-\bar{B}(n-1)

[formula]

Indeed, reasoning indetically as in the proof of Lemma
3.4
(with
v(x_{i}-x_{j})
in place of

V_{ij}
) we have that

\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\bm{M}(\tau)}\setminus E_{\tau}^{+}}v(x_{i}-x_{j})\geq\sum_{s=1}^{k}\sum_{\{i,j\}\subset{V}_{\tau_{s}}}v(x_{i}-x_{j})

Now, by (3.80), for any
I\subset[n]
it holds that

\sum_{\{i,j\}\subset{I}}v(x_{i}-x_{j})\geq-(|I|-1)\bar{B}
Therefore

\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\bm{M}(\tau)}\setminus E_{\tau}^{+}}v(x_{i}-x_{j})\geq\sum_{s=1}^{k}-|(V_{\tau_{s}}|-1)\bar{B}\geq-(n-1)\bar{B}

Proceeding now as in the proof of Theorem
3.9
we get the inequality (3.84).

\Box

Let us now go back to the Virial expansion of the Pressure given in (3.77) and let us state and prove the following theorem.

Theorem 3.10

Let
V
be a stable and tempered pair potential with Basuev stability constant
\bar{B}
. Then
the convergence radius
{\cal R}
of the Virial series (3.77) admits the following lower bound.

{\cal R}\geq{0.14477\over\tilde{C}(\beta)e^{\beta\bar{B}}}

[formula]

Proof
.
We follow the steps done by Lebowitz and Penrose in
[
25
]
to obtain, via Lagrange inversion, a lower bound for
{\cal R}
.

We start by observing that, due to (3.76) and the fact that
C_{1}(\beta,\Lambda)=1
, there exists a circle
C

of some radius
R<1/[\tilde{C}(\beta)e^{\beta B+1}]
and center in the origin
\lambda=0
of the complex
\lambda
-plane such that

\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)

has only one zero in the disc
{D_{R}}=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}:|\lambda|\leq R\}
and this zero occurs precisely at
\lambda=0
.
Let now
\rho\in\mathbb{C}
be such that

|\rho|<\min_{\lambda\in C}|\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)|

[formula]

Then by Rouché’s theorem
\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
and

\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)-\rho
have the same number of zeros (i.e. one) in the region
D_{R}=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}:|\lambda|\leq R\}
. In other words, for any complex
\rho
satisfying
(3.87) there is only one
\lambda\in D_{R}
such that
\rho=\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
and therefore we can invert the equation
\rho=\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
and write

\lambda=\lambda_{\Lambda}(\beta,\rho)
. Thus, according to Cauchy’s argument principle, we can write the pressure
\beta P_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)

as a function of the density
\rho=\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
as

P_{\Lambda}(\rho,\beta)={1\over 2\pi i}\oint_{\gamma}P_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda){d\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)\over d\lambda}{d\lambda\over\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)-\rho}

[formula]

where
\gamma
can be any circle centered at the origin in the complex
\lambda
-plane fully contained in the region
D_{R}

and such that

|\rho|<\min_{\lambda\in\gamma}|\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)|

[formula]

By standard complex analysis
P_{\Lambda}(\rho,\beta)
is analytic in
\rho
in the region (3.89). Indeed,
once (3.89) is satisfied we can write

{1\over\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)-\rho}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\rho^{n}\over[\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)]^{n+1}}

[formula]

and inserting (3.90) in (3.88) we get

P_{\Lambda}(\rho,\beta)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}c_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)\rho^{n}

[formula]

with

\displaystyle c_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)

\displaystyle={1\over 2\pi i}\oint_{\gamma}P_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda){d\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)\over d\lambda}{d\lambda\over[\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)]^{n+1}}

\displaystyle=-{1\over 2\pi in}\oint_{\gamma}P_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda){d\over d\lambda}\left[{1\over[\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)]^{n}}\right]d\lambda

\displaystyle={1\over 2\pi in}\oint_{\gamma}{dP_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)\over d\lambda}{1\over[\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)]^{n}}d\lambda

\displaystyle=~{1\over 2\pi in\beta}\oint_{\gamma}{1\over[\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)]^{n-1}}{d\lambda\over\lambda}

Therefore

|c_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)|\leq{1\over n\beta}{1\over\left[\min_{\lambda\in\gamma}|\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)|\right]^{n-1}}

[formula]

Inequality (3.92) shows that the convergence radius
{\cal R}
of the series (3.91) (i.e. of the virial series (3.77)) is such
that

{\cal R}\geq\min_{\lambda\in\gamma}|\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)|

[formula]

Therefore the game is to find an optimal circle
\gamma
in the region
D_{R}
which maximizes the r.h.s. of (3.93).
We proceed as follows.
Recalling (3.76) we have, by the triangular inequality, that

|\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)|\geq|\lambda|-\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}n|C_{n}(\beta,\Lambda)||\lambda|^{n}

[formula]

We now use estimate (3.84) to bound the sum in the r.h.s. of (3.94).
Therefore we get

\displaystyle|\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)|

\displaystyle\geq~|\lambda|-\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}{n^{n-1}\over n!}[\tilde{C}(\beta)e^{\beta\bar{B}}]^{n-1}|\lambda|^{n}

\displaystyle=2|\lambda|-{1\over\tilde{C}(\beta)e^{\beta\bar{B}}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{n^{n-1}\over n!}[\tilde{C}(\beta)e^{\beta\bar{B}}|\lambda|]^{n}

Now following
[
25
]
let us set
w
to be the first positive solution of

we^{-w}=\tilde{C}(\beta)e^{\beta\bar{B}}|\lambda|

[formula]

If we take
|\lambda|<1/e^{\beta\bar{B}+1}\tilde{C}(\beta)
(which is
surely inside the convergence region since
\bar{B}\geq B
) then
\tilde{C}(\beta)e^{\beta\bar{B}}|\lambda|<1/e
and since the function
we^{-w}
is
increasing in
the interval
[0,1]
and takes the value
1/e
at
w=1
, there is a unique
w
in the interval
[0,1]
which solves (3.95).
Now we use the Euler’s formula

w=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{n^{n-1}\over n!}(we^{-w})^{n}

to get

|\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)|\geq{w\over\tilde{C}(\beta)e^{\beta\bar{B}}}\left[2e^{-w}-1\right]

[formula]

Observe that the r.h.s. (3.96) is greater than zero when
w
varies in the interval
(0,\ln 2)
. Therefore, any circle
\gamma
around the origin with radius
R_{\gamma}
between zero and

\ln 2/(2e^{\beta\bar{B}+1}\tilde{C}(\beta))
, which corresponds, in force of (3.95), to any
w
in the interval
(0,\ln 2)
, is surely in the region
D_{R}
. So
we have

\max_{\gamma\subset D_{R}}\min_{\lambda\in\gamma}|\rho_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)|\geq{1\over\tilde{C}(\beta)e^{\beta\bar{B}}}\max_{w\in(0,\ln 2)}w\left[2e^{-w}-1\right]

[formula]

which, recalling (3.93) and since
\max_{w\in(0,\ln 2)}{w}[2e^{-w}-1]\geq 0.14477
, concludes the proof.

Part II
Discrete systems

Chapter 4
The Abstract Polymer gas

4.1
Setting

The abstract polymer gas is a discrete model which plays a very important role in many physical situations,
such as spin systems on the lattice at low or high temperature, or continuous or discrete field theories.
The polymer gas was first introduced by Gruber and Kunz
[
20
]
in 1970. In the original Gruber and Kunz
paper polymer were finite subsets of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
(the unit cubic lattice). Later, Kotecyý and Preiss proposed
the abstract model in which polymers were abstract objects belonging to some set
{\cal P}
, the polymer set,
whose unique structure was given by mean of a symmetric and reflexive relation in
{\cal P}
, that they called the incompatibility relation.
As we will see, this is equivalent to assume
that the interaction between polymers occurs via a hard core pair potential.
In the present chapter we
revisit the abstract polymer gas.

We first specify a countable set
{\cal P}
whose elements are all possible polymers (i.e.
{\cal P}
is the

single particle state space
).
We then associate to each polymer
\gamma\in{\cal P}
a complex number
z_{\gamma}
(a positive number in physical situations)
which is interpreted as the
activity
of the polymer
\gamma
. We will denote
\bm{z}=\{z_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in{\cal P}}
and for any

\Lambda\subset{\cal P}
,
\bm{z}_{\Lambda}=\{z_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Lambda}
.

In the general situation polymers interact through a pair potential. Namely, the energy
E
of a configuration
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}

of
n
polymers is given by

E(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}V(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{j})

[formula]

where pair potential
V(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})
is a symmetric function in
{\cal P}\times{\cal P}
taking values in

\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
. We will make the assumption that the pair interaction
V(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})
is purely hard-core.
Namely
V(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})
takes values in the set
\{0,+\infty\}
. Observe that
an hard-core pair potential
V(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})
in
{\cal P}
induces a relation
\mathcal{R}_{V}

in
{\cal P}\times{\cal P}
(i.e.
\mathcal{R}_{V}
is a subset of

{\cal P}\times{\cal P}
). Namely, we say that a pair
(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})
belongs to
\mathcal{R}_{V}
if and only if

V(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})=+\infty
.
Clearly
\mathcal{R}_{V}
is symmetric because by assumption
V
is symmetric.
When
(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}_{V}
(i.e.
V(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})=+\infty
), we write
\gamma\not\sim\gamma^{\prime}
and say that
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
are
incompatible
. Conversely, if

(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})\notin\mathcal{R}_{V}
we say that the polymers
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
are

compatible
and we write
\gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}
. Note that if
V
is such that
\mathcal{R}_{V}
is reflexive, then

\gamma\not\sim\gamma
for all
\gamma\in{\cal P}
. The relation
{\cal R}_{V}
induced by the pair potential
V
is called
the incompatibility relation
.

Fix now a finite set
\Lambda\subset{\cal P}
(the “volume” of the gas). Then, for
z_{\gamma}\geq 0
, the probability to see the configuration

(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in\Lambda^{n}
is given by

Prob(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})={1\over\Xi_{\Lambda}}{1\over n!}{{z_{\gamma_{1}}}{z_{\gamma_{2}}}\dots{z_{\gamma_{n}}}e^{-\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}V(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{j})}}

where the normalization constant
\Xi_{\Lambda}
is
the grand-canonical partition function in the volume
\Lambda
and is given by

\Xi_{\Lambda}(\bm{z}_{\Lambda})=1+\sum_{n\geq 1}{1\over n!}\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in\Lambda^{n}}{z_{\gamma_{1}}}{z_{\gamma_{2}}}\dots{z_{\gamma_{n}}}e^{-\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}V(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{j})}

[formula]

Note that configurations
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}
for which there exists some incompatible pair
\gamma_{i},\gamma_{j}
, i.e. such that
V(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{j})=+\infty

have zero probability to occur, i.e. are forbidden.

Remark
. The fact that
V(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})\geq 0
immediately
implies that
\Xi_{\Lambda}
is convergent and

|\Xi_{\Lambda}(\bm{z}_{\Lambda})|\leq 1+\sum_{n\geq 1}{1\over n!}\Bigg[\sum_{\gamma\subset\Lambda}|z_{\gamma}|\Bigg]^{n}\leq\exp\Big\{\sum_{\gamma\in\Lambda}|z_{\gamma}|\Big\}\leq\max_{\gamma\in\Lambda}e^{|z_{\gamma}||\Lambda|}

Actually,
V(\gamma\,\gamma^{\prime})\geq 0
implies that
\Xi_{\Lambda}(z)
is analytic in the whole
\mathbb{C}^{|\Lambda|}
(
|\Lambda|
is the cardinality of
\Lambda
).

The “pressure” of this gas is defined via the formula

P_{\Lambda}(\bm{z}_{\Lambda})={1\over|\Lambda|}\log\Xi_{\Lambda}(\bm{z}_{\Lambda})

[formula]

While the partition function
\Xi_{\Lambda}(\bm{z}_{\Lambda})
diverges as
\Lambda\to{\cal P}
, the pressure
P_{\Lambda}(\bm{z}_{\Lambda})
is supposed to have
a finite limit at least when
z
varies in some finite polydisc

|z_{\gamma}|\leq\rho_{\gamma}
with
\bm{\rho}=\{\rho_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in{\cal P}}
being some positive function

\bm{\rho}:{\cal P}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}:\gamma\mapsto\rho_{\gamma}
defined on
{\cal P}
independent on
\Lambda
.
So that in principle it should be possible to give an upper bound for
|P_{\Lambda}(\bm{z})|
which is uniform in
\Lambda
.

The pressure (4.3) can be written as a formal series using the
Mayer trick in the partition function (4.2)
by writing the Gibbs factor
as

e^{-\!\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}V(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{j})}\,=\,\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\!\left[(e^{-V(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{j})}-1)+1\right]

Proceeding exactly as in section
3.1.2
with the only difference if that now
V(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{j})
is in place of
V(x_{i}-x_{j})
and
\sum_{\gamma_{i}\in\Lambda}
is in place of

\int_{\Lambda}dx_{i}
we obtain

\log\Xi_{\Lambda}(\bm{z}_{\Lambda})=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{1\over n!}\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in\Lambda^{n}}\phi^{T}(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})\,z_{\gamma_{1}}\dots\,z_{\gamma_{n}}

[formula]

with

\phi^{T}(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})=\begin{cases}1&{\text{i}f}~n=1\\
\sum\limits_{g\in G_{n}}\prod\limits_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}(e^{-V(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{j})}-1)&{\text{i}f}~n\geq 2\end{cases}

[formula]

where
\sum_{g\in{G}_{n}}
is the sum over all connected
graphs between
[n]
.

Observe that, since
V(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})\geq 0
, by Proposition
3.3
it holds

\phi^{T}(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})=(-1)^{n-1}|\phi^{T}(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})|

[formula]

The equation (4.4) makes sense only for those
\bm{z}\in\mathbb{C}^{\cal P}
such that the formal series in the r.h.s. of (4.4) converge absolutely.
To study absolute convergence,
we will consider, for any
\Lambda
, the positive term series

|\log\Xi|_{\Lambda}(\bm{\rho}_{\Lambda})=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{1\over n!}\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in\Lambda^{n}}|\phi^{T}(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})|\rho_{\gamma_{1}}\cdots{\rho_{\gamma_{n}}}

[formula]

for
\bm{\rho}\in(0,\infty)^{{\cal P}}
.
Note that

|\log\Xi_{\Lambda}(\bm{z}_{\Lambda})|\leq|\log\Xi|_{\Lambda}(\bm{\rho}_{\Lambda})

for all
\bm{z}\in\mathbb{C}^{|\Lambda|}
in the poly-disc
\{|z_{\gamma}|\leq\rho_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Lambda}
, so that if we are able to prove
that the series (4.7) converges, for all
\Lambda
,
at some value
\bm{\rho}=\{\rho_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in{\cal P}}\in(0,\infty)^{{\cal P}}
,
then we have also proved that
the series (4.4) converges absolutely, for all
\Lambda
, whenever
\bm{z}
is in the poly-disk
\{|z_{\gamma}|\leq\rho_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in{\cal P}}
.
We also observe that, since
V(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})\geq 0
, by (4.6)

|\log\Xi|_{\Lambda}(\bm{\rho}_{\Lambda})=-\log\Xi_{\Lambda}(-\bm{\rho}_{\Lambda})

[formula]

To study the convergence of the pressure (4.3) it is convenient to consider the quantity

|\Pi|_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{1\over n!}\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in{\cal P}^{n}}|\phi^{T}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})|\,\,\rho_{\gamma_{1}}\cdots{\rho_{\gamma_{n}}}

[formula]

Note that in (4.9) the sum over each polymer
\gamma_{i}
is not anymore restricted to the finite “volume”
\Lambda
and it runs over the full polymer space
{\cal P}
.

If we are able to show that
|\Pi|_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})
converges
for some (bounded) positive function
\bm{\rho}\in[0,\infty)^{\cal P}
, then also the pressure

P_{\Lambda}(\bm{z}_{\Lambda})
converges absolutely, for all
\Lambda
, whenever
z
is in the poly-disk
\{|z_{\gamma}|\leq\rho_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in{\cal P}}
and in this poly-disk it uniformly in
\Lambda
. Indeed
in the poly-disk
\{|z_{\gamma}|\leq\rho_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in{\cal P}}
we have

|P_{\Lambda}(\bm{z}_{\Lambda})|={1\over|\Lambda|}|\log\Xi_{\Lambda}(\bm{z}_{\Lambda})|\leq{1\over|\Lambda|}|\log\Xi|_{\Lambda}(\bm{\rho}_{\Lambda})~=

={1\over|\Lambda|}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{1\over n!}\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in\Lambda^{n}}|\phi^{T}(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})|\rho_{\gamma_{1}}\cdots{\rho_{\gamma_{n}}}=

={1\over|\Lambda|}\sum_{\gamma_{0}\in\Lambda}\rho_{\gamma_{0}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{1\over n!}\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n-1})\in\Lambda^{n-1}}|\phi^{T}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n-1})|\rho_{\gamma_{1}}\cdots{\rho_{\gamma_{n-1}}}~=

\leq~{1\over|\Lambda|}\sum_{\gamma_{0}\in\Lambda}\rho_{\gamma_{0}}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}{1\over(m+1)!}\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{m})\in\Lambda^{m}}|\phi^{T}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}\dots,\gamma_{m})|\rho_{\gamma_{1}}\cdots{\rho_{\gamma_{m}}}~\leq

{1\over|\Lambda|}\sum_{\gamma_{0}\in\Lambda}\rho_{\gamma_{0}}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}{1\over m!}\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in\Lambda^{m}}|\phi^{T}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}\dots,\gamma_{m})|\rho_{\gamma_{1}}\cdots{\rho_{\gamma_{m}}}~=

=~{1\over|\Lambda|}\sum_{\gamma_{0}\in\Lambda}\rho_{\gamma_{0}}|\Pi|_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})~\leq\sup_{\gamma_{0}\in\Lambda}\,\,\rho_{\gamma_{0}}\,|\Pi|_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})~\leq~\sup_{\gamma_{0}\in{\cal P}}\,\,\rho_{\gamma_{0}}\,|\Pi|_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})

In short, for all
\bm{z}
in the polydisk
\{|z_{\gamma}|\leq\rho_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in{\cal P}}
, it holds

|P_{\Lambda}(\bm{z}_{\Lambda})|~\leq~\sup_{\gamma_{0}\in{\cal P}}\,\,\rho_{\gamma_{0}}\,|\Pi|_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})

[formula]

So in the next sections we will focus our attention on the formal series
|\Pi|_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})

defined in equation (4.9). Indeed, when one is able to prove that the series (4.9) then, by (4.10)
he has also proved
the absolute convergence of the pressure of the polymer gas uniformly in the volume
\Lambda
.

4.2
Convergence of the abstract polymer gas

As explained in the previous section, to study absolute convergence of the pressure
we will just need to prove that the positive term series defined in (4.9)

|\Pi|_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{1\over n!}\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in{\cal P}^{n}}|\phi^{T}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})|\,\,\rho_{\gamma_{1}}\cdots{\rho_{\gamma_{n}}}

is convergent for some
\bm{\rho}\in(0,+\infty)^{\cal P}
.

Since the interaction
V(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})
is purely hard core, we can use the original Penrose identity to bound the factor Ursell factor
|\phi^{T}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})|
. We define the
map
\bm{m}:T^{0}_{n}\to G^{0}_{n}
as in Definition
3.6
. By rooting trees in
T_{n}^{0}
in
0
, given
\tau\in T^{0}_{n}
,
d(i)
denotes the depth of the vertex
i
(i.e. its edge distance from
0
)
and that
i^{\prime}
denotes the parent of
i
.
The map
{\rm\bf m}:T^{0}_{n}\to G^{0}_{n}
is therefore the map such that to each tree
\tau\in T^{0}_{n}
associates the graph

{\rm\bf m}(\tau)\in G^{0}_{n}
formed by adding to
\tau

all edges
\{i,j\}
such that either:

(p1)

d_{\tau}(i)=d_{\tau}(j)
(edges between vertices of the same generation), or

(p2)

d_{\tau}(j)=d_{\tau}(i)-1
and
j>i^{\prime}
(edges between vertices with generations differing by one).

As shown previously the map
{\rm\bf m}
is a partition scheme and therefore by Theorem
3.3
we have that

\phi^{T}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})=\sum\limits_{g\in G^{0}_{n}}\prod\limits_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}(e^{-V(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{j})}-1)=\sum_{\tau\in T^{0}_{n}}w_{\tau}(\gamma_{0}\,\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})

with

w_{\tau}(\gamma_{0}\,\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})=e^{-\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E_{{\rm\bf m}(\tau)}\backslash E_{\tau}}V(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{j})}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in\tau}\left(e^{-V(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{j})}-1\right)

As shown in Section
3.4
(see there Theorem
3.5
and formula (3.55)) we have

w_{\tau}(\gamma_{0}\,\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})=(-1)^{n}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{P(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})}(\tau)

where
P(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})
is the set of Penrose trees and

\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{\tau\in P(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})}
is the characteristic function of the set
P(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})
in
T^{0}_{n}
, i.e.

\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{P(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})}(\tau)\;=\;\begin{cases}1&{\text{i}f}~\tau\in P(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})\\
0&{\text{i}fotherwise}\end{cases}

We remind its definition below.

Definition 4.1

Given
(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in{\cal P}^{n+1}
, we define
the set of
Penrose trees

P(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})
, as the set formed by the trees
\tau\in T^{0}_{n}
such that

(t0)

if
\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}
then
\gamma_{i}\nsim\gamma_{j}

(t1)

if two vertices
i
and
j
siblings
then
\gamma_{i}\sim\gamma_{j}
;

(t2)

if two vertices
i
and
j
are not siblings but
d_{\tau}(i)=d_{\tau}(j)
, then
\gamma_{i}\sim\gamma_{j}
;

(t3)

if two vertices
i
and
j
are s. t.
d_{\tau}(j)=d_{\tau}(i)-1
and
j>i^{\prime}
, then

\gamma_{i}\sim\gamma_{j}
.

Therefore we get

\phi^{T}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})\;=\;(-1)^{n}\sum\limits_{\tau\in T_{n}^{0}}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{P(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})}(\tau)

[formula]

Using (4.11) we can now rewrite the formal series (4.9) as

|\Pi|_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{1\over n!}\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in{\cal P}^{n}}\sum\limits_{\tau\in T_{n}^{0}}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{P_{G}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})}(\tau)\,\,{\rho_{\gamma_{1}}}\dots{\rho_{\gamma_{n}}}\;=

\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,=\;\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{1\over n!}\sum\limits_{\tau\in T_{n}^{0}}\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in{\cal P}^{n}}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{P_{G}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})}(\tau)\,\,{\rho_{\gamma_{1}}}\dots{\rho_{\gamma_{n}}}\;=

=\;\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{1\over n!}\sum\limits_{\tau\in T_{n}^{0}}\phi_{\gamma_{0}}(\tau)\;\;\;\;\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,~~~~~~~

[formula]

where

\phi_{\gamma_{0}}(\tau)=\;\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in{\cal P}^{n}}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{P_{G}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})}(\tau)\,\,{\rho_{\gamma_{1}}}\dots{\rho_{\gamma_{n}}}\;

[formula]

The structure of (4.12) is crucial. This equation shows that the formal series
|\Pi|_{\gamma_{0}}(\rho)
can be
reaorganized as a sum over terms associated to labelled trees.

We stress that here the factor
\phi_{\gamma_{0}}(\tau)
depends on the
labelled
tree
\tau
because of the Penrose condition
(t3) which indeed depends on the labelling of the tree.

We will see below that efficient bounds on the factor
\phi_{\gamma_{0}}(\tau)
defined in (4.13) above can be obtained by
choosing a family of trees
\tilde{P}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})
such that

{P}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})\subset{\tilde{P}}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})
.
Below we will consider three possible choice of
\tilde{P}{(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})}
which are the choices which yield the
three known criteria for the convergence of cluster expansion of the abstract polymer gas.

Definition 4.2

Given
(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in{\cal P}^{n+1}
, the set of weakly the Penrose trees, which we denote by is defined as

{P}^{*}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})
, is formed by all trees
\tau
with vertex set
\{0,1,\dots,n\}
and edge set
E_{\tau}
such that

\rm(t0)\;\,

if
\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}
then
\gamma_{i}\not\sim\gamma_{j}

\rm(t1)^{*}

if
i
and
j
are siblings then
\gamma_{i}\sim\gamma_{j}
;

Definition 4.3

Given
(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in{\cal P}^{n+1}
, the set of the Dobrushin trees, which we denote by is defined as

{P}^{\rm Dob}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})
,
is the set the trees
\tau
with vertex set
\{0,1,\dots,n\}
and edge set
E_{\tau}
such that

\rm(t0)\;\,

if
\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}
then
\gamma_{i}\not\sim\gamma_{j}

\rm(t1)^{D}

if
i
and
j
are siblings then
\gamma_{i}\neq\gamma_{j}
;

Definition 4.4

Given
(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in{\cal P}^{n+1}
, the set of Kotecký-Preiss trees, which we denote by
{P}^{\rm KP}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})
is formed by
all trees
\tau
with vertex set
\{0,1,\dots,n\}
and edge set
E_{\tau}
such that

\rm(t0)\;\,

if
\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}
then
\gamma_{i}\not\sim\gamma_{j}

Note that we have, by definition, that

\displaystyle{P}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})

\displaystyle\subset\;{P}^{*}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})

[formula]

\displaystyle\subset\;{P}^{\rm Dob}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})

\displaystyle\subset\;{P}^{\rm KP}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})

So that, by (3.51), we have the bounds

\displaystyle|\phi^{T}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}^{0}}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{{P}^{*}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})}(\tau)

[formula]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}^{0}}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{P^{\rm Dob}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})}(\tau)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}^{0}}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{P^{\rm KP}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})}(\tau)

The latter (4.15), i.e. the worst among the three bound proposed,
is known as the Rota bound. This was the bound which was used by Cammarota
[
9
]
, Brydges
[
7
]
and Simon

[
50
]
to obtain a direct proof of the absolute convergence of the pressure of a Polymer gas by directly
estimating the Ursell coefficent.

Hence, we can bound the positive term series
|\Pi|_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})
defined in (4.9), using of course the first of the estimates (4.15) which is the best
among the three given above, as

|\Pi|_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})\;=\;\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{1\over n!}\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in{\cal P}^{n}}|\phi^{T}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})|{\rho_{\gamma_{1}}}\dots{\rho_{\gamma_{n}}}\;\leq

\leq\;\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{1\over n!}\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in{\cal P}^{n}}\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}^{0}}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{{P}^{*}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})}(\tau){\rho_{\gamma_{1}}}\dots{\rho_{\gamma_{n}}}\;=

=\;\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{1\over n!}\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}^{0}}\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in{\cal P}^{n}}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{{P}^{*}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})}(\tau){\rho_{\gamma_{1}}}\dots{\rho_{\gamma_{n}}}

So we get

|\Pi|_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})\;\leq\;\Pi^{*}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})

[formula]

where

\Pi^{*}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})\;=\;\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{1\over n!}\sum_{\tau\in T^{0}_{n}}\phi^{*}_{\gamma_{0}}(\tau,\bm{\rho})\;

[formula]

with

\phi^{*}_{\gamma_{0}}(\tau,\bm{\rho})=\;\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in{\cal P}^{n}}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{{P}^{*}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})}(\tau)\,\,{\rho_{\gamma_{1}}}\dots{\rho_{\gamma_{n}}}\;

[formula]

Analogously, using the second and third bounds (4.15) we can define two more series
which also majorize the series
|\Pi|_{\gamma_{0}}(\rho)
. Namely

\Pi^{\rm Dob}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})\;=\;\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{1\over n!}\sum_{\tau\in T^{0}_{n}}\phi^{\rm Dob}_{\gamma_{0}}(\tau,\rho)\;

[formula]

and

\Pi^{\rm KP}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})\;=\;\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{1\over n!}\sum_{\tau\in T^{0}_{n}}\phi^{\rm KP}_{\gamma_{0}}(\tau,\rho)\;

[formula]

with

\phi^{\rm Dob}_{\gamma_{0}}(\tau,\bm{\rho})=\;\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in{\cal P}^{n}}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{P^{\rm Dob}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})}(\tau)\,\,{\rho_{\gamma_{1}}}\dots{\rho_{\gamma_{n}}}\;

[formula]

and

\phi^{\rm KP}_{\gamma_{0}}(\tau,\bm{\rho})=\;\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in{\cal P}^{n}}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{P^{\rm KP}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})}(\tau)\,\,{\rho_{\gamma_{1}}}\dots{\rho_{\gamma_{n}}}\;

[formula]

Here it is important to stress that, differently form the factor
\phi_{\gamma_{0}}(\tau)
defined in (4.13),
the three factors
\phi^{*,\,\rm Dob,\,KP}_{\gamma_{0}}(\tau)
do not depend on the labels of the tree
\tau
, but only on its topological structure.
This means that the terms in the series
\Pi^{*}_{\gamma_{0}}(\rho)
can further be grouped together in terms of non unlabelled rooted trees.
We will make this concept precise in the next section. We conclude this section by remarking that inequalities (4.15)
immediately imply

|\Pi|_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})\;\leq\;\Pi^{*}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})\;\leq\;\Pi^{\rm Dob}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})\;\leq\;\Pi^{\rm KP}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})

[formula]

4.2.1
Reorganization of the series
\Pi^{*}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})

We now reorganize the sum over rooted labelled trees appearing
in formula (4.17) in terms of the plane rooted trees. Such reorganization
is motivated by the observation that
the factors (4.18)-(4.22) do not depend on the labels
assigned to the vertices of
\tau
but only its topological structure.
As a matter of fact,
to each labelled ordered rooted tree
\tau\in T^{0}_{n}
we can associate a drawning in the plane known as
the “plane rooted tree” associated to
\tau
.
The drawing of
\tau
is obtained by putting parents at the left of their children which are ordered
in the top-to-bottom order consistently with the order of their labels.
For example the plane rooted trees with
n+1=5
vertices associated to the trees
\bf a
with edge set

\{0,3\},\{1,3\},\{2,3\},\{1,4\}
,
\bf b
with edge set
\{0,2\},\{0,3\},\{1,2\},\{2,4\}
and
\bf c
with edge set

\{0,2\},\{0,4\},\{4,3\},\{1,4\}

are drawn below

Figure

Observe that
\it b
and
\it c
are
different
plane rooted tree
(because of the rule of the ordering of the children from top-to-bottom).

In this way we have defined a map
m:\tau\mapsto m(\tau)
which associate to each labelled tree
\tau\in T^{0}_{n}
a unique drawing
t=m(\tau)
in the plane,
called the
planar rooted tree
associated to
\tau
.
We denote by
{\mathcal{T}}^{0}_{n}=
the set of all planar rooted trees
with
n+1
vertices and by
\mathcal{T}^{0,k}
the
set of planar rooted trees with maximal generation number
k
; let also
\mathcal{T}^{0}=\cup_{n\geq 0}{\mathcal{T}}^{0}_{n}=\cup_{k\geq 0}\mathcal{T}^{0,k}
be
the set of all planar rooted trees.
An element
t\in\mathcal{T}^{0}_{n}
can also be viewed as an equivalence class of elements

\tau\in T_{n+1}
with the equivalence relation being that
two elements
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
are equivalent if the produce the same planar rooted tree. So
when we write
\tau\in t
with
t\in\mathcal{T}^{0}_{n}
we mean that
\tau
is an element of the set of all labelled trees in
T^{0}_{n}

that produce the same plane ordered rooted tree.

We will use the following notations.
Given a vertex
v\neq 0
in a rooted tree (with root
0
), we denote by
v^{\prime}
is parent,
we denote by
s_{v}
the number of its children and we denoted by
v^{1},\dots,v^{s_{v}}
the
children of
v
. If
s_{v}=0
we say that
v
is an end-point or a
leaf
of
\tau
.

Note that the set of vertices of a labeled rooted tree
\tau\in T^{0}_{n}
(a plane rooted tree
t\in\mathcal{T}^{0}_{n}
) can be endowed with a total order
\prec
in a natural way
in such way that for any
v
the father
v^{\prime}
of
v
is such that
v^{\prime}\prec v
and children of any vertex
v
of a labeled tree
\tau\in T^{0}_{n}
(a plane rooted tree
t\in\mathcal{T}^{0}_{n}
) are ordered
following the order of their labels (from high to low); namely, the ordering of
v^{1},\dots,v^{s_{v}}
is such that

v^{1}\prec v^{2}\prec\cdots\prec v^{s_{v}}
.

Clearly the map
\tau\mapsto m(\tau)=t
is many-to-one and the cardinality of the
pre-image of a planar rooted tree

t
(=number of ways of labelling the
n
non-root vertices of the tree with
n
distinct labels
consistently with the rule “from high to low”) is given by

\left|\{\tau\in T^{0}_{n}:m(\tau)=t\}\right|\;=\;{n!\over\prod_{v\succeq 0}s_{v}!}

[formula]

As a matter of fact, it is very easy to count how many labeled trees
\tau\in T^{0}_{n}
belong to the same equivalent class

t
, i.e. are associated to the same plane rooted tree. One have just to count all permutations

\sigma
of
\{0,1,2,\dots,n\}
which leaves the root unchanged and which respect the order of the children
in any vertex. Let
\tau\in T^{0}_{n}
and let
t=[\tau]
the plane root tree associated to

\tau
characterized by the sequence
\{s_{v}\}_{v\succeq 0}
then we have

|[\tau]|={n!\over\prod_{v\succeq 0}s_{v}!}

Indeed,
n!
is the number of all permutations in the set
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
,
while for each vertex
v
,
s_{v}!
are the permutation of the children. So
{n!/\prod_{v\succeq 0}s_{v}!}

is the number of permutations of the vertices of
\tau
different from the root which do not change the order of the children in every vertex.

Now observe that the factor (4.18) depend actually only on the plane rooted tree associated to
\tau
.
We have indeed

\phi^{*}_{\gamma_{0}}(\tau,\bm{\rho})=\phi^{*}_{\gamma_{0}}([\tau],\bm{\rho})=\phi^{*}_{\gamma_{0}}(t,\bm{\rho})=\prod_{v\succeq 0}\Bigg[\sum_{(\gamma_{v^{1}},\dots,\gamma_{v^{s_{v}}})\in{\cal P}^{s_{v}}\atop\gamma_{v^{i}}\not\sim\gamma_{v},\,\,\,\gamma_{v^{i}}\sim\gamma_{v^{j}}}\rho_{\gamma_{v^{1}}}\dots\rho_{\gamma_{v^{s_{v}}}}\Bigg]

[formula]

Note that, since in each vertex
v
the sum over polymers
\gamma_{v_{1}},\dots,\gamma_{v_{s_{v}}}

associated to children of
v
depends on the polymer
\gamma_{v}
associated to
v
,
in the expression above the order of the product is relevant and it is organized in such way that
products corresponding to ancestors are at the left of products corresponding
to descendants.
In the r.h.s. of (4.25) it also adopted the convention that the sum in brackets is equal to 1 for a vertex
v
such that
s_{v}=0
.

We now ready to reorganize the sum in the the r.h.s. of (4.17)

\Pi^{*}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})=\sum_{n\geq 0}{1\over n!}\sum_{\tau\in T^{0}_{n}}\phi^{*}_{\gamma_{0}}(\tau,\bm{\rho})\;=\sum_{n\geq 0}{1\over n!}\sum\limits_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{0}_{n}}\sum_{\tau\in t}\phi^{*}_{\gamma_{0}}(t,\rho)=

=\sum_{n\geq 0}{1\over n!}\sum\limits_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{0}_{n}}\phi^{*}_{\gamma_{0}}(t,\rho)\sum_{\tau\in t}1=\sum_{n\geq 0}{1\over n!}\sum\limits_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{0}_{n}}\phi^{*}_{\gamma_{0}}(t,\rho)|t|=

=\sum_{n\geq 0}\sum\limits_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{0}_{n}}\Big[\prod_{v\succeq 0}{1\over s_{v}!}\Big]\phi^{*}_{\gamma_{0}}(t,\rho)=\sum_{n\geq 0}\sum\limits_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{0}_{n}}\prod_{v\succeq 0}\Bigg[{1\over s_{v}!}\sum_{(\gamma_{v^{1}},\dots,\gamma_{v^{s_{v}}})\in{\cal P}^{s_{v}}\atop\gamma_{v^{i}}\not\sim\gamma_{v},\,\,\,\gamma_{v^{i}}\sim\gamma_{v^{j}}}\rho_{\gamma_{v^{1}}}\dots\rho_{\gamma_{v^{s_{v}}}}\Bigg]

In conclusion we have obtained

{\Pi^{*}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})\;=}

{=\;\sum\limits_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{0}}\prod_{v\succeq 0}\Bigg\{{1\over s_{v}!}\sum_{(\gamma_{v^{1}},\dots,\gamma_{v^{s_{v}}})\in{\cal P}^{s_{v}}}\prod^{s_{v}}_{i=1}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{\{\gamma_{v^{i}}\nsim\gamma_{v}\}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq s_{v}}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{\{\gamma_{v^{i}}\sim\gamma_{v^{j}}\}}\rho_{\gamma_{v^{1}}}\dots\rho_{\gamma_{v^{s_{v}}}}\Bigg\}}

[formula]

In a completely analogous way we also can obtain

{\Pi^{\rm Dob}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})\;=}

{=\;\sum\limits_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{0}}\prod_{v\succeq 0}\Bigg\{{1\over s_{v}!}\sum_{(\gamma_{v^{1}},\dots,\gamma_{v^{s_{v}}})\in{\cal P}^{s_{v}}}\prod^{s_{v}}_{i=1}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{\{\gamma_{v^{i}}\nsim\gamma_{v}\}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq s_{v}}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{\{\gamma_{v^{i}}\neq\gamma_{v^{j}}\}}\rho_{\gamma_{v^{1}}}\dots\rho_{\gamma_{v^{s_{v}}}}\Bigg\}}

[formula]

and

{\Pi^{\rm KP}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})\;=\;\sum\limits_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{0}}\prod_{v\succeq 0}\Bigg\{{1\over s_{v}!}\sum_{(\gamma_{v^{1}},\dots,\gamma_{v^{s_{v}}})\in{\cal P}^{s_{v}}}\prod^{s_{v}}_{i=1}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{\{\gamma_{v^{i}}\nsim\gamma_{v}\}}\rho_{\gamma_{v^{1}}}\dots\rho_{\gamma_{v^{s_{v}}}}\Bigg\}}

[formula]

4.2.2
Trees and convergence

We start by defining a proper domain in the space of function
(0,\infty)^{{\cal P}}
, i.e. the space of the functions

\bm{\mu}:{\cal P}\to(0,\infty):\gamma\mapsto\mu_{\gamma}
.
We are agree that given two functions
\bm{\mu}
and
\bm{\nu}
in
(0,\infty)^{{\cal P}}
, we say that
\bm{\mu}<\bm{\nu}
if
and only if
\mu_{\gamma}<\nu_{\gamma}
for all
\gamma\in{\cal P}
.

Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
and

(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n})\in{\cal P}^{n+1}
, let
b_{n}(\gamma_{0};\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n})\geq 0
.
Once numbers
b_{n}(\gamma_{0};\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n})
are given for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and

(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n})\in{\cal P}^{n+1}
, we can
define a function

\varphi^{b}:(0,\infty)^{{\cal P}}\to(0,\infty]^{{\cal P}}:\bm{u}\mapsto\varphi^{b}(\bm{u})

with entries

[\varphi^{b}(\bm{u})]_{\gamma}\doteq\varphi^{b}_{{\gamma}}(\bm{u})\;=\;1+\sum_{n\geq 1}\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in{\cal P}^{n}}b_{n}(\gamma;\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n})\,u_{\gamma_{1}}\dots u_{\gamma_{n}}

[formula]

We define the set

\mathcal{D}^{b}=\Big\{\bm{u}\in(0,\infty)^{{\cal P}}:\;\;\varphi^{b}_{{\gamma}}(\bm{u})<+\infty,\,\,\forall\gamma\in{\cal P}\Big\}

Then the restriction of
\varphi^{b}
to
\mathcal{D}^{b}
is a function in
(0,\infty)^{\cal P}
,
i.e.

\varphi^{b}_{{\gamma}}(\bm{u})\;<\;\infty,\,\,\forall\gamma\in{\cal P},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\mbox{whenever $\bm{u}\in\mathcal{D}^{b}$}

Note also that, if
\bm{u}\in\mathcal{D}^{b}
and
\bm{u}^{\prime}<\bm{u}
then also
\bm{u}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}^{b}
.

Let now
\bm{\mu}:{\cal P}\to(0,\infty)
be a function in the set

\mathcal{D}^{b}\subset(0,\infty)^{{\cal P}}

and let
\bm{r}\in(0,\infty)^{{\cal P}}
be defined such that
its entries
r_{\gamma}
, as
\gamma
varies in
{\cal P}
, are given by

r_{\gamma}={\mu_{\gamma}\over\varphi^{b}_{{\gamma}}(\bm{\mu})}

[formula]

Note that
\bm{r}\in\mathcal{D}^{b}
because
\bm{r}\leq\bm{\mu}
by construction, since
\varphi^{b}_{\gamma}(\bm{\mu})\geq 1
for all
\gamma\in{\cal P}
. Morevover
the assumption
\bm{\mu}\in(0,\infty)^{{\cal P}}
is equivalent to say

\mu_{\gamma}>0\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\mbox{for all $\gamma_{0}\in{\cal P}$}

[formula]

while assumption
\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{D}^{b}
means that

\varphi^{b}_{\gamma}(\bm{\mu})<+\infty\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\mbox{for all $\gamma\in{\cal P}$}

[formula]

Assumptions (4.31) and (4.32) guarantee that
\bm{r}\in(0,\infty)^{{\cal P}}
, i.e.

r_{\gamma}>0\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\mbox{for all $\gamma\in{\cal P}$}

Let us now consider, for any
\bm{\rho}\in\mathcal{D}^{p}
, the map
T^{\bm{\rho}}=\bm{\rho}\varphi^{b}
.
T^{\bm{\rho}}
is the map

T^{\bm{\rho}}:(0,\infty)^{\cal P}\to(0,\infty]^{\cal P}:\bm{u}\mapsto T^{\bm{\rho}}(\bm{u})

with entries

[T^{\bm{\rho}}(\bm{u})]_{\gamma}\doteq T^{\bm{\rho}}_{\gamma}(\bm{u})=\rho_{\gamma}\varphi^{b}_{\gamma}(\bm{u})\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\gamma\in{\cal P}

From (4.30) we get

\bm{\mu}=T^{\bm{r}}(\bm{\mu})

[formula]

I.e.
\bm{\mu}
is fixed point for the map
T^{\bm{r}}
. So, by (4.33) we have that, for all
\gamma_{0}\in{\cal P}

\mu_{\gamma_{0}}=T^{\bm{r}}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\mu})=

=r_{\gamma_{0}}\;+\;r_{\gamma_{0}}\sum_{\gamma_{1}\in{\cal P}}b_{1}(\gamma_{0};\gamma_{1})\,\mu_{\gamma_{1}}+r_{\gamma_{0}}\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\in{\cal P}^{2}}b_{2}(\gamma_{0};\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\,\mu_{\gamma_{1}}\mu_{\gamma_{2}}+\;\dots

\dots\;+\,r_{\gamma_{0}}\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in{\cal P}^{n}}b_{n}(\gamma_{0};\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n})\,\mu_{\gamma_{1}}\dots\mu_{\gamma_{n}}~+\dots

[formula]

Equation (4.34), recalling the definition (4.29) of
\varphi_{\gamma}^{b}(\bm{\mu})
can be visualized in the diagrammatic form

where

\circ_{\gamma_{0}}=r_{\gamma_{0}}~~~~~~~~~~~\bullet_{\gamma_{i}}=\mu_{\gamma_{i}}

and, for any
n\geq 1

The iteration
[{T^{r}}]^{2}(\mu)=T^{r}(T^{r}(\mu))
corresponds to
replacing each of the bullets by each one of the diagrams of the
expansion for
T^{r}
.

This leads to plane rooted trees of up to two
generations, with open circles at first-generation vertices and bullets
at second-generation ones. The

k
-th iteration of
T
involves all possible
plane rooted trees
previously seen with generation up to
k
.
In each tree of the expansion, vertices of the last generation are occupied
by bullets and all the others by open circles.
Let us recall that
\mathcal{T}^{0,k}
denotes the
set of trees with maximal depth (or maximal generation number) equal to
k
.
A straightforward inductive argument shows that

[T^{\bm{r}}]^{k}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\mu})\;=\;r_{\gamma_{0}}\Bigl[\sum_{\ell=0}^{k-1}\Phi^{(\ell)}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{r})+R^{(k)}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{r},\bm{\mu})\Bigr]

[formula]

with

\Phi^{(\ell)}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{r})\;=\,\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{0,\ell}}\,\prod_{v\succeq 0}\Bigg\{\sum_{(\gamma_{v^{1}},\dots,\gamma_{v^{s_{v}}})\in{\cal P}^{s_{v}}}b_{s_{v}}(\gamma_{v};\gamma_{v^{1}},\ldots,\gamma_{v^{s_{v}}})\,r_{\gamma_{v^{1}}}\dots r_{\gamma_{v^{s_{v}}}}\Bigg\}

[formula]

while

R^{(k)}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{r},\bm{\mu})=\,\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{0,k}}\,\prod_{v\succeq 0}\Bigg\{\sum_{(\gamma_{v^{1}},\dots,\gamma_{v^{s_{v}}})\in{\cal P}^{s_{v}}}b_{s_{v}}(\gamma_{v};\gamma_{v^{1}},\ldots,\gamma_{v^{s_{v}}})\,\chi^{t}_{\gamma_{v^{1}}}\dots\chi^{t}_{\gamma_{v^{s_{v}}}}\Bigg\}

[formula]

where

\chi^{t}_{\gamma_{v}}=\begin{cases}r_{\gamma_{v}}&{\text{i}f}\,\,\,d_{t}(v)<k\\
\mu_{\gamma_{v}}&{\text{i}f}\,\,\,d_{t}(v)=k\end{cases}

[formula]

with, we recall,
d_{t}(v)
indicating the depth (distance from the root) of
v
in
t
.
In other words
R^{(k)}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{r},\bm{\mu})
has an expression similar to
\Phi^{(k)}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{r})
but
with the activities of the vertex of the
k
-th generation
weighted by
\bm{\mu}
. Here we agree that if
v
is such that
s_{v}=0
then
b_{0}(\gamma_{v})\equiv 1
.
Now, by (4.34) we have

[T^{\bm{r}}]^{k}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\mu})\;=\;\mu_{\gamma_{0}}

which implies immediately, via (4.35),

r_{\gamma_{0}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{k-1}\Phi^{(\ell)}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{r})\;\leq\;\mu_{\gamma_{0}}\;\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\mbox{for all $k\in\mathbb{N}$ }

[formula]

Equation (4.39) immediately implies the following proposition.

Proposition 4.1

Let
\bm{\mu}
be a function

\bm{\mu}\in(0,\infty)^{\cal P}
and, for any
\gamma\in{\cal P}
let
\varphi^{b}_{{\gamma}}(\bm{\mu})
be function defined in (4.29)
supposed to satisfy (4.32). Let
\bm{r}\in(0,\infty)^{\cal P}
be defined by (4.30).
Then, for all
\bm{\rho}\leq\bm{r}

i)

The series

\Phi^{b}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})\;:=\;\,\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{0}}\,\prod_{v\succeq 0}\Bigg\{\sum_{(\gamma_{v^{1}},\dots,\gamma_{v^{s_{v}}})\in{\cal P}^{s_{v}}}b_{s_{v}}(\gamma_{v};\gamma_{v^{1}},\ldots,\gamma_{v^{s_{v}}})\,\rho_{\gamma_{v^{1}}}\dots\rho_{\gamma_{v^{s_{v}}}}\Bigg\}

[formula]

converges for each
\gamma_{0}\in{\cal P}
and admits, for each
\gamma_{0}\in{\cal P}
, the bound

\Phi^{b}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})\leq\Phi^{b}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{r})\leq\varphi^{b}_{{\gamma_{0}}}(\bm{\mu})

[formula]

ii)

\rho_{\gamma_{0}}\Phi^{b}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})=\lim_{n\to\infty}[T^{\bm{\rho}}]^{n}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})

[formula]

and

\rho_{\gamma_{0}}\Phi^{b}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})

is solution of the equation (4.34), i.e. is fixed point of the map
T^{\bm{\rho}}
, i.e.

\rho_{\gamma_{0}}\Phi^{b}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})=T^{\bm{\rho}}_{\gamma_{0}}\Big(\rho_{\gamma}\Phi^{b}_{\gamma}(\bm{\rho})\Big)

[formula]

This proposition can be viewed as a generalization of the Lagrange inversion formula for series depending on infinite (countable) variables.

Proof
.

i). By (4.35) we get

r_{\gamma_{0}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}\Phi^{(\ell)}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{r})\,\,\leq\,\,[T^{\bm{r}}]_{\gamma_{0}}^{n+1}(\bm{\mu})\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\forall n\in\mathbb{N}

but, by definition (4.34) we have, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
that
[T^{\bm{r}}]^{k}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\mu})=\mu_{\gamma_{0}}
. So we obtain

r_{\gamma_{0}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}\Phi^{(\ell)}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{r})\;\leq\;\mu_{\gamma_{0}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\mbox{for all $n$}

i.e., by (4.30),

\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}\Phi^{(\ell)}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{r})\;\leq\;\varphi^{b}_{{\gamma_{0}}}(\bm{\mu})\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\mbox{for all $n$}

which implies

\Phi^{b}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{r})\leq\varphi^{b}_{{\gamma_{0}}}(\bm{\mu})

Therefore, by monotonicity, for any
\bm{\rho}\leq\bm{r}

\Phi^{b}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})\leq\Phi^{b}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{r})\leq\varphi^{b}_{{\gamma_{0}}}(\bm{\mu})

ii) By (4.35) we have that

[T^{\bm{\rho}}]_{\gamma_{0}}^{k}(\bm{\rho})\;=\;\rho_{\gamma_{0}}\Bigl[\sum_{\ell=0}^{k-1}\Phi^{(\ell)}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})+R^{(k)}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho},\bm{\mu})|_{\bm{\mu}=\bm{\rho}}\Bigr]

But, recalling definition (4.38)

R^{(k)}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho},\bm{\mu})|_{\bm{\mu}=\bm{\rho}}\;=

=\,\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{0,k}}\,\prod_{v\succeq 0}\Bigg\{\sum_{(\gamma_{v^{1}},\dots,\gamma_{v^{s_{v}}})\in{\cal P}^{s_{v}}}b_{s_{v}}(\gamma_{v};\gamma_{v^{1}},\ldots,\gamma_{v^{s_{v}}})\,\rho_{\gamma_{v^{1}}}\dots\rho_{\gamma_{v^{s_{v}}}}\Bigg\}\,\,=\,\,\Phi^{(k)}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})

So

[T^{\bm{\rho}}]^{k}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})\;=\;\rho_{\gamma_{0}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\Phi^{(\ell)}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})

Hence, for any
\bm{\rho}\leq\bm{r}

\lim_{k\to\infty}[T^{\bm{\rho}}]^{k}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})\,=\,\lim_{k\to\infty}\rho_{\gamma_{0}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\Phi^{(\ell)}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})\,=\,\rho_{\gamma_{0}}\Phi^{b}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})

Finally, for any
\bm{\rho}\leq\bm{r}

\displaystyle\rho_{\gamma_{0}}\Phi^{b}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}[T^{\bm{\rho}}]^{n+1}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})

\displaystyle=\,\lim_{n\to\infty}T^{\bm{\rho}}_{\gamma_{0}}\Bigl([T^{\bm{\rho}}]^{n}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\,\rho_{\gamma_{0}}\lim_{n\to\infty}\varphi^{b}_{{\gamma_{0}}}\Bigl([T^{\bm{\rho}}]^{n}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\,\rho_{\gamma_{0}}\varphi^{b}_{\gamma}\Bigl(\lim_{n\to\infty}[T^{\bm{\rho}}]^{n}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\,\rho_{\gamma_{0}}\varphi^{b}_{{\gamma_{0}}}\Bigl(\rho_{\gamma_{0}}\Phi^{b}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\,T^{\bm{\rho}}_{\gamma_{0}}\Bigl(\rho_{\gamma_{0}}\Phi^{b}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})\Bigr)

\Box

4.2.3
Convergence criteria

Fernández-Procacci criterion
.

Let us now choose

b_{n}(\gamma_{0};\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n})=b^{*}_{n}(\gamma_{0};\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n})\doteq{1\over n!}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{\{\gamma_{i}\not\sim\gamma_{0}\}}\;\;\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{\{\gamma_{i}\sim\gamma_{j}\}}

[formula]

and thus

\varphi^{b^{*}}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\mu})\;=\;1+\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{1}{n!}\,\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in{\cal P}^{n}\atop\gamma_{i}\not\sim\gamma_{0},\,\,\gamma_{i}\sim\gamma_{j}}{\mu_{\gamma_{1}}}\dots{\mu_{\gamma_{n}}}\;=\;\Xi_{{\cal P}_{\gamma_{0}}}(\bm{\mu})

[formula]

Then, proposition
4.1
tells us that the series

\Phi^{b^{*}}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})=

=\,\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{0}}\,\prod_{v\succeq 0}\Bigg\{\sum_{(\gamma_{v^{1}},\dots,\gamma_{v^{s_{v}}})\in{\cal P}^{s_{v}}}{1\over s_{v}!}\prod_{i=1}^{s_{v}}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{\{\gamma_{v^{i}}\not\sim\gamma_{v}\}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq s_{v}}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{\{\gamma_{v^{i}}\sim\gamma_{v^{j}}\}}\,\rho_{\gamma_{v^{1}}}\dots\rho_{\gamma_{v^{s_{v}}}}\Bigg\}

[formula]

converges as soon as
\bm{\rho}\leq\bm{r}^{*}
with

r^{*}_{\gamma}={\mu_{\gamma}\over\Xi_{{\cal P}_{\gamma}}(\bm{\mu})}

[formula]

comparing (4.26) with (4.46) we immediately
see that

\Pi^{*}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})=\Phi^{b^{*}}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})

So
we immediately get, by Proposition
4.1
,
the following criterion for the convergence of cluster expansions.

Theorem 4.1

Choose
\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{D}^{b^{*}}\subset(0,\infty)^{\cal P}
and let
\bm{r}^{*}\in(0,\infty)^{\cal P}
s.t.

r^{*}_{\gamma_{0}}={\mu_{\gamma_{0}}\over\Xi_{{\cal P}_{\gamma_{0}}}(\bm{\mu})}

Let
\bm{\rho}
such that

\rho_{\gamma}\;\leq\;r^{*}_{\gamma}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\forall\gamma\in{\cal P}

[formula]

Then the series
|\Pi|_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})
defined in (4.9)
is finite for each
\gamma_{0}\in{\cal P}
and

|\Pi|_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})\leq\Xi_{{\cal P}_{\gamma_{0}}}(\bm{\mu})

[formula]

and hence

\rho_{\gamma_{0}}|\Pi|_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})\leq\mu_{\gamma_{0}}

[formula]

for each
\gamma_{0}\in{\cal P}
.

Proof.
By proposition
4.1
we have immediately that the series
\Pi^{*}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})
defined in (4.17)
is finite for each
\gamma_{0}\in{\cal P}
and for all
\bm{\rho}
such that
\rho_{\gamma}\leq r^{*}_{\gamma}
where
r^{*}_{\gamma}
is defined in (4.48). Moreover

\Pi^{*}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})\leq\Xi_{{\cal P}_{\gamma_{0}}}(\bm{\mu})

for each
\gamma_{0}\in{\cal P}
. Now recalling (4.16) we obtain that the same is true also for the series
|\Pi|_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})
.
\Box

Dobrushin criterion
.

If we now choose

b_{n}(\gamma_{0};\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n})=b^{\rm Dob}_{n}(\gamma_{0};\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n})\doteq{1\over n!}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{\{\gamma_{i}\not\sim\gamma_{0}\}}\;\;\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{\{\gamma_{i}\neq\gamma_{j}\}}

and thus

\varphi^{b^{\rm Dob}}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\mu})\;=\;1+\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{1}{n!}\,\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in{\cal P}^{n}\atop\gamma_{0}\nsim\gamma_{i}\,,\,\gamma_{i}\neq\gamma_{j}}{\mu_{\gamma_{1}}}\dots{\mu_{\gamma_{n}}}\;=\;\prod_{\gamma\nsim\gamma_{0}}[1+\mu_{\gamma}]\;,

[formula]

Then again proposition
4.1
tells us that the series

\Phi^{b^{\rm Dob}}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})\;=

=\,\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{0}}\,\prod_{v\succeq 0}\Bigg\{\sum_{(\gamma_{v^{1}},\dots,\gamma_{v^{s_{v}}})\in{\cal P}^{s_{v}}}{1\over s_{v}!}\prod_{i=1}^{s_{v}}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{\{\gamma_{v^{i}}\not\sim\gamma_{v}\}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq s_{v}}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{\{\gamma_{v^{i}}\neq\gamma_{v^{j}}\}}\,\rho_{\gamma_{v^{1}}}\dots\rho_{\gamma_{v^{s_{v}}}}\Bigg\}

[formula]

converges as soon as
\bm{\rho}\leq\bm{r}^{\rm Dob}
with

r^{\rm Dob}_{\gamma}={\mu_{\gamma}\over\prod_{\gamma\nsim\gamma_{0}}[1+\mu_{\gamma}]}

[formula]

Comparing (4.27) with (4.52) we get

\Pi^{\rm Dob}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})=\Phi^{b^{\rm Dob}}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})

So
Proposition
4.1
also yields
the following (weaker) criterion for the convergence of cluster expansions.

Corollary 4.1
(Dobrushin)

Choose
\bm{\mu}\in{\mathcal{D}}^{b^{\rm Dob}}
and let
\bm{r}^{\rm Dob}\in(0,\infty)^{\cal P}
s.t.

r^{\rm Dob}_{\gamma}={\mu_{\gamma}\over\prod_{{\tilde{\gamma}}\nsim\gamma}[1+\mu_{\tilde{\gamma}}]}

[formula]

Let
\bm{\rho}\in[0,\infty)^{\cal P}
such that

\rho_{\gamma}\;\leq\;r^{\rm Dob}_{\gamma}\,={\mu_{\gamma}\over\prod_{{\tilde{\gamma}}\nsim\gamma}[1+\mu_{\tilde{\gamma}}]}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\forall\gamma\in{\cal P}

[formula]

Then the series
|\Pi|_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})
defined in (4.9)
is finite for each
\gamma\in{\cal P}
and

|\Pi|_{\gamma}(\bm{\rho})\leq\prod_{{\tilde{\gamma}}\nsim\gamma}[1+\mu_{\tilde{\gamma}}]

[formula]

or

\rho_{\gamma}|\Pi|_{\gamma}(\bm{\rho})\leq\mu_{\gamma}

[formula]

for each
\gamma\in{\cal P}
.

For the benefit of the readers we stress that
in the literature the Dobrushin condition is generally written in a different (but equivalent) form. In particular,
in the Dobrushin paper
[
11
]
and also in
[
31
]
the condition (4.55) is written as follows.

\rho_{\gamma}\;\leq\;r^{\rm Dob}_{\gamma}\;=(e^{{\tilde{\mu}}_{\gamma}}-1)e^{-\sum_{{\tilde{\gamma}}\in{\cal P}:\,{\tilde{\gamma}}\not\sim\gamma}\tilde{\mu}_{\tilde{\gamma}}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\forall\gamma\in{\cal P}

This is clearly the same condition (4.55) by defining

\tilde{\mu}_{\gamma}=\log[1+\mu_{\gamma}]

[formula]

Koptecký-Preiss criterion
.

Finally, if we now choose

b_{n}(\gamma_{0};\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n})=b^{\rm KP}_{n}(\gamma_{0};\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n})\doteq{1\over n!}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{\{\gamma_{i}\not\sim\gamma_{0}\}}

and

\varphi^{\rm KP}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\mu})\;=\;1+\sum_{n\geq 1}\,\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in{\cal P}^{n}\atop\gamma_{0}\nsim\gamma_{i}\,,\,1\leq i\leq n}{\mu_{\gamma_{1}}}\dots{\mu_{\gamma_{n}}}\;=\;\exp\Bigl[\sum_{\gamma\nsim\gamma_{0}}\mu_{\gamma}\Bigr]

[formula]

Then once again proposition
4.1
tells us that the series

\Phi^{b^{\rm KP}}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})\;=\;\,\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{0}}\,\prod_{v\succeq 0}\Bigg\{\sum_{(\gamma_{v^{1}},\dots,\gamma_{v^{s_{v}}})\in{\cal P}^{s_{v}}}{1\over s_{v}!}\prod_{i=1}^{s_{v}}\hbox to0.0pt{\mbox{\small\rm 1}\hss}\kern 1.49994pt1_{\gamma_{v^{i}}\not\sim\gamma_{v}}\;\;\rho_{\gamma_{v^{1}}}\dots\rho_{\gamma_{v^{s_{v}}}}\Bigg\}

[formula]

converges as soon as
\bm{\rho}\leq\bm{r}^{\rm KP}
with

r^{\rm KP}_{\gamma}={\mu_{\gamma}\over\exp\Bigl[\sum_{{\tilde{\gamma}}\nsim\gamma}\mu_{\tilde{\gamma}}\Bigr]}

[formula]

Comparing (4.28) with (4.60) we get

\Pi^{\rm KP}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})=\Phi^{b^{\rm KP}}_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})

So we get the criterion of Kotecký and Preiss. Namely,

Corollary 4.2
(Kotecký-Preiss)

Choose
\bm{\mu}\in{\mathcal{D}}^{b^{\rm KP}}
and let
\bm{r}^{\rm KP}\in(0,\infty)^{\cal P}
s.t.

r^{\rm KP}_{\gamma_{0}}={\mu_{\gamma_{0}}\over\exp\Bigl[\sum_{\gamma\nsim\gamma_{0}}\mu_{\gamma}\Bigr]}

[formula]

Let
\bm{\rho}\in[0,\infty)^{\cal P}
such that

\rho_{\gamma}\;\leq\;r^{\rm KP}_{\gamma}\;=\;{\mu_{\gamma}\over\exp\Bigl[\sum_{{\tilde{\gamma}}\nsim\gamma}\mu_{\tilde{\gamma}}\Bigr]},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\forall\gamma\in{\cal P}

[formula]

Then the series
|\Pi|_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})
defined in (4.9)
is finite for each
\gamma_{0}\in{\cal P}
and

|\Pi|_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})\leq\exp\Bigl[\sum_{\gamma\nsim\gamma_{0}}\mu_{\gamma}\Bigr]

[formula]

or

\rho_{\gamma_{0}}|\Pi|_{\gamma_{0}}(\bm{\rho})\leq\mu_{\gamma_{0}}

for each
\gamma_{0}\in{\cal P}
.

Remark
. Again for the benefit of the readers we stress that
in the literature the Kotecky-Preiss condition is generally written in a different (but equivalent) form. Namely in the original Kotecky-Preiss paper convergence is guaranteed by choosing
\bm{\rho}
such that
there is a function
a_{\gamma}
such that

\sum_{{\tilde{\gamma}}\nsim\gamma}\rho_{\tilde{\gamma}}e^{a_{\tilde{\gamma}}}\leq a_{\gamma}~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\forall\gamma\in{\cal P}

This is clearly the same condition (4.62) by setting

\mu_{\gamma}=\rho_{\gamma}e^{a_{\gamma}}

[formula]

Summing up, available convergence conditions are of the form

\rho_{\gamma}\,\;\leq\;r_{\gamma}=\,{\mu_{\gamma}\over\varphi_{\gamma}(\mu)}

[formula]

with

\varphi_{\gamma}(\bm{\mu})\;=\;\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\exp\Bigl[\sum_{{\tilde{\gamma}}\nsim\gamma}\mu_{\tilde{\gamma}}\Bigr]&\mbox{ (Koteck\'{y}-Preiss)}\\[10.0pt]
\prod_{{\tilde{\gamma}}\nsim\gamma}\bigl(1+\mu_{\tilde{\gamma}}\bigr)&\mbox{ (Dobrushin)}\\[10.0pt]
\Xi_{{\cal P}_{\gamma}}(\bm{\mu})&\mbox{ (Fern\'{a}ndez-Procacci)}\end{array}\right.

[formula]

Each condition is strictly weaker than the preceding one. Namely,
since, for fixed
\bm{\mu}\in(0,\infty)^{\cal P}
,

\Xi_{{\cal P}_{\gamma}}(\bm{\mu})\leq\prod_{{\tilde{\gamma}}\nsim\gamma}\bigl[1+\mu_{\tilde{\gamma}}\bigr]\leq\exp\Bigl[\sum_{\gamma\nsim\gamma_{0}}\mu_{\tilde{\gamma}}\Bigr]

we get

\bm{r}^{*}_{\gamma}\geq\bm{r}^{\rm Dob}_{\gamma}\geq\bm{r}_{\gamma}^{\rm KP}

So the criterion given by the Corollary
4.2
(i.e. Kotecky-Preiss condition) yields the worst estimate for convergence radius for the cluster expansion;
the Dobrishin Criterion of Corollary
4.1
gives an estimate which is better (i.e. larger) than
that given by the Kotecký-Preiss criterion for the same radius and finally the criterion
4.1
give the best estimate
for convergence radius for the cluster expansion
among the three proposed.

4.2.4

Elementary Examples

In this section we give some elementary in order to illustrate how the criterion (4.48) represents a sensible improvement
on previous criteria in applications.

Example 1. The Domino model on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

This model has also been considered by Dobrushin in
[
11
]
. The elements of the polymer space
{\cal P}

are in this case nearest neighbor bonds of the bidimensional
cubic lattice. For any
\gamma\in{\cal P}
we put
\rho_{\gamma}=\rho
, where
\rho>0
(all polymers have the same activity).
Two polymers are incompatible if and only if they have non empty intersection.
We can choose by symmetry that the function
\mu_{\gamma}
appearing in the Kotecky-Preiss, Dobrushin and Fernández-Procacci criteria are constant at the value
\mu
.

The Kotecky-Preiss criterion (4.63) for the domino model then reads as

\rho_{\gamma}\leq\mu_{\gamma}e^{-\sum_{\tilde{\gamma}\not\sim\gamma}\mu_{\tilde{\gamma}}}~~\Longleftrightarrow~~\rho\leq\mu e^{-7\mu}

which yelds at best

\rho\leq{1\over 7e}\approx 0.0525

On the other hand the Dobrushin condition (4.55) reads

\rho_{\gamma}\leq{\mu_{\gamma}\over\prod_{\tilde{\gamma}\not\sim\gamma}[1+\mu_{\tilde{\gamma}}]}~~\Longleftrightarrow~~\rho\leq{\mu\over(1+\mu)^{7}}

which yields at best

\rho\leq{{1\over 6}\over(1+{1\over 6})^{7}}\approx 0.0566

Finally, the condition (4.48) gives

\rho_{\gamma}\leq{\mu_{\gamma}\over\Xi_{{\cal P}_{\gamma}}(\bm{\mu})}~~\Longleftrightarrow~~\rho\leq{\mu\over 1+7\mu+9\mu^{2}}

which yields at best

\rho\leq{1\over 13}\approx 0.0769

Example 2. The lattice gas on a bounded degree graph
\mathbb{G}=(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{E})
with hard core
self repulsion and hard core pair interaction and the triangular lattice on then plane

Let
\mathbb{G}=(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{E})
be a bounded degree infinite graph with vertex set
\mathbb{V}
and edge
set
\mathbb{E}
, and maximum degree
\Delta
. A polymer system is obtained by choosing
{\cal P}=\mathbb{V}
and
by defininig the incompatibility relation
\nsim
by saying that two polymers
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
(i.e. two vertices
of
\mathbb{G}
) are incompatible if and only if either
\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}
or
\{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\}\in\mathbb{E}
. This polymer gas realization
is called the self repulsive hard core lattice gas on
\mathbb{G}
. In this case
the polymers are the vertices of
\mathbb{G}
and two polymers
\{x,y\}\subset\mathbb{V}
are incompatible if either

y=x
(self repulsion) or
\{x,y\}\in\mathbb{E}
(hard core pair interaction). In general, since polymers have no structure
(they are just vertices in a graph) one can suppose that the activity of a polymer
x\in{\cal P}
is a constant,
i.e.
\rho_{x}=\rho
for all
x\in\mathbb{V}
. Of course expect that the convergence radius depends
strongly on the topological structure of
\mathbb{G}
. We first consider the worst case i.e.
when the graph
\mathbb{G}
is such that the nearest neighbors of any vertex are pairwise compatible.
This happens e.g. if
\mathbb{G}
is a tree or if it is the cubic lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
The Kotecky-Preiss condition for this model then reads as

\rho_{x}\leq\mu_{x}e^{-\sum_{y\not\sim x}\mu_{y}}~~\Longleftrightarrow~~\rho\leq\mu e^{-(\Delta+1)\mu}

which yields at best

\rho\leq{1\over(\Delta+1)e}

[formula]

On the other hand the Dobrushin condition reads

\rho_{x}\leq{\mu_{x}\over\prod_{y\not\sim x}[1+\mu_{y}]}~~\Longleftrightarrow~~\rho\leq{\mu\over(1+\mu)^{\Delta+1}}

which yields at best

\rho\leq{{1\over\Delta}\over(1+{1\over\Delta})^{\Delta+1}}={\Delta^{\Delta}\over(\Delta+1)^{\Delta+1}}={1\over\Delta+1}{1\over(1+{1\over\Delta})^{\Delta}}

[formula]

Finally, the condition (4.48) gives

\rho_{x}\leq{\mu_{x}\over\Xi_{{\cal P}_{x}}(\bm{\mu})}~~\Longleftrightarrow~~\rho\leq{\mu\over 1+(\Delta+1)\mu+\sum_{k=2}^{\Delta}{\Delta\choose k}\mu^{k}}={\mu\over\mu+(1+\mu)^{\Delta}}

which yields at best

\rho\leq{{1\over\Delta-1}\over{1\over\Delta-1}+\left(1+{1\over\Delta-1}\right)^{\Delta}}={1\over 1+{\Delta^{\Delta}\over(\Delta-1)^{\Delta-1}}}={1\over\Delta(1+{1\over\Delta-1})^{\Delta-1}+1}

[formula]

To illustrate that Theorem
4.1
permits to improve this last bound (4.70) if we know more about the
topological structure of
\mathbb{G}
, we now consider a case of the triangular lattice in
d=2
(a regular graph with degree
\Delta=6
, where our bound turns to be
more efficient than the Dobrushin bound and the Shearer-Sokal bound (4.68). For the triangular lattice the tree bound (4.70)
gives

\varepsilon<{5^{5}\over 6^{6}}\approx 0,067

while our bound
gives

\varepsilon\leq{c\over\Xi_{\gamma}(c)}={c\over 1+7c+9c^{2}+2c^{3}}

The maximum occurs when
4c^{3}+9c^{2}-1=0
, which is somewhere between 1/3 and 3/10. For example choosing
c=1/3
(which is not the best choice) we obtain

\varepsilon\leq{c\over\Xi_{\gamma}(c)}={{1\over 3}\over 1+{7\over 3}+{1}+{2\over 27}}\approx 0,075

4.3

Gas of non overlapping finite subsets

In this section we will study a particular realization of the polymer gas
which appears in the most part of the examples in statistical mechanics.

We will suppose that it is given an infinite countable set
\mathbb{V}
, and we define the space of polymers as

{\cal P}_{\mathbb{V}}=\{R\subset\mathbb{V}:|R|<\infty\}

and the incompatibility relation in
{\cal P}_{\mathbb{V}}
is defined as

\gamma\not\sim{\tilde{\gamma}}\,\,\,\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\,\,\,\gamma\cap\tilde{\gamma}\neq\emptyset

Note that now polymers have
a cardinality, so that we can speak about big polymers and small polymers. Of course, as before, to a
polymer
\gamma
is associated an activity
\zeta(\gamma)
. We assume in general that
\zeta(\gamma)\in\mathbb{C}
as far as
\gamma\in{\cal P}_{\mathbb{V}}
and we set

|\zeta(\gamma)|=\rho(\gamma)

[formula]

Note that here we allow the value
\zeta(\gamma)=0
for some
\gamma
in order to stay more general. For example in the polymer
expansion of high temperature spin systems, the polymer space is always
{\cal P}_{\mathbb{V}}

for some suitable
\mathbb{V}
but it happens that
\zeta(\gamma)=0
whenever
|\gamma|=1
.

In most of the physics realizations
\mathbb{V}
is the vertex set of
an infinite
graph
\mathbb{G}=(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{E})
with edge set
\mathbb{E}
.
For example
\mathbb{V}=\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
\mathbb{E}
is the set of nearest neighbor in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. When
\mathbb{V}
is the vertex
set of a graph
\mathbb{G}
then
\mathbb{V}
has a natural metric structure induced by the graph distance in
\mathbb{G}
.
This metric structure on
\mathbb{V}
allow us to talk about how spread is a polymers (a polymer is spread if its points are
far apart) and we can say now if two polymers

\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
are close or far apart. So, from the abstract context
we can pass to more concrete realizations which have richer structures. Namely,
if one suppose that polymers are finite subsets of an underlying countable set
\mathbb{V}
with
\not\sim=\cap
,
the any polymer has an activity and a cardinality, so we an distinguish between big and small polymers.
If we further suppose that the underlying set
\mathbb{V}
is the vertex set of some graph
\mathbb{G}
we also can
talk about distance between polymers and spread polymers.
In any case, in the whole section below we will not suppose any graph structure for the set
\mathbb{V}
.
Our abstract polymer space is just the set of all finite subsets of a countable set with the incompatibility
relation being the the non void intersection.

Let now
\Lambda
be a finite set of
\mathbb{V}
. A configuration
of polymer gas in
\Lambda
is given once we specify the set of polymers which are present in
\Lambda
.
Of course this polymers must be pairwise compatible, i.e. a configuration in
\Lambda
is an unordered

n
-ple
\{\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\}
such that
\gamma_{i}\cap\gamma_{j}=\emptyset
for all
i,j=1,\dots,n
.
The ‘‘probability”
1
1

1

(4.79) is a real probability only if
\zeta(\gamma)\in[0,+\infty)
to see the configuration
\{\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\}
in the box
\Lambda
is defined as

{\rm Prob}_{\bm{\zeta}}(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})=\Xi_{\Lambda}^{-1}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\zeta(\gamma_{i})

[formula]

where
\Xi_{\Lambda}
is the partition function defined as

\Xi_{\Lambda}(\bm{\zeta})=1+\sum_{n\geq 1}\sum_{\{\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\}:~\gamma_{i}\subset\Lambda\atop\gamma_{i}\cap\gamma_{j}=\emptyset}\zeta(\gamma_{1})\dots\zeta(\gamma_{n})

[formula]

4.3.1

Convergence via the abstract polymer criteria

We compare the three conditions for this model. Starting with the Kotecky-Preiss condition, choosing

\mu(\gamma)=\rho(\gamma)e^{a|\gamma|}
(recall:
|\zeta(\gamma)|=\rho(\gamma)
), the condition (4.63) becomes the well known inequality

\sum_{{\tilde{\gamma}}\in{\cal P}_{\mathbb{V}}\atop\tilde{\gamma}\not\sim\gamma}\rho({\tilde{\gamma}})\;e^{a|{\tilde{\gamma}}|}\>\leq\>a|\gamma|,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\forall\gamma\in{\cal P}

[formula]

Now using that
\tilde{\gamma}\not\sim\gamma
means for the present model
{\tilde{\gamma}}\cap\gamma\neq\emptyset
we have that

\sum_{\tilde{\gamma}\not\sim\gamma}\rho({\tilde{\gamma}})e^{a|{\tilde{\gamma}}|}\leq|\gamma|\sup_{x\in\mathbb{V}}\sum_{\tilde{\gamma}\ni x}\rho({\tilde{\gamma}})e^{a|{\tilde{\gamma}}|}

Hence (4.74) becomes the well known condition

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{V}}\,\sum_{\gamma\in{\cal P}_{\mathbb{V}}\atop\gamma\ni x}\,\rho(\gamma)\,e^{a|\gamma|}\,\,\leq\,\,a

[formula]

On the other hand the Dobrushin condition (4.55) can be written as

\rho(\gamma)\leq{c(\gamma)\over\prod_{{\tilde{\gamma}}\in{\cal P}_{\mathbb{V}}:\,\tilde{\gamma}\not\sim\gamma}[1+c(\tilde{\gamma})]}

choosing again
c(\gamma)=|\rho(\gamma)|e^{a|\gamma|}
the condition above becomes

\prod_{{\tilde{\gamma}}\in{\cal P}_{\mathbb{V}}\atop\tilde{\gamma}\not\sim\gamma}(1+\rho({\tilde{\gamma}})e^{a|{\tilde{\gamma}}|})\leq e^{a|\gamma|},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\forall\gamma\in{\cal P}

i.e.

\sum_{{\tilde{\gamma}}\in{\cal P}_{\mathbb{V}}\atop\tilde{\gamma}\not\sim\gamma}\log(1+\rho({\tilde{\gamma}})e^{a|{\tilde{\gamma}}|})\leq a|\gamma|\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\forall\gamma\in{\cal P}

[formula]

i.e.

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{V}}\sum_{\gamma\in{\cal P}_{\mathbb{V}}\atop\gamma\ni x}\log(1+\rho(\gamma)e^{a|\gamma|})\leq a

[formula]

which is slightly better than (4.75).

Finally, the condition (4.48), putting again
c(\gamma)=\rho(\gamma)e^{a|\gamma|}
, becomes

\Xi_{\,{\cal P}_{\gamma}}(c)\leq e^{a|\gamma|}

[formula]

where
{\cal P}_{\gamma}=\{\gamma^{\prime}\in{\cal P}_{\mathbb{V}}:\gamma^{\prime}\cap\gamma\neq\emptyset\}
and

\Xi_{\,{\cal P}_{\gamma}}(c)=1+\sum_{n=1}^{|\gamma|}{1\over n!}\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in{\cal P}_{\mathbb{V}}^{n}\atop\gamma_{i}\not\sim\gamma,\gamma_{i}\sim\gamma_{j}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\rho(\gamma_{i})e^{a|\gamma_{i}|}

We again use the fact that
\gamma_{i}\not\sim\gamma_{j}\Longleftrightarrow\gamma_{i}\cap\gamma_{j}\neq\emptyset
and
\gamma_{i}\sim\gamma_{j}\Longleftrightarrow\gamma_{i}\cap\gamma_{j}=\emptyset

to estimate the factor

\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in{\cal P}_{\mathbb{V}}^{n}\atop\gamma_{i}\not\sim\gamma,\gamma_{i}\sim\gamma_{j}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\rho(\gamma_{i})e^{a|\gamma_{i}|}

Note that this factor is zero whenever
n>|\gamma|
, since there is no way to choose
n
subsets
\gamma_{i}
of
\mathbb{V}
such that they are all pairwise compatible
(i.e. non intersecting)
and all incompatible (i.e. intersecting) with a fixed subset
\gamma
of
\mathbb{V}
with a number of elements equal to
|\gamma|
.
On the other hand, when the sum above is not zero, i.e. for
n\leq|\gamma|
, it can be bounded at least by (a very rough bound)

\displaystyle\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in{\cal P}^{n}\atop\gamma_{i}\not\sim\gamma,\gamma_{i}\sim\gamma_{j}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\rho(\gamma_{i})e^{a|\gamma_{i}|}

\displaystyle\leq|\gamma|(|\gamma|-1)\cdots(|\gamma|-n+1)\Bigg[\sup_{x\in\mathbb{V}}\,\sum_{\gamma\in{\cal P}_{\mathbb{V}}\atop x\in\gamma}{\rho(\gamma)}e^{a|\gamma|}\Bigg]^{n}

\displaystyle=~{|\gamma|\choose n}n!\Bigg[\sup_{x\in\mathbb{V}}\,\sum_{\gamma\in{\cal P}_{\mathbb{V}}\atop x\in\gamma}{\rho(\gamma)}e^{a|\gamma|}\Bigg]^{n}

Thus

\Xi^{\,\gamma}_{\,{\cal P}_{\mathbb{V}}}(c)\leq 1+\sum_{n=1}^{|\gamma|}{|\gamma|\choose n}\Bigg[\sup_{x\in\mathbb{V}}\,\sum_{\gamma\in{\cal P}_{\mathbb{V}}\atop x\in\gamma}{\rho(\gamma)}e^{a|\gamma|}\Bigg]^{n}=\Bigg[1+\sup_{x\in\mathbb{V}}\,\sum_{\gamma\in{\cal P}_{\mathbb{V}}\atop x\in\gamma}{\rho(\gamma)}e^{a|\gamma|}\Bigg]^{|\gamma|}

[formula]

Thus (4.78) can be written as

\Bigg[1+\sup_{x\in\mathbb{V}}\,\sum_{\gamma\in{\cal P}_{\mathbb{V}}\atop x\in\gamma}{\rho(\gamma)}e^{a|\gamma|}\Bigg]^{|\gamma|}\leq e^{a|\gamma|}

i.e.

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{V}}\,\sum_{\gamma\in{\cal P}_{\mathbb{V}}\atop x\in\gamma}{\rho(\gamma)}e^{a|\gamma|}\leq e^{a}-1

[formula]

Note that by (4.49) we also get the upper bound

\Pi_{\gamma}(\rho)\leq e^{a|\gamma|}

We have therefore proved the following theorem

Theorem 4.2

Let
\mathbb{V}
be a countable set and let
{\cal P}_{\mathbb{V}}=\{\gamma\subset\mathbb{V}:|\gamma|<\infty\}
be the polymer set with incompatibility relation:

\gamma\not\sim\gamma^{\prime}\,\,\Leftrightarrow\,\,\gamma\cap\gamma^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
and (complex) activity
\zeta(\gamma)
.
Assume that there is a positive number
a>0
, such that, for all
x\in\mathbb{V}

\sum\limits_{\gamma\in{\cal P}_{\mathbb{V}}\atop x\in\gamma}|\zeta(\gamma)|~e^{a|\gamma|}\leq e^{a}-1

[formula]

then, for all finite
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{V}
, we have that
{1\over|\Lambda|}\ln\Xi_{\Lambda}(\bm{\zeta})
, where
\Xi_{\Lambda}(\bm{\zeta})
is the partition function define in (4.73), can be written as an absolutely convergent series uniformly in
\Lambda
.

4.3.2

The subset gas: proof by Induction

We now illustrate the reasoning by induction to prove convergence of the cluster expansion
for the subset gas in the space
{\cal P}_{\mathbb{V}}
in the special case in which the activity is non negative (i.e.
\rho(\gamma)\geq 0
for all
\gamma\in{\cal P}_{\mathbb{V}}
. We use the Miracle-sole
approach.

Theorem 4.3

Let
\mathbb{V}
be a countable set and let
{\cal P}_{\mathbb{V}}=\{\gamma\subset\mathbb{V}:|\gamma|<\infty\}
be the polymer set with incompatibility relation:

\gamma\not\sim\gamma^{\prime}\,\,\Leftrightarrow\,\,\gamma\cap\gamma^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
and activity
\rho(\gamma)\geq 0
.
Assume that there is a positive number
a>0
, such that, for all
x\in\mathbb{V}

\sum\limits_{\gamma\in{\cal P}_{\mathbb{V}}\atop x\in\gamma}\rho(\gamma)~e^{a|\gamma|}\leq e^{a}-1

[formula]

(here of course
\rho\geq 0
)
then, for all
\Lambda\in\mathbb{V}
, we have

|\Sigma^{\Lambda}_{x}|(\rho)\leq a

[formula]

where
x\in\Lambda
and

|\Sigma^{\Lambda}_{x}|(\rho)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{1\over n!}\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in\Lambda^{n}\atop\exists i:~x\in\gamma_{i}}|\phi^{T}(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})|\;{\rho(\gamma_{1})}\dots{\rho(\gamma_{n})}

Proof
. First observe that, by the alternate sign property of
\phi^{T}(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})
we have
that

|\Sigma^{\Lambda}_{x}|(\rho)=-\log\Xi_{\Lambda}(-\rho)+\log\Xi_{\Lambda\backslash\{x\}}(-\rho)

[formula]

We will perform the proof by induction on
\Lambda
. We assume that, under the condition (4.82), the inequality
(4.83) is satisfied for a given
\Lambda
and any of its subsets. Take now
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\backslash\Lambda
. We want to bound

|\Sigma^{\Lambda\cup\{x\}}_{x}|(\rho)=-\log\Xi_{\Lambda\cup\{x\}}(-\rho)+\log\Xi_{\Lambda}(-\rho)=-\log{\Xi_{\Lambda\cup\{x\}}(-\rho)\over\Xi_{\Lambda}(-\rho)}

But now we know that

\Xi_{\Lambda\cup\{x\}}(-\rho)=\Xi_{\Lambda}(-\rho)-\sum_{S\subset\Lambda}\rho(\{x\}\cup S)\Xi_{\Lambda\backslash S}(-\rho)

So

{\Xi_{\Lambda\cup\{x\}}(-\rho)\over\Xi_{\Lambda}(-\rho)}=1-\sum_{S\subset\Lambda}\rho(\{x\}\cup S){\Xi_{\Lambda\backslash S}(-\rho)\over\Xi_{\Lambda}(-\rho)}

Now, let
S=\{y_{1},\dots,y_{k}\}
with of course
k=|S|
, then

{\Xi_{\Lambda\backslash S}(-\rho)\over\Xi_{\Lambda}(-\rho)}={\Xi_{\Lambda\backslash\{y_{1}\}}(-\rho)\over\Xi_{\Lambda}(-\rho)}{\Xi_{\Lambda\backslash\{y_{1},y_{2}\}}(-\rho)\over\Xi_{\Lambda\backslash\{y_{1}\}}(-\rho)}\cdots{\Xi_{\Lambda\backslash\{y_{1},\dots,y_{k}\}}(-\rho)\over\Xi_{\Lambda\backslash\{y_{1},\dots,y_{k-1}\}}(-\rho)}

The r.h.s. of equation above is a product of
k=|S|
terms of the form

{\Xi_{\tilde{\Lambda}\backslash\{z\}}(-\rho)\over\Xi_{\tilde{\Lambda}}(-\rho)}

with
\tilde{\Lambda}\subset\Lambda
and
z\in\tilde{\Lambda}
. So by the induction hypothesis for each of these terms
we have

\log{\Xi_{\tilde{\Lambda}\backslash\{z\}}(-\rho)\over\Xi_{\tilde{\Lambda}}(-\rho)}=\log\Xi_{\tilde{\Lambda}\backslash\{z\}}(-\rho)-\log\Xi_{\tilde{\Lambda}}(-\rho)=

=-\log\Xi_{\tilde{\Lambda}}(-\rho)+\log\Xi_{\tilde{\Lambda}\backslash\{z\}}(-\rho)=|\Sigma^{\tilde{\Lambda}}_{z}|(\rho)\leq a

So, adopting the convention
\Lambda\backslash\{y_{1},\dots,y_{i-1}\}=\Lambda
for
i=1
,

\log{\Xi_{\Lambda\backslash S}(-\rho)\over\Xi_{\Lambda}(-\rho)}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\log{\Xi_{\Lambda\backslash\{y_{1},\dots,y_{i}\}}(-\rho)\over\Xi_{\Lambda\backslash\{y_{1},\dots,y_{i-1}\}}(-\rho)}\leq k\,a=|S|\,a

and hence

{\Xi_{\Lambda\backslash S}(-\rho)\over\Xi_{\Lambda}(-\rho)}\leq e^{a|S|}

Thus

|\Sigma^{\Lambda\cup\{x\}}_{x}|(\rho)=-\log\Xi_{\Lambda\cup\{x\}}(-\rho)+\log\Xi_{\Lambda}(-\rho)=-\log{\Xi_{\Lambda\cup\{x\}}(-\rho)\over\Xi_{\Lambda}(-\rho)}=

-\log\Bigg[1-\sum_{S\subset\Lambda}\rho(\{x\}\cup S){\Xi_{\Lambda\backslash S}(-\rho)\over\Xi_{\Lambda}(-\rho)}\Bigg]\leq-\log\Bigg[1-\sum_{S\subset\Lambda}\rho(\{x\}\cup S)e^{a|S|}\Bigg]=

=-\log\Bigg[1-\sum_{S\subset\Lambda}\rho(\{x\}\cup S)e^{a|S|}\Bigg]=-\log\Bigg[1-{1\over e^{a}}\sum_{S\subset\Lambda}\rho(\{x\}\cup S)e^{a(|S|+1)}\Bigg]

\leq-\log\Bigg[1-{1\over e^{a}}(e^{a}-1)\Bigg]=a

To complete the induction we have to show that the claim is true when
\Lambda
contains just one point,
i.e.
\Lambda=\{x\}
. If
\Lambda=\{x\}
then we have only to check the function

\displaystyle|\Sigma^{\{x\}}_{x}|(\rho)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{1\over n!}\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in(\{x\})^{n}\atop\exists i:~x\in\gamma_{i}}|\phi^{T}(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})|\;{\rho(\gamma_{1})}\dots{\rho(\gamma_{n})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{1\over n!}|\phi^{T}(\{x\},\dots,\{x\})|\;[{\rho(\{x\})}]^{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{1\over n}\;[{\rho(\{x\})}]^{n}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{1\over n}\;\Big[1-{1\over e^{a}}\Big]^{n}

\displaystyle=-\log\Big[1-\Big(1-{1\over e^{a}}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=a

where in the third line we have used that
|\phi^{T}(\{x\},\dots,\{x\})|=\,(n-1)!
and
in the fourth line we used that, by (4.82),
\rho(\{x\})\,\,\,\leq 1-{1\over e^{a}}
. So
the induction is completed.
\Box

Chapter 5

Two systems in the cubic lattice

To show the utility of the polymer expansion in the study os discrete systems, we will consider in this chapter two systems on the
d
-dimensional cubic lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. We agree that
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is equipped with the usual “graph distance” metric
|\cdot|
. Namely, given two
points
x
and
y
in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, then
|x-y|
denotes the graph distance between
x
and
y
(i.e. the number of nearest neighbor edges of the shortest path between
x
and
y
).

5.1

The Self repuslive Lattice gas

The self repulsive lattice gas in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is a system formed by “particles” which may or may not occupy the vertices
x

of the unit cubic lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. In other words we associate to any site
x
of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
a variable
n_{x}
taking values in the set
\{0,1\}
. When
n_{x}=1
a particle is occupying the site
x
, and when
n_{x}=0
, the site
x
is empty. The name self-repulsive is because each site can be occupied by at most one particle. Let us then suppose that each particle occupying the site
x
has an activity

\lambda_{x}
assumed to be constant, i.e.
\lambda_{x}=\lambda
for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. Finally, let us suppose that particles in the self repulsive lattice gas interact via a pair potential
V(x,y)
which we assume to be symmetric
V(x,y)=V(y,x)
(and, of course, self repulsive, i.e. only one particle can occupy a site
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
). Namely:

V(x,x)=+\infty\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\ \mbox{for all $x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}$}

[formula]

We will also assume that the pair potential
is summable (regular) in the following sense

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\atop y\neq x}|V(x,y)|=J<\infty

[formula]

Note that the assumptions (5.2) and (5.2) automatically guarantee that the pair potential
is stable with stability constant not greater that
J/2
. Indeed
for any
n\in{}\mathbb{N}
and
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in(\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{n}
,

\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}V(x_{i},x_{j})\geq-{1\over 2}\sum_{(i,j)\in[n]^{2}\atop i\neq j}|V(x_{i},x_{j})|=-{1\over 2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{i\in[n]\atop j\neq 1}|V(x_{i},x_{j})|\geq-{J\over 2}n,

[formula]

The Grand Canonical partition function of such a lattice gas is

Z_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)=~\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{n}\over n!}\sum_{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\Lambda^{n}}~e^{-\beta\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}V(x_{i},x_{j})}

The specific case in which

V_{\rm h.c.}(x,y)=\begin{cases}+\infty&{\rm if}~|x-y|\leq 1\\
0&{\rm otherwise}\end{cases}

belongs to the class of pair potentials satisfying assumptions (5.2) and (5.3).
so by the discussion done in Section
4.2.4
, if
V(x_{i},x_{j})=V_{\rm h.c.}(x_{i},x_{j})
then the finite volume pressure

p_{\Lambda}(\lambda)={1\over|\Lambda|}\ln Z_{\Lambda}(\lambda)
(now independent from
\beta
) converge absolutely and it is bounded by
(2d-1)^{-1}
uniformly in
\Lambda

as soon as

\lambda\leq\lambda_{c}\equiv{1\over 1+{(2d)^{2d}\over(2d-1)^{2d-1}}}

[formula]

In other words,
p_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
is analytic in the disk
|\lambda|\leq\lambda_{c}
.

5.1.1

Covergence by direct Mayer expansion

As far as the general case is concerned (i.e.
V(x,y)
regular and stable pair potential
\beta
), we can obtain the convergent condition for the high tempretaure/low activity phase of such lattice gas by directly perform a Mayer expansion of the
grand canonical partition function similarly to what we did in Secrion
3.1.2
. Let us sketch how.

The Grand Canonical partition function is
