Z_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)=~\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{n}\over n!}\sum_{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\Lambda^{n}}~e^{-\beta\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}V(x_{i},x_{j})}

where of course we are assuming in that

V(x,x)=+\infty
. By Mayer expansion on the factor

e^{-\beta\sum_{i<j}V(x_{i},x_{j})}
we find, as usual

\ln Z_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)~=~\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\lambda^{n}\over n!}\sum_{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\Lambda^{n}}~\sum_{g\in{G}_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}(e^{-\beta V(x_{i},x_{j})}-1)

Then, by Theorem
3.8
and tree graph inequality (3.70), we have, using stability also condition (5.3),

|\sum_{g\in{G}_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}(e^{-\beta V(x_{i},x_{j})}-1)|\leq e^{{\beta J\over 2}n}\sum_{\tau\in{T}_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}(1-e^{-\beta|V(x_{i},x_{j})|})

hence

|\ln Z_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)|\leq\sum_{n~=~1}^{\infty}{|\lambda|^{n}\over n!}\sum_{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\Lambda^{n}}~e^{{\beta J\over 2}n}\sum_{\tau\in{T}_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}(1-e^{-\beta|V(x_{i},x_{j})|})\leq

\leq\sum_{n~=~1}^{\infty}{|\lambda|^{n}\over n!}~e^{{\beta J\over 2}n}\sum_{\tau\in{T}_{n}}\sum_{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\Lambda^{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}(1-e^{-\beta|V(x_{i},x_{j})|})

but similarly to what we saw in Proposition
3.1
, formula (3.17) it is not difficult to show that

\sum_{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\Lambda^{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}(1-e^{-\beta|V(x_{i},x_{j})|})\leq|\Lambda|\left[\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}(1-e^{-\beta|V(x,y)|})\right]^{n-1}

note that the sum over
y
after the sup includes also
y=x
where
V(x,x)~=~\infty
.
Observing that

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\atop y\neq x}(1-e^{-\beta|V(x,y)|})\leq\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\atop y\neq x}\beta|V(x,y)|\leq\beta J

we get

\sum_{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\Lambda^{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}(1-e^{-\beta|V(x_{i},x_{j})|})\leq|\Lambda|\left[1+\beta J\right]^{n-1}

and therefore

\displaystyle|\ln Z_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{|\lambda|^{n}\over n!}~e^{{\beta J\over 2}n}|\Lambda|[1+\beta J]^{n-1}n^{n-2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{|\lambda|^{n}\over n^{2}}~e^{{\beta J\over 2}n}|\Lambda|[1+\beta J]^{n-1}{n^{n}\over n!}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{|\lambda|^{n}\over n^{2}}~e^{{\beta J\over 2}n}|\Lambda|[1+\beta J]^{n-1}e^{n}

thus in this case the condition for the convergence is

|\lambda|e^{{\beta J\over 2}+1}[1+\beta J]<1

[formula]

This condition is quite unsatisfactory since it says that, for any temperature, even very high (i.e even for
\beta
very small),
one needs to set the activity smaller than
1/e
to ensure convergence.

5.1.2

High temperature polymer expansion of the lattice gas

We now obtain the convergence condition for the same
\ln Z_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
performing a first example of high temperature polymer expansion. We will get a much satisfactory bound and this will illustrate
quite well how convenient can be to perform polymer expansion in discrete systems when
possible!

The
Grand canonical partition function of the same lattice gas enclosed
in
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, with activity
\lambda
, inverse temperature
\beta
,
interacting via a pair potential
V(x,y)
(
x
and
y
sites in

\Lambda
) such that
V(x,x)~=~\infty
(a site
x
can be occupied at most by one particle) can be written as follows

\displaystyle Z_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)

\displaystyle=~\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{n}\over n!}\sum_{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\Lambda^{n}}~e^{-\beta\sum_{i<j}V(x_{i},x_{j})}

\displaystyle=~\sum_{n=0}^{|\Lambda|}{\lambda^{n}\over n!}\sum_{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\Lambda^{n}\atop x_{i}\neq x_{j}}~e^{-\beta\sum_{i<j}V(x_{i},x_{j})}

\displaystyle=~\sum_{n=0}^{|\Lambda|}\lambda^{n}\sum_{\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset\Lambda}~e^{-\beta\sum_{i<j}V(x_{i},x_{j})}

\displaystyle=~\sum_{S\subset\Lambda}\lambda^{|S|}~e^{-\beta\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset S}V(x,y)}

Define then for
x\in\Lambda
the variable
n_{x}
taking values in the set
\{0,1\}
(
n_{x}
can be interpreted as the occupation number of the site
x
:
n_{x}=0
means that
the site is empty and
n_{x}=1
means that the site is occupied).

We can rewrite the partition function above by considering that to each
S\subset\Lambda
we can associate a unique function
\bm{n}_{\Lambda}:\Lambda\to\{0,1\}:x\mapsto n_{x}
such that
\bm{n}^{-1}(1)=S
(we remind that
\bm{n}_{\Lambda}
is usually called a configuration of the lattice gas in
\Lambda
and it specifies which sites are occupied by a particle and which sites are empty). We let
N_{\Lambda}
be the set of all such functions. Clearly, if
|N_{\Lambda}|
denotes the cardinality of
N_{\Lambda}
and

|\Lambda|
is the number of sites in
\Lambda
, then
|N_{\Lambda}|=2^{|\Lambda|}
. Moreover, in general, if
R\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we denote by

\bm{n}_{R}
a possible configuration in
R
and by
N_{R}
the set
of all possible configurations
n_{R}
.

As remarked earlier, there is a one to one correspondence between
\bm{n}_{\Lambda}\in N_{\Lambda}
and
S\subset\Lambda
by defining
S(n_{\Lambda})=\{x\in\Lambda:n_{x}=1\}
.
Therefore we can write

\displaystyle Z_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)

\displaystyle=~\sum_{S\subset\Lambda}\,\lambda^{|S|}~e^{-\beta\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset S}V(x,y)}

[formula]

\displaystyle=~\sum_{\bm{n}_{\Lambda}\in N_{\Lambda}}\lambda^{\sum_{x\in\Lambda}n_{x}}e^{-\beta\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}n_{x}n_{y}V(x,y)}

Expand now the exponential in (5.6)

e^{-\beta\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}n_{x}n_{y}V(x,y)}~=~\prod_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}[e^{-\beta n_{x}n_{y}V(x,y)}-1+1]~=~

~=~\sum_{s=1}^{|\Lambda|}\sum_{\{R_{1},\dots,R_{s}\}\in\pi(\Lambda)}\rho(R_{1})\cdots\rho(R_{r})

where
\pi(\Lambda)~=~
set of all partitions of
\Lambda
(so that
\{R_{1},\dots,R_{s}\}
is a partition of
\Lambda
), and

\rho(R)~=~\begin{cases}1&{\text{i}f}|R|~=~1\\
\displaystyle{\sum\limits_{g\in G_{R}}\prod\limits_{\{x,y\}\in E_{g}}[e^{-\beta n_{x}n_{y}V(x,y)}-1]}&{\text{i}f}~|R|\geq 2\end{cases}

where
G_{R}
is the set of connected graph with vertex set
R
.

Thus (5.6) can be written as,

\displaystyle Z_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)

\displaystyle=~\sum_{\bm{n}_{\Lambda}\in N_{\Lambda}}\lambda^{\sum_{x\in\Lambda}n_{x}}\sum_{s=1}^{|\Lambda|}\sum_{R_{1},\dots,R_{s}\in\pi(\Lambda)}\rho(R_{1})\cdots\rho(R_{s})

\displaystyle=~\sum_{s=1}^{|\Lambda|}\sum_{R_{1},\dots,R_{s}\in\pi(\Lambda)}\sum_{\bm{n}_{R_{1}}\in N_{R_{1}}}\rho(R_{1})\lambda^{\sum\limits_{x\in R_{1}}n_{x}}\cdots\!\!\!\sum_{\bm{n}_{R_{s}}\in N_{R_{s}}}\!\!\!\rho(R_{s})\lambda^{\sum\limits_{x\in R_{s}}n_{x}}

Define now

{\tilde{\rho}}(R)\,~=~\,\sum_{n_{R}\in N_{R}}\;\rho(R)\;\lambda^{\sum_{x\in R}\;n_{x}}

Now observe that, for any
g\in G_{R}
, the factor

\prod_{\{x,y\}\in E_{g}}[e^{-\beta n_{x}n_{y}V(x,y)}-1]\neq 0

is different from zero only for the configuration
\bm{n}_{R}
such that
n_{x}=1
for all
x\in R
. Therefore

{\tilde{\rho}}(R)~=~\begin{cases}1+\lambda&{\rm if}~|R|=1\\
\lambda^{|R|}\sum\limits_{g\in G_{R}}\prod\limits_{\{x,y\}\in E_{g}}[e^{-\beta V(x,y)}-1]&{\rm if}~|R|\geq 2\end{cases}

Defining now

\zeta(R)~=~\begin{cases}1&{\text{i}f}~|R|~=~1\\
{\lambda^{|R|}\over(1+\lambda)^{|R|}}\sum\limits_{g\in G_{R}}\prod\limits_{\{x,y\}\in E_{g}}[e^{-\beta V(x,y)}-1]&{\text{i}f}~|R|\geq 2\end{cases}

[formula]

we obtain

\displaystyle Z_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1+\lambda)^{|\Lambda|}\sum_{s=1}^{|\Lambda|}\sum_{R_{1},\dots,R_{s}\in\pi(\Lambda)}\zeta(R_{1})\cdots\zeta(R_{s})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1+\lambda)^{|\Lambda|}\sum_{s\geq 0}\sum_{\{R_{1},\dots,R_{s}\}\subset\Lambda\atop|R_{i}|\geq 2,~R_{i}\cap R_{j}=\emptyset}\zeta(R_{1})\cdots\zeta(R_{s})

where the term
s=0
in the last sum is equal to 1 and corresponds to the partition of
\Lambda
in
|\Lambda|
subsets each of cardinality 1. Clearly

\displaystyle\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)

\displaystyle\doteq\sum_{s\geq 0}\sum_{\{R_{1},\dots,R_{s}\}\subset\Lambda\atop|R_{i}|\geq 2,~R_{i}\cap R_{j}=\emptyset}\zeta(R_{1})\cdots\zeta(R_{s})

\displaystyle=~1+\sum_{n\geq 1}{1\over n!}\sum_{R_{1},\dots,R_{n}\atop|R_{i}|\geq 2,\,R_{i}\cap R_{j}~=~\emptyset}\zeta(R_{1})\cdots\zeta(R_{n})

I.e.
\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
is the grand canonical partition function of a polymer gas in which polymers are
finite subsets of
R\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
such that
|R|\geq 2
and with activity
\zeta(R)
.

We finally have

Z_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)=(1+\lambda)^{|\Lambda|}\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)

and hence

\ln Z_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)=|\Lambda|\ln(1+\lambda)+\ln\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)

Hence the pressure of the lattice gas
{1\over|\Lambda|}\log Z_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
converges absolutely uniformly in
\Lambda
if
{1\over|\Lambda|}\log\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)
does.

I.e., by (4.80) conditions if,

\sum_{n\geq 2}e^{an}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{R\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\atop x\in R,~|R|=n}|\zeta(R)|\leq e^{a}-1

[formula]

or a little bit “roughly” (i.e choosing
a=\ln 2
which is not an optimal choice)

\sum_{n\geq 2}2^{n}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{R\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\atop x\in R,~|R|=n}|\zeta(R)|~\leq~1

[formula]

Recalling definition (5.7) of the activity
\zeta(R)

and setting
{\tilde{\lambda}}~=~{\lambda\over(1+\lambda)}
we have

\sup_{x\in Z^{d}}\sum_{R\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\atop x\in R,~|R|=n}|\zeta(R)|~=~{|{\tilde{\lambda}}|}^{n}\sup_{x\in Z^{d}}\sum_{R\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\atop x\in R,~|R|=n}\bigg|\sum_{g\in G_{R}}\prod_{\{x,y\}\in E_{g}}[e^{-\beta V(x,y)}-1]\bigg|

Now note that

\sum_{R\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\atop x\in R,~|R|=n}~=~{1\over(n-1)!}\sum_{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{dn}\atop x_{1}=x,\;x_{i}\neq x_{j}\ {\rm for}\ i\neq j}

Hence

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{R\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\atop x\in R,~|R|=n}|\zeta(R)|~=

={{|{\tilde{\lambda}}|}^{n}\over(n-1)!}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{dn}\atop x_{1}=x,\;x_{i}\neq x_{j}\ {\rm for}\ i\neq j}\bigg|\sum_{g\in G_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}[e^{-\beta V(x_{i},x_{j})}-1]\bigg|

[formula]

Once again, by Theorem
3.8
, tree graph inequality (3.69) and using stability condition
(5.3) we have
the estimate

\bigg|\sum_{g\in G_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}[e^{-\beta V(x_{i},x_{j})}-1]\bigg|\leq e^{{\beta J\over 2}n}\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}(1-e^{-\beta|V(x_{i},x_{j})|})

Now observe that, for any
\tau\in T_{n}

\displaystyle\sum_{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{dn}\atop x_{1}=x,\;x_{i}\neq x_{j}\ {\rm for}\ i\neq j}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}(1-e^{-\beta|V(x_{i},x_{j})|})

\displaystyle\leq~\Big[\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\atop y\neq x}\beta|V(x,y)|\Big]^{n-1}

\displaystyle=~[\beta J]^{n-1}

Therefore, recalling also Cayley formula (3.6), we get

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in Z^{d}}\sum_{R\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\atop x\in R,~|R|=n}|\zeta(R)|

\displaystyle\leq~{n^{n-2}\over(n-1)!}[\beta J]^{n-1}(|{\tilde{\lambda}}|e^{\beta J\over 2})^{n}

\displaystyle\leq~|{\tilde{\lambda}}|e^{\beta J\over 2}\Big[|{\tilde{\lambda}}|e^{{\beta J\over 2}+1}\,\beta J\Big]^{n-1}

Hence the condition for convergence (5.8) is satisfied if

2|{\tilde{\lambda}}|e^{\beta J\over 2}\sum_{n\geq 2}\Big[2|{\tilde{\lambda}}|e^{{\beta J\over 2}+1}\,\beta J\Big]^{n-1}~\leq~1

[formula]

which, after some calculus, is satisfied if

|{\tilde{\lambda}}|e^{{\beta J\over 2}+1}\leq{1\over\beta J}{1\over 1+\sqrt{1+{4\over e\beta J}}}

[formula]

This condition is much better than (5.5).
Indeed, denoting by
\beta_{c}

the unique solution of the equation

e^{{\beta J\over 2}+1}~=~{1\over\beta J}{1\over 1+\sqrt{1+{4\over e\beta J}}}

and recalling that
{\tilde{\lambda}}=\lambda/(1+\lambda)
,
we get that, as soon as
\beta\leq\beta_{c}
,
the pressure
of the lattice gas is an absolute convergent expansion for all

\lambda
real. The temperature
\beta_{c}
is a first example of critical temperature: below
\beta_{c}
the lattice gas is in a pure phase for any activity
\lambda>0
.

5.2

The BEG model at low temperature

As an example, we consider the Blume-Emery-Griffiths (BEG) model
in the low temperature disordered
phase. The model is defined on the cubic unit lattice in

d
-dimensions
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
by supposing that in each vertex
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}

there is a spin variable
\sigma_{x}
taking values in the set

\{0,-1,+1\}
. Given a volume
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
(typically, a cube centered at the origin), a spin configuration in
\Lambda

is a function
\bm{\sigma}:\Lambda\to\{0,-1,+1\}:x\mapsto\sigma_{x}
, We denote by
\Sigma_{\Lambda}
the set of all spin configurations in
\Lambda
(note that
|\Sigma_{\Lambda}|=3^{|\Lambda|}
). Given

\bm{\sigma}\in\Sigma_{\Lambda}
, its energy
H_{\Lambda}(\bm{\sigma})
is given by the Hamiltonian

H_{\Lambda}(\bm{\sigma})=-\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda\atop|x-y|=1}[\sigma_{x}\sigma_{y}+{\rm Y}\sigma^{2}_{x}\sigma^{2}_{y}]+{\rm X}\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\sigma_{x}^{2}

[formula]

where in general
{\rm X}
and
{\rm Y}
are real parameters. In other words spins interacts via a nearest neighbor pair potential.
We will further suppose that

{\rm X}>d(1+|{\rm Y}|)

[formula]

With this hypothesis it is not difficult to check that the minimum of
H_{\Lambda}(\bm{\sigma})
is reached at the configuration
\bm{\sigma}
such that
\sigma_{x}=0
for all
\xi\in\Lambda
. This
region is called the disordered phase. Via the Hamiltonian (5.13) we can assign a probability
Prob(\bm{\sigma})
to each configuration
\bm{\sigma}
in
\Lambda
. Namely,

Prob(\bm{\sigma})={e^{-\beta H_{\Lambda}(\bm{\sigma})}\over Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)}

where
\beta>0
is the inverse temperature and

Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)=\sum_{\bm{\sigma}\in\Sigma_{\Lambda}}e^{-\beta H(\bm{\sigma})}

[formula]

is the partition function of the system. The thermodynamics of the BEG model is recovered from the the partition function
Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)
. namely, the (finite volume) free energy
of the model is given by

f_{\Lambda}(\beta)={1\over|\Lambda|}\ln Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)

[formula]

We’ll show in this section that the free energy (5.16) of the BEG model is analytic as function of
\beta
for
\beta
sufficiently large (i.e low temperature). This is achieved
by rewriting
Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)
defined in (5.15) as the partition function of a polymer system of the type considered in the previous sections. Hence the free energy of the BEG model
coincides with the pressure of this polymer system.
Then, using theorem
4.2
, we will prove that, in the disordered phase (5.14) and with the assumptions
(5.14), the pressure of such polymer system converges absolutely for
\beta
large enough (i.e. for sufficiently low temperatures).

In order to do that, let us define,
for a fixed spin configuration
\bm{\sigma}\in\Sigma_{\Lambda}
,
the subset of
\Lambda
given
by
P=\{x\in\Lambda:\sigma_{x}\neq 0\}
. We view this set as the union of its connected components,
i.e.
P=\cup_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}
with each set
p_{i}\subset\Lambda
being connected in the sense that
for each partition
A,B
of
p_{i}
(i.e.
A\cup B=p_{i}
and
A\cap B=\emptyset
) there exist
x\in A
and
y\in B
such that

|x-y|=1
.
The configuration
\bm{\sigma}
induces a (non zero) spin configuration
\bm{s}_{p_{i}}
on each connected component

p_{i}
of
P
which is a function

\bm{s}_{p_{i}}:p_{i}\to\{-1,+1\}:x\mapsto s_{x}
. The pairs
{\bm{p}}_{i}=(p_{i},\bm{s}_{p_{i}})
are the polymers associated to the configuration
\bm{\sigma}
.

We denote by
{\mathcal{Q}}
the set of all subsets of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
which are connected and finite.
Given
p\in{\mathcal{Q}}
, we denote by
S_{p}
the set of all (non zero) spin configurations
\bm{s}_{p}
in
p
(note that
|S_{p}|=2^{|p|})
.
By construction the correspondence
\bm{\sigma}\leftrightarrow\{\bm{p}_{1},\dots,\bm{p}_{n}\}
is one to one.
The distance between two polymers
\bm{p}=(p,\bm{s}_{p})
and
\tilde{\bm{p}}=(\tilde{p},\bm{s}_{\tilde{p}})

is the number
d(p,\tilde{p})=\min_{x\in p,\,\,y\in\tilde{p}}|x-y|
. Note that if
\{\bm{p}_{1},\dots,\bm{p}_{n}\}
are the polymers
associated to the configuration
\bm{\sigma}
, then necessarily
d(p_{i},p_{j})\geq 2
for all
\{i,j\}\subset\{1,\dots,n\}
.

With these definitions, given
\bm{\sigma}\in\Sigma_{\Lambda}
such that
\bm{\sigma}\leftrightarrow\{\bm{p}_{1},\dots,\bm{p}_{n}\}
, we can rewrite the Hamiltonian (5.13) as

H_{\Lambda}(\bm{s})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big[{\rm X}|p_{i}|-A(\bm{p}_{i})\Big]

where

A(\bm{p}_{i})=\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset p_{i}\atop|x-y|=1}[s_{x}s_{y}+{\rm Y}]

[formula]

Observe now that to sum over configuration
\bm{\sigma}\in\Sigma_{\Lambda}
is equivalent to sum over polymers configurations

\{\bm{p}_{1},\dots,\bm{p}_{n}\}
in
\Lambda
such that
n\geq 0
(
n=0
, i.e. no polymers, is the lowest energy configuration
\sigma_{x}=0
for all
x\in\Lambda
)
and
d(p_{i},p_{j})\geq 2
for all pairs
\{i,j\}\subset\{1,\dots,n\}
.
Hence the partition function of the system, at inverse temperature
\beta
and with free boundary conditions, is rewritten as

\displaystyle Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)

\displaystyle=~\sum_{\sigma_{\Lambda}}e^{-\beta H(\sigma_{\Lambda})}

[formula]

\displaystyle=~1~+~\sum_{n\geq 1}{1\over n!}\sum_{(\bm{p}_{1},\dots,\bm{p}_{n})\in{\cal P}^{n}\atop p_{i}\subset\Lambda,\;d(p_{i},p_{j})\geq 2}\rho_{\bm{p}_{1}}\dots\rho_{\bm{p}_{n}}

where

\rho_{\bm{p}}=e^{-\beta\big[{\rm X}|p|-A(\bm{p})\big]}

[formula]

and
{\cal P}
is the set

{\cal P}=\Big\{\bm{p}=(p,\bm{s}_{p}):\mbox{$p\in{\mathcal{Q}}$ and $\bm{s}_{p}\in S_{p}$}\Big\}

[formula]

Thus we have rewritten the partition function
Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)
of the BEG model as the partition function of an hard-core polymer gas in which the space of polymers is the set
given in (5.20). Each polymer
\bm{p}\in{\cal P}
has activity
\rho_{\bm{p}}
given by (5.19) and the hard core interaction
V(\bm{p}_{i},\bm{p}_{j})
between pair of polymers

p_{i},p_{j}
is

V(\bm{p}_{i},\bm{p}_{j})=\begin{cases}0&{\rm if}~d(p_{i},p_{j})\geq 2\\
+\infty&{\rm otherwise}\end{cases}

[formula]

With these definitions it is immediate to see that r.h.s. of (5.18) can be written as

\displaystyle Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{n\geq 1}{1\over n!}\sum_{(\bm{p}_{1},\dots,\bm{p}_{n})\in{\cal P}^{n}_{\Lambda}}\rho_{\bm{p}_{1}}\dots\rho_{\bm{p}_{n}}e^{-\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}V(\bm{p}_{i},\bm{p}_{j})}

[formula]

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{n\geq 1}{1\over n!}\sum_{(\bm{p}_{1},\dots,\bm{p}_{n})\in{\cal P}^{n}_{\Lambda}\atop\bm{p}_{i}\sim\bm{p}_{j}}\rho_{\bm{p}_{1}}\dots\rho_{\bm{p}_{n}}

where we have denoted shortly
{\cal P}_{\Lambda}=\{(p,\bm{s}_{p})\in{\cal P}:\;p\subset\Lambda\}
and
\bm{p}_{i}\sim\bm{p}_{j}
means that
d(p_{i},p_{j})\geq 2
(and similarly
ahead we use the symbol
\bm{p}_{i}\nsim\bm{p}_{j}
when
d(p_{i},p_{j})<2
).

So, by Theorem
4.1
, the pressure of this polymer gas (i.e. the free energy of our BEG model) is absolutely convergent
if there exist
\{\mu_{\bm{p}}\}_{\bm{p}\in{\cal P}}
such that
such that

\rho_{\bm{p}}\leq{\mu_{\bm{p}}\over\Xi_{\bm{p}}(\bm{\mu})}\;\;\;\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\forall\gamma\in{\cal P}

[formula]

with

\Xi_{\bm{p}}(\bm{\mu})=1+\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{1}{n!}\,\sum_{(\bm{p}_{1},\dots,\bm{p}_{n})\in{\cal P}^{n}\atop\bm{p}_{i}\not\sim\bm{p},\,\,\bm{p}_{i}\sim\bm{p}_{j}}{\mu_{\bm{p}_{1}}}\dots{\mu_{\bm{p}_{n}}}

Given
p\in{\mathcal{Q}}
let
\partial p=\{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus p:\exists y\in p~s.t.~|x-y|=1\}
be the the boundary of
p
and let

\bar{p}=p\cup\partial p
.

Given
\alpha>0
, we set

\mu_{\bm{p}}=\rho_{\bm{p}}e^{\alpha|\bar{p}|}

[formula]

Hence, inserting (5.24) in (5.23), we obtain that
the pressure of such contour gas can be written in terms of an absolutely convergent series if, for some
\alpha>0

\Xi_{\bm{p}}(\bm{\mu})\leq e^{\alpha|\bar{p}|}

[formula]

Now, similarly to what we did in Section
4.3.1
we can bound

\Xi_{\bm{p}}(\bm{\mu})\leq\Bigg[1+\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\,\sum_{\bm{p}\in{\cal P}\atop x\in p}\rho_{\bm{p}}e^{\alpha|\bar{p}|}\Bigg]^{|\bar{p}|}

so that condition (5.25) is satisfied if

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{\bm{p}\in{\cal P}\atop x\in p}\rho_{\bm{p}}e^{\alpha|\bar{p}|}\leq e^{\alpha}-1

[formula]

Now, recalling (5.19), observe that

A(\bm{p}_{i})=\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset p_{i}\atop|x-y|=1}[s_{x}s_{y}+{\rm Y}]\leq d(1+|{\rm Y}|)|p_{i}|

and so

\rho_{\bm{p}}\leq e^{-\beta D|p|}

where

D={\rm X}-d(1+|{\rm Y}|)

and observe that
D>0
by hypotheis (5.14). Therefore, choosing for simpliciy
\alpha=\ln 2
, condition (5.26) is satisfied if

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{\bm{p}\in{\cal P}\atop x\in p}e^{-\beta D|p|}2^{|\bar{p}|}\leq 1

[formula]

Now observe that surely
|\bar{p}|\leq 2d|p|+2\leq 3d|p|
. Therefore we get that condition (5.27) is satisfied if

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(2^{3d+1}e^{-\beta D})^{n}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{\bm{p}\in{\cal P}\atop x\in p,\;|p|=n}1\leq 1

[formula]

Now we have that

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{\bm{p}\in{\cal P}\atop x\in p,\;|p|=n}1=2^{n}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{p\in{\mathcal{Q}}\atop x\in p,\;|p|=n}1=2^{n}C_{n}

where
C_{n}
is the number of connected sets of vertices of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with cardinality
n

containing a fixed point
x
of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
(this number does not depend on
x
and can be chosen to be the orgin)
and the extra factor
2^{n}
counts the number of functions
\sigma_{p}

from
p
to
\{-1,+1\}
when
|p|=n
.
C_{n}
can be easily bounded by
C^{n}
for some
C
, e.g. one can
take
C_{n}\leq(4d)^{n}
. So condition (5.28) becomes

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(d2^{3d+3}e^{-\beta D})^{n}\leq 1

which is satisfied if

\beta\geq\beta^{*}\doteq{\ln(d2^{3d+4})\over D}

[formula]

Therefore for all
\beta\geq\beta^{*}
the free energy (5.16) of the BEG mode under the condition (5.14) is analytic in
\beta
an no phase transitions occur.

Chapter 6

Spin systems in a lattice

We denote by
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
the unit lattice
in
d
dimensions, i.e. an element
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is an
ordered
d
-ple
x~=~(n_{1},\dots,n_{d}):n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
. We will
consider
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
embedded in the standard way in

\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
\Lambda
be a finite subset of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
,
e.g.usually one takes al set
\Lambda
the set
\Lambda~=~\{(n_{1},\dots,n_{d}):n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z};-{L\over 2}\leq n_{i}\leq{L\over 2},\forall i~=~1,\dots,d\}
, i.e. all sites of the unit lattice inside
a cube with the center in the origin and with size
L
.

The definition of a spin system on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
begins by associating to each
site
x\in\Lambda
a variable
\phi_{x}
, (the ”spin” at the site
x
).
The spin
\phi_{x}
takes values in some space
\Omega
(which is the same for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
) equipped
with a probability measure
d\mu(\phi_{x})
and with a norm

\|\phi_{x}\|
. The single spin probability space
\Omega
depends of the type of
system we are treating and, in general, its structure can be very
different from case to case. The probability measure
d\mu(\phi_{x})

is called sometimes the single spin a priori distribution.

The simplest choice for which
\Omega
is assuming that
\Omega
is a finite set,
with
d\mu
assigning equal probability to any value of the spin in
this set
\Omega
. E.g. the choice
\Omega~=~\{1,-1\}
(i.e. the spin

\phi_{x}
at a site
x
can take only the two values
\phi_{x}~=~\pm 1
) and
d\mu(\phi_{x})~=~{1\over 2}\delta_{\phi_{x},+1}+{1\over 2}\delta_{\phi_{x},-1}
, (i.e. the probability that the spin
\phi_{x}
at
the site
x
has the value
+1
is
{1\over 2}
and so for

\phi_{x}~=~-1
) corresponds to Ising systems. For such systems
the random variable
\phi_{x}
is usually denote in the literature
by
\sigma_{x}
and when
\sigma_{x}~=~1
people say that the spin is “up”
while
\sigma_{x}~=~-1
is referred as spin “down”.

The choice
\Omega~=~\{0,1\}
and

d\mu(\phi_{x})~=~{1\over 2}\delta_{\phi_{x},+1}+{1\over 2}\delta_{\phi_{x},0}

corresponds to the Lattice gas considered in the previous sections. In this case the
variable
\phi_{x}
is usually denoted by
n_{x}
and interpreted as
an occupation number, namely if
n_{x}=1
then we say that a
particle is present at the site
x
and the site
x
is occupied,
while if
n_{x}=0
then no particle is present at
x
and the site

x
is empty.

The choice
\Omega~=~\{1,2,\dots,q\}
with
q
positive integer
and
d\mu(\phi_{x})~=~{1\over q}[\delta_{\phi_{x},1}+{1\over q}\delta_{\phi_{x},2}+\dots+{1\over q}\delta_{\phi_{x},q}]
correspond to
the so called Potts model.

A less simple choice for
\Omega
is to assume
\Omega
infinite but compact. A typical example
is the (classical) Heisenberg model for which
\Omega~=~\{\phi_{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|\phi_{x}\|~=~1\}
, i.e.

\Omega
is the unit sphere in
d
dimensions, and
d\mu(\phi_{x})~=~[{\rm volume}~\Omega]^{-1}d\Omega(\phi_{x})
, where
d\Omega(\phi_{x})
is the
usual Rienmann measure on the sphere.

Spin systems for which
\Omega
is a compact space
(in the sense of the norm
\|\phi_{x}\|
), are usually called bounded spin systems. But we could also consider the case in
which
\Omega
is not a compact space. Spin systems for which the
single spin space
\Omega
is a non compact space are usually known as
unbounded spin systems. In general bounded spin systems are
treated easier than unbounded spin systems.

As an example of a unbounded spin system we will consider ahead the so called

\lambda\phi^{4}
field theory, for which
\Omega~=~\mathbb{R}
, (i.e. the
spin take values in
\mathbb{R}
) and

d\mu(\phi_{x})~=~{Z}^{-1}e^{-U(\phi_{x})}d\phi_{x}

where
U(\phi_{x})~=~\lambda\phi^{4}
with
\lambda>0

and
Z~=~\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-U(\phi_{x})}d\phi_{x}
. In this case

\phi_{x}
is interpreted as the value of the field at the site
x
.

A configuration
\phi
of a general spin system in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is given once it has been specified
the value of
\phi_{x}\in\Omega
for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. More
precisely, a configuration
\phi
is a function
\phi:\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\Omega
. The set of all configuration
\phi
of the system is the set

\Phi=\Omega^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
. So a configuration
\phi
is
an element of a very big space
\Phi
and in general it is
mathematically problematic to define a probability measure on this
space. Even the product measure
\prod_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\mu(\phi_{x})
can
be difficult to define rigorously depending on the structure of
the space
\Omega
(e.g. in case that
\Omega
in non compact).

Hence, next step towards the definition of a spin system in the lattice it to fix some finite
subset
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and then consider our system of spins
restricted to this set
\Lambda
. So a configuration
\phi_{\Lambda}
of the
system restricted to
\Lambda
is given once it has been specified the
value of
\phi_{x}\in\Omega
for all
x\in\Lambda
.
We denote by
\Phi_{\Lambda}=\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\Omega
the set of all spin configurations restricted to
\Lambda
.
Now the product measure

d\mu(\phi_{\Lambda})=\prod_{x\in\Lambda}d\mu(\phi_{x})
in
\Phi_{\Lambda}
is
rigorously defined without no particular difficulties. If
X
is
any subset of
\Lambda
, then symbols as
\Phi_{X}
and
d\mu(\phi_{X})

are defined in the obvious way.

The boundary conditions are in general chosen by
fixing a spin configuration
\phi_{\Lambda^{c}}
for the spins outside
\Lambda
(i.e. in the set
\Lambda^{c}~=~\mathbb{V}-\Lambda
). Such choice can be sometimes
limited due to the structure of the single spin space
\Omega_{x}
. But other choices are also possible. E.g. two very popular choices
of the boundary conditions are the so-called free boundary conditions and periodic boundary conditions.
In particular, the free boundary condition choice is to say that
“there is no outside”, i.e. simply to study the spin system in the finite volume
\Lambda
with the identification

\mathbb{V}=\Lambda
. Also the periodic boundary conditions follows the same philosophy. However to be stated
\mathbb{V}
has to possesses some additional structure.
For example, if
\mathbb{V}=\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, one has imposed boundary conditions by saying that
\Lambda
is a torus in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

Finally, the Hamiltonian or interaction between spins, denoted here by
H(\phi_{\Lambda},\phi_{\Lambda^{c}})
, is
in general expressed in terms of a potential

\Psi
, i.e. an assignment to each nonempty subset
X\subset\mathbb{V}
of real valued measurable function

\Psi(X,\phi_{X})
definied in
\Omega_{X}
. Once
\Psi
has been given, and
boundary conditions are give through a fixed
configuration
\phi_{\Lambda^{c}}
in
\mathbb{V}\backslash\Lambda
,
the Hamiltonian
H(\phi_{\Lambda},\phi_{\Lambda^{c}})
of the discrete
spin system confined in the volume
\Lambda
is given (when boundary conditions are give through a fixed
configuration
\phi_{\Lambda^{c}}
in
\mathbb{V}\backslash\Lambda
) by

H(\phi_{\Lambda},\phi_{\Lambda^{c}})=\sum_{X:X\cap\Lambda\neq\emptyset\atop|X|\geq 2}\Psi(X,\phi_{X})

[formula]

In the majority of the applications presented in this book the potential
\Psi

will be defined only for pairs of spins, namely
\Psi(X,\phi)\neq 0
only for
|X|=2
. In this case
\Psi
is called a
pair potential.

Note that the
Hamiltonian (6.1) can be also written as

V(\phi_{\Lambda},\phi_{\Lambda^{c}})~=~H_{\rm bulk}(\phi_{\Lambda})+W(\phi_{\Lambda},\phi_{\Lambda^{c}})

[formula]

where

H_{\rm bulk}(\phi_{\Lambda})~=~\sum_{X\subset\Lambda}\Psi(X,\phi_{X})

and

W(\phi_{\Lambda},\phi_{\Lambda^{c}})~=~\sum_{X:X\cap\Lambda\neq\emptyset\atop X\cap\Lambda^{c}\neq\emptyset}\Psi(X,\phi_{X})

where evidently the term
H_{\rm bulk}
represent the “internal”
energy inside
\Lambda
of the system, which does not depend on boundary
conditions, while
W(\phi_{\Lambda},\phi_{\Lambda^{c}})
, which is the boundary
condition term, represents the interaction of the system in

\Lambda
with the “outside”. Note that in the
free boundary conditions one sets
W(\phi_{\Lambda},\phi_{\Lambda^{c}})=0
, which indeed corresponds to suppose that there is no outside.

Once we give explicitly
(\Omega,\mu)
, the boundary conditions and the Hamiltonian,
then the measure
\Pi
of the lattice system is defined by

d\Pi(\phi_{\Lambda})={d\mu(\phi_{\Lambda})e^{-\beta H(\phi_{\Lambda},\phi_{\Lambda^{c}})}\over\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta)}

[formula]

where
\beta>0
is a parameterusually interpreted in statistical mechanics as the inverse temperature of the system
and the normalization constant
\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta)
, given by

Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)=\int d\mu(\phi_{\Lambda})e^{-\beta H(\phi_{\Lambda},\phi_{\Lambda^{c}})}

[formula]

is usually called the Partition functions of the system.

6.1

Some examples

1. The celebrated Ising model falls easily in the structure defined above
.
Here the set
\mathbb{V}
is typically the lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
in
d
dimensions,
and the model is defined on a box
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

The role of the spin
\phi_{x}
,
x\in\Lambda
, is played by a random variable
usually denoted by
\sigma_{x}
which can
take one of the two possible value
\sigma_{x}~=~\pm 1
. A configuration

{\bf\sigma}_{\Lambda}\in\{-1,1\}^{|\Lambda|}
of the system is given once the value of the
spin
\sigma_{x}
for each site
x\in\Lambda
is specified. Hence
{\bf\sigma}_{\Lambda}
is a set

{\bf\sigma}_{\Lambda}~=~(\sigma_{x_{1}},\dots,\sigma_{x_{|\Lambda|}})
of
|\Lambda|
numbers
\pm 1
. Equivalently one can say that a configuration
\sigma_{\Lambda}
of the
system is a function
\sigma_{\Lambda}:\Lambda\to\{+1,-1\}
.
Observe that the total number of configurations of the system in

\Lambda
is
2^{|\Lambda|}
.

Given a configurations
{\bf\sigma}_{\Lambda}
of the system in
\Lambda
, the energy
of such configuration is

H_{\Lambda}({\bf\sigma}_{\Lambda})~=~-J\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda\atop x,y~{\rm nearest~neighbors}}\sigma_{x}\sigma_{y}-h\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\sigma_{x}+{\cal B}(\sigma_{\Lambda})

[formula]

where
h
is an “external magnetic field”,
J
is a real
constant representing the intensity of the nearest neighbors interaction,
and the model is said ferromagnetic when
J>0
and antiferromagnetic
otherwise.

{\cal B}(\sigma_{\Lambda})
represents the interaction of the
spins inside the box
\Lambda
with a fixed configuration outside
\Lambda
(i.e. in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}\backslash\Lambda
).

The statistical mechanics
of the Ising model is obtained by assigning to any configuration
\sigma_{\Lambda}

a probability to occur
P(\sigma_{\Lambda})
. This probability is given by

P(\sigma_{\Lambda})~=~{e^{-\beta H_{\Lambda}({\bf\sigma}_{\Lambda})}\over Z_{\Lambda}(\beta,h)}

[formula]

where

Z_{\Lambda}(\beta,h)~=~\sum_{{\bf\sigma}_{\Lambda}}e^{-\beta H_{\Lambda}({\bf\sigma}_{\Lambda})}

is the partition function. The term
e^{-\beta H_{\Lambda}({\bf\sigma}_{\Lambda})}
is called
Boltzmann weight of the configuration
{\bf\sigma}_{\Lambda}
.

The structure of the probability distribution above shows that if the system
is ferromagnetic the spins tend to align, i.e. the configurations have
higher probability if they have a large number of
nearest neighbors assuming the same direction of the spin,
while in the antiferromagnetic case the configuration
with nearest neighbors of the opposite sign are privileged.

In order to point out the ingredients of the general structure of the
discrete systems described above, we will consider for simplicity free boundary conditions,
namely we put
{\cal B}(\sigma_{\Lambda})=0
in (6.5). Then observe that

Z_{\Lambda}(\beta,h)~=~\sum_{{\bf\sigma}_{\Lambda}}\Big[\prod_{x\in\Lambda}e^{h\sigma_{x}}\Big]e^{-\beta J\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda\atop x,y~{\rm near.~neigh.}}\sigma_{x}\sigma_{y}}=

~=~[2\cosh(\beta h)]^{|\Lambda|}\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,h)

where

\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,h)=\int d\mu(\sigma_{\Lambda})e^{-\beta J\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda\atop x,y~{\rm near.~neigh.}}\sigma_{x}\sigma_{y}}

[formula]

It is clear now that giving to the term
J\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda\atop x,y~{\rm near.~neigh.}}\sigma_{x}\sigma_{y}
the role of the potential
V

defined in (6.1),
we have that the Ising model is a lattice system in which
\Omega_{x}=\{1,-1\}
,
the single spin distribution is the discrete measure given by

\mu(\sigma_{x}=+1)={e^{\beta Jh}\over\cosh\beta h},~~~~~\mu(\sigma_{x}=-1)={e^{-\beta Jh}\over\cos h\beta Jh}

and the interactions
\Psi(X,\phi_{X})
are defined only for
X
of the form
X=\{x,y\}
with

x
and
y
nearest neighbors.

The Ising model, despite its simplicity, has a very rich structure which make it
one of the most interesting model in statistical mechanics. We shall investigate
some of its properties in chapter ??.

2. The Potts model is a generalization of the Ising model.
The system is again defined on a box
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}

which is a square subset of the lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
in
d
dimensions,
but here the spin
\sigma_{x}
varies in the set
\{1,2,\dots,q\}

where
q
is an integer. The Hamiltonian is given by

H_{\Lambda}(\sigma_{\Lambda})=-J\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda\atop x,y~{\rm nearest~neighbors}}\delta_{\sigma_{x}\sigma_{y}}

[formula]

where

\delta_{\sigma_{x}\sigma_{y}}
is the Kronecker symbol which is equal to one when
\sigma_{x}=\sigma_{y}
and
zero otherwise.
Again, the coupling
J
is called
ferromagnetic if
J>0
and anti-ferromagnetic if

J<0
.

As in the case of the Ising model the statistical mechanics of the system
can be done by
introducing the Boltzmann weight of a configuration
\sigma_{\Lambda}
, defined a
s

\exp\{-\beta H_{\Lambda}(\sigma_{\Lambda})\}
. Then the
probability to find the system in the configuration
\sigma_{\Lambda}
is given b
y

{\rm Prob}(\sigma_{\Lambda})={e^{-\beta H_{\Lambda}(\sigma_{\Lambda})}\over Z_{\Lambda}(q,\beta)}

[formula]

Again the normalization constant in the denominator
is called the partition function and is given by

Z_{\Lambda}(q,\beta)=\sum_{\sigma_{\Lambda}\in\{1,2,...,q\}^{|\Lambda|}}e^{-\beta H_{\Lambda}(\sigma_{\Lambda})}

[formula]

The case
\beta J=-\infty
is the anti-ferromagnetic and
zero temperature Potts model with
q
-states. In this case
configurations with non zero probability are only those in
which adjacent spins have different values (or colors) and

Z_{\Lambda}(q,\beta)
becomes simply the number of all allowed
configurations (proper colorings).

The Potts model is obviously in the class above. The spin
\sigma_{x}
plays the role
of
\phi_{x}
,

\Omega_{x}=\{1,\dots,q\}
, the single spin distribution
is the uniform distribution on
\{1,\dots,q\}

and as above the interaction is defined only for
X
of the form
X=\{x,y\}
wit
h

x
and
y
nearest neighbors.

Namely, we can write

Z_{\Lambda}(q,\beta)=q^{|\Lambda|}\int d\mu(\sigma_{\Lambda})\in e^{-\beta H_{\Lambda}(\sigma_{\Lambda})}

where
\Omega_{x}=\{1,2,\dots,q\}
, and
the single spin distribution is the discrete uniform measure on
\Omega_{x}=\{1,2,\dots,q\}
given by

\mu(\sigma_{x}=i)={1\over q}~~~~~~~~~~\forall i\in\{1,2,\dots,q\}

and the interactions
\Psi(X,\phi_{X})
are defined only for
X
of the form
X=\{x,y\}
with

x
and
y
nearest neighbors.

3. Let us now consider the example of lattice gases interacting through
pair potential. This will be a crucial example in the following sections.

In statistical mechanics (see also below, section ??), the gran canonical
partition function of a lattice gas of identical particles
enclosed in a cubic volume
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with activity
\lambda
,
interacting via a pair potential

V(x,y)
(
x
and
y
sites in
\Lambda
)
is given by

Z_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\lambda^{n}\over n!}\sum_{(x_{1},...,x_{n})\in\Lambda^{n}}e^{-\beta\sum_{i,j}V(x_{i},x_{j})}

[formula]

As far as lattice gas is concerned, it is natural to assume that at most one particle can sit in each site

x
,
hence we suppose that
V(x,x)=+\infty
. In this case (6.11) can be written in
the following way

Z_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)=\sum_{\sigma_{\Lambda}\in\{0,1\}^{|\Lambda|}}\lambda^{\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\sigma_{x}}e^{-\beta\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}\sigma_{x}\sigma_{y}V(x,y)}

[formula]

where

\sum_{\sigma_{\Lambda}\in\{0,1\}^{|\Lambda|}}
means sum over all configurations

\{\sigma_{x}\}
with
x\in I
and
the variables
\sigma_{x}
are such that
\sigma_{x}=1
if the site is occupied
by a particle
and
\sigma_{x}=0
if it is empty.

It is easy to see that this model falls in the class of discrete systems defined
in
(6.3) and (6.4). Namely we can write

Z_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)=(1+\lambda)^{|\Lambda|}\int d\mu(\sigma_{\Lambda})e^{-\beta\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}\sigma_{x}\sigma_{y}V(x,y)}

where
\Omega_{x}=\{0,1\}
,
the single spin distribution is

\mu(\sigma_{x}=0)={1\over 1+\lambda},~~~~~\mu(\sigma_{x}=1)={\lambda\over 1+\lambda}

and the interactions
\Psi(X,\phi_{X})
are defined only for
X=\{x,y\}

and have the form
\Psi(X,\phi_{X})=\sigma_{x}\sigma_{y}V(x,y)
.

The model can be easily generalized to a lattice gas with a many body potential
writing

\Xi_{\Lambda}(\beta,\lambda)=\sum_{\sigma_{\Lambda}\in\{0,1\}^{|\Lambda|}}\lambda^{\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\sigma_{x}}e^{-\beta\sum_{X\subset\Lambda}\sigma_{X}V(X)}

[formula]

where
\sigma_{X}=\prod_{x\in X}\sigma_{x}

4. An example of discrete system in which the single spin state space is continuous
but compact is the so-called Heisemberg model. The system has the same structure
of the Ising model, but the spins are unit vector in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, i.e.
\sigma_{x}\in S_{1}^{d}
,
where
S_{1}^{d}
is the unit sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We will denote with
a\cdot b
the scalar product
between the two vectors
a
and
b
.
Hence the Hamiltonian of the system is given by

H_{\Lambda}({\bf\sigma}_{\Lambda})~=~-J\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda\atop x,y~{\rm nearest~neighbors}}\sigma_{x}\cdot\sigma_{y}-\sum_{x\in\Lambda}h\cdot\sigma_{x}

where
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is an “external magnetic field”,
J

represents again the intensity of the nearest neighbors interaction,
and the model is said ferromagnetic when
J>0
and antiferromagnetic
otherwise.
We are using once again free boundary condition for sake of simplicity.

The partition function of the Heisemberg model is

Z_{\Lambda}(\beta,h)~=~\int_{(S_{1}^{d})^{|\Lambda|}}\prod_{x\in\Lambda}d\sigma_{x}e^{-\beta H_{\Lambda}({\bf\sigma}_{\Lambda})}

The Heisemberg model falls in the class of the discrete systems defined above.
We can indeed write

Z_{\Lambda}(\beta,h)~=~M^{|\Lambda|}\int d\mu(\sigma_{x})\prod_{x\in\Lambda}d\sigma_{x}e^{-J\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda\atop x,y~{\rm nearest~neighbors}}\sigma_{x}\cdot\sigma_{y}}

where

d\mu(\sigma_{x})={d\sigma_{x}\ e^{\beta h\cdot\sigma_{x}}\over M}\qquad M=\int_{S_{1}^{d}}d\sigma_{x}\ e^{\beta h\cdot\sigma_{x}}

and
d\sigma_{x}
denotes the Lebesgue measure on the surface of the sphere
S_{1}^{d}
.

5. As a final example we introduce here a discrete model in which the single spin state space
in non-compact. This model is very important, both in statistical mechanics and
in field theory.

We take as
\mathbb{V}
the lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{d}

equipped with
the usual Euclidean distance. Let
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
be a cubic subset of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
.

The spin variable
\phi_{x}
, called also
field, takes values in
{\mathbb{R}}
.
A configuration

\phi_{\Lambda}

is a function
\phi:\Lambda\to{\mathbb{R}}
.

We define the boundary conditions of the system fixing a value of the
field
\phi_{x}
for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\Lambda

We consider the discrete model described by the (Gibbs) measure

\mu_{\Lambda}^{\omega}(\cdot)={1\over Z(\Lambda,\omega)}\int\prod_{x\in\Lambda}d\phi_{x}e^{-H(\phi_{\Lambda},\omega)}(\cdot)

[formula]

where
d\phi_{x}
is the Lebesgue measure in
\mathbb{R}
and
the partition function
Z(\Lambda,\omega)
is defined by

Z(\Lambda,\omega)=\int\prod_{x\in\Lambda}d\phi_{x}e^{-H(\phi_{\Lambda},\omega)}

[formula]

The Hamiltonian of the system is

H(\phi_{\Lambda},\omega)=\sum_{x\in\Lambda}U(\phi_{x})-\sum_{\{x,y\}\cap\Lambda\neq\emptyset}J_{xy}\phi_{x}\phi_{y}+\sum_{x\in\Lambda}h_{x}\phi_{x}=

=\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\left[U(\phi_{x})-\phi_{x}\omega_{x}\right]-\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}J_{xy}\phi_{x}\phi_{y}

[formula]

U(x)
is an even polynomial of degree
2k
,
k>1
,
of the form

U(x)=x^{2k}+\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}u_{2i}x^{2i}

[formula]

with
u_{2i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
;
the pair potential
J_{xy}
is such that

\sup_{x\in{\bf Z}^{d}}\sum_{y\neq x}|J_{xy}|=J<+\infty

[formula]

h_{x}\in\mathbb{R}
represents the external field, and in the last line
of (6.16) we defined

\omega_{x}=-h_{x}+\sum_{y\in\Lambda^{c}}J_{xy}\phi_{y}

[formula]

The boundary fields
\phi_{y}
with
y\in\Lambda^{c}
must be
chosen in such way that
\sum_{y\in\Lambda^{c}}J_{xy}\phi_{y}
is finite
for all
x
.

This model is again in the class defined above. We can write

Z(\Lambda,\omega)=M^{|\Lambda|}\int\prod_{x\in\Lambda}d\mu(\phi_{x})e^{-\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}J_{xy}\phi_{x}\phi_{y}}

[formula]

The single spin distribution is in this
case
dependent form the site
x
:

d\mu(\phi_{x})={d\phi_{x}e^{-U(\phi_{x})-\phi_{x}\omega_{x}}\over M}

where

M=\int d\phi_{x}e^{-U(\phi_{x})-\phi_{x}\omega_{x}}

The system we just defined has a lot of very interesting features, and
some of them will be extensively discussed in details in section ??

6.2

Polymer gas representation of
Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)

We need to recall here some definitions and notations about graphs and more generally hypergraphs.

If
A
is any finite
set, we denote by
|A|
the number of elements of
A
.
An hypergraph

g
is a pair
g=(V,E)
where
V
is a finite or countable set and
E
is a collection
of distinct subsets of
V
with cardinality greater or equal than 2. The elements
x\in V
are called vertices
of the hypergraph
g
and
V
is the set of vertices of
g
. The elements
e\in E
are called edges of the hypergraph
and
E
is the set of edges of
g
. A graph
g

such that

|e|=2
for all
e\in E

is called a graph.
Let
g=(V,E)
be a hypergraph, let
U\subset V
and let
E|_{U}=\{e\in E:e\subset U\}
. Then the graph
g|_{U}=(U,E|_{U})
is
called the restriction of
G
to
U
. A hypergraph
g^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})
is a subgraph of
g=(V,E)
if
V^{\prime}\subset V
, and
E^{\prime}\subset E|_{V^{\prime}}
.

An hypergraph
g=(V_{g},E_{g})
is said to be connected
if for any pair
B,C
of subsets of
V
such that

B\cup C=V_{g}
and
B\cap C=\emptyset
, there is a
e\in E_{g}
such
that
e\cap B\neq\emptyset
and
e\cap C\neq\emptyset
. A connected component of an hypergraph
g=(V_{g},E_{g})

is a a subset
U\subset V
such that
g|_{U}
is connected and for any
U^{\prime}
such that
V\supseteq U^{\prime}\supset U
,
g|_{U^{\prime}}
is not connected. Note that
a connected component of
g=(V_{g},E_{g})
can be a singleton. The set of all connected components of
g=(V_{g},E_{g})
form a partition
of
V_{g}
.
We denote by
{\cal G}_{V}
the set of all hypergraphs
with vertex set
V
and by
{G}_{V}
the set of all connected hypergraphs
with vertex set
V
.

In order to make things easier we will
consider just free boundary condition. This means that the
partition function of our spin system is given by

Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)~=~\int d\mu(\phi_{\Lambda})\exp\left[-\beta\sum_{X\subset\Lambda}\Psi(X,\phi_{X})\right]

[formula]

Let us now use the
Mayer expansion trick and rewrite our partition function in the following way

Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)~=~\int d\mu(\phi_{\Lambda})\prod_{X\subset\Lambda}\exp\left[-\beta\Psi(X,\phi_{X})\right]~=~

~=~\int d\mu(\phi_{\Lambda})\prod_{X\subset\Lambda}[(e^{-\beta\Psi(X,\phi_{X})}-1)+1]~=~

Now, posing
U_{X}=e^{-\beta\Psi(X,\phi_{X})}-1
we
see that

\prod_{X\subset\Lambda}[U_{X}+1]=\sum_{g\in{\cal G}_{\Lambda}}\prod_{X\in E_{g}}U_{X}

(here we agree that the graph with no edges, i.e.
g
such that with
E_{g}=\emptyset
yields the contribution
1 to the sum above) and hence we get

Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)~=~\int d\mu(\phi_{\Lambda})\sum_{g\in{\cal G}_{\Lambda}}\prod_{X\in E_{g}}(e^{-\beta\Psi(X,\phi_{X})}-1)

Note again that the graph
with empty edge set yields the contribution
1 in equation above, since by definition
\int d\mu(\phi_{\Lambda})1~=~1
.

We now can group together connected components
in
\sum_{g\in{\cal G}_{\Lambda}}
as follows. First note every graph
g\in{\cal G}_{\Lambda}
determines
a partition of
\Lambda
into its connected components. Hence

\sum_{g\in{\cal G}_{\Lambda}}\prod_{X\in g}(e^{-\beta\Psi(X,\phi_{X})}-1)~=~\sum_{k=0}^{|\Lambda|}\sum_{\{R_{1},\dots R_{k}\}\atop{\rm partition~of~\Lambda}}\prod_{i~=~1}^{k}\sum_{g\in{{G}_{R_{i}}}}\prod_{X\in E_{g}}(e^{-\beta\Psi(X,\phi_{X})}-1)

where by convention the term corresponding to
k=0
is set equal to 1 and
\sum_{g\in{{G}_{R}}}\prod_{X\in g}(e^{-\beta\Psi(X,\phi_{X})}-1)=1
if
|R|=1
.

Hence we can also write

Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)=\int d\mu(\phi_{\Lambda})\sum_{k=0}^{|\Lambda|}\sum_{\{R_{1},\dots R_{k}\}:~R_{i}\subset\Lambda\atop R_{i}\cap R_{j}~=~\emptyset,~|R_{i}|\geq 2}\prod_{i~=~1}^{k}\sum_{g\in{{G}_{R_{i}}}}\prod_{X\in g}(e^{-\beta\Psi(X,\phi_{X})}-1)

where
\{R_{1},\dots R_{k}\}
is a non ordered
k
-ple of mutually non
intersecting subsets of
\Lambda
with cardinality greater than 2.
Now, recalling that
d\mu(\phi_{\Lambda})
is a product
measure, we get

Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)~=~\sum_{k=1}^{|\Lambda|}\sum_{\{R_{1},\dots R_{k}\}:~R_{i}\subset\Lambda\atop R_{i}\cap R_{j}~=~\emptyset,~|R_{i}|\geq 2}\rho_{\beta}(R_{1})\dots\rho_{\beta}(R_{k})

with

\rho_{\beta}(R)~=~\int\prod_{x\in R}d\mu(\phi_{x})\sum_{g\in{G}_{R}}\prod_{X\in g}(e^{-\beta\Psi(X,\phi_{X})}-1)

and evidently

\sum_{k=0}^{|\Lambda|}\sum_{\{R_{1},\dots R_{k}\}:~R_{i}\subset\Lambda\atop R_{i}\cap R_{j}~=~\emptyset,~|R_{i}|\geq 2}~~\rho_{\beta}(R_{1})\dots\rho_{\beta}(R_{k})=~~~~\sum_{k\geq 0}{1\over k!}\sum_{(R_{1},\dots,R_{k}):~R_{i}\subset\Lambda\atop R_{i}\cap R_{j}=\emptyset,~|R_{i}|\geq 2}~~~~\rho_{\beta}(R_{1})\dots\rho_{\beta}(R_{k})

where in the l.h.s we are summing over unordered
k
-uples
\{R_{1},\dots R_{k}\}
while in the r.h.s we are summing over
ordered
k
-uples
(R_{1},\dots R_{k})
.

So we have shown that the free boundary condition
partition function
Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)
defined in (6.21) can always
be written as

Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)~=~\sum_{k\geq 0}{1\over k!}\sum_{(R_{1},\dots,R_{k}):~R_{i}\subset\Lambda\atop R_{i}\cap R_{j}~=~\emptyset,~|R_{i}|\geq 2}\rho_{\beta}(R_{1})\dots\rho_{\beta}(R_{k})

[formula]

It is now easy to convince one-self that expression above is actually
the grand canonical partition function of a
gas of particle of different species in which the particles (or “polymers”)
R
are subsets of

\Lambda
with cardinality greater or equal 2, with activity
\rho(R)
. Note that the activity
is not the same for every polymer, i.e. different polymers have in principle
different activities. These polymers are interacting via a two-body
interaction which prevent the overlapping between different polymers. Indeed, the
only interaction between polymers is a repulsive
hard core pair interaction. Namely, if we define for any pair of
polymers
R_{i},R_{j}

U(R_{i},R_{j})~=~\begin{cases}0&ifR_{i}\cap R_{j}~=~\emptyset\\
+\infty&ifR_{i}\cap R_{j}\neq\emptyset\end{cases}

[formula]

we have proved the following elementary but fundamental theorem

Theorem 6.1

The partition function
Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)
defined in (6.21) can be written as

Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)~=~\sum_{k\geq 0}{1\over k!}\!\sum_{R_{1},\dots,R_{k}\atop|R_{i}|\geq 2}\!\prod_{j=1}^{k}\rho_{\beta}(R_{j})e^{-\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}U(R_{i},R_{j})}

[formula]

where

\rho_{\beta}(R)~=~\int\prod_{x\in R}d\mu(\phi_{x})\sum_{g\in{G}_{R}}\prod_{X\in g}(e^{-\beta\Psi(X,\phi_{X})}-1)

[formula]

In conclusion theorem
6.1
states that the partition function of discrete spin systems can be rewritten
as the grand canonical partition function of a polymer gas in which the polymers
R
are finite subsets
of
\mathbb{V}
with cardinality greater or equal than 2 and a polymer
R
has activity
\rho(R)
. Such way to rewrite the partition
function of a discrete system is called the high temperature polymer gas expansion of the system.

Note that all the information about the original system is now contained in the structure of the
activity
\rho(R)
of a given polymer, given by (6.25).

Note also that
\rho_{\beta}(R)\to 0
as
\beta\to 0
. Hence the activity of
polymers is small when
\beta
is small. So it is reasonable to
expect the convergence of this polymer gas when the parameter

\beta
is sufficiently small. This is the reason why such an
expansion is called “high temperature expansion”.

We want to outline here that we meet again in the structure of polymer activity (6.25), the combinatorial
problems we have seen in section
3.1.3
. Here such problems are even worst because the factors

(e^{-\beta\Psi(X,\phi_{X})}-1)
in (6.25) when dealing with many-body interactions and hypergraphs.
As far as discrete spin systems with purely pair interactions are concerned,
we will get rid of this challenging
combinatorial problem by means of the previously seen tree graph identities.

Many body interaction may also be treated, with a completely different technique, as we will see in the section
6.3
.

6.2.1

General spin systems interacting via pair potentials

General many body
interactions are usually quite difficult be treated, in particular
in the case in which the single spin probability space
\Omega
is non
compact. Hereafter we will consider only pair interaction,
i.e. we will assume that
\Psi(X,\phi_{X})~=~0
whenever
|X|\neq 2
.
Hence our interactions will be always pair interactions of the
form
\Psi(\{x,y\},\phi_{x},\phi_{y})
with
x
and
y
varying in

\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. This function
\Psi(x,y,\phi_{x},\phi_{y})
is supposed to
satisfies some properties. In particular we will demand that there
exist a positive symmetric function
J(\{x,y\})
(i.e.
J_{yx}=J_{xy}
) such that

|\Psi(\{x,y\},\phi_{x},\phi_{y})|\leq J_{xy}\|\phi_{x}\|\|\phi_{y}\|

[formula]

and

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\atop y\neq x}J_{xy}~\doteq~J<\infty

[formula]

It is crucial to point out that conditions (6.26) and (6.27) automatically implies that the potential

\Psi(\{x,y\},\phi_{x},\phi_{y})
is stable in the following sense. Given any finite set
R\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
(with cardinality
|R|\geq 2
), it holds that

\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset R}\Psi(\{x,y\},\phi_{x},\phi_{y})\geq-{J\over 2}\sum_{x\in R}\|\phi_{x}\|^{2}

[formula]

Indeed,

\displaystyle\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset R}\Psi(\{x,y\},\phi_{x},\phi_{y})

\displaystyle\geq-\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset R}|J_{xy}\|\phi_{x}\|\|\phi_{y}\|

\displaystyle\geq~-\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset R}J_{xy}{1\over 2}\Big[\|\phi_{x}\|^{2}+\|\phi_{y}\|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset R}J_{xy}\|\phi_{x}\|^{2}

\displaystyle={1\over 2}\sum_{x\in R}\|\phi_{x}\|^{2}\sum_{y\in R\atop y\neq x}J_{xy}

\displaystyle\geq-{J\over 2}\sum_{x\in R}\|\phi_{x}\|^{2}

Once a function
\Psi
with the properties (6.26) and (6.27) above has been given, we can
construct the Hamiltonian of the system. To do this we first
fix a spin configuration
\phi_{\Lambda^{c}}
for the spins outside the
box
\Lambda
(i.e. in the set
\Lambda^{c}~=~\mathbb{Z}^{d}-\Lambda
). This correspond to
choose the boundary conditions for the system restricted to

\Lambda
. Such choice is totally free in the case of bounded spin
system, while, for consistency reasons, we will see in a moment
that there are limitations for such choice in the case of
unbounded spin systems.

Once the boundary condition
\phi_{\Lambda^{c}}
has been fixed outside

\Lambda
, then, for any configuration
\phi_{\Lambda}
inside
\Lambda
, the
Hamiltonian of the system is given by

H^{\phi_{\Lambda^{c}}}(\phi_{\Lambda})~=~\sum_{\{x,y\}\cap\Lambda\neq\emptyset}\Psi(\{x,y\},\phi_{x},\phi_{y})

[formula]

The number
H(\phi_{\Lambda})
represents the energy associated to the
configuration
\phi_{\Lambda}
of the spin system.
In order to make things easier we will consider hereafter just free boundary condition (no influence of the exterior on the system confined in the volume
\Lambda
). Namely we will suppose that our Hamiltonian is

H(\phi_{\Lambda})~=~\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}\Psi(\{x,y\},\phi_{x},\phi_{y})

[formula]

Now that we have defined the spin systems (and its single spin a priori distribution), the
space of configurations, the Hamiltonian (i.e. the energy
associated to each system configuration) and the boundary
conditions we are ready to study the statitic/thermodynamic
behavior of the system. The last step is to define a probability
associate to each configuration
\phi_{\Lambda}
of the system in
\Lambda
.
So we define the probability to find the system in a given
configuration
\{\phi_{x}\}_{x\in\Lambda}
of spins, with energy

H(\phi_{\Lambda})
as

{\rm Prob}(\phi_{\Lambda})~=~{e^{-\beta H(\phi_{\Lambda})}d\mu(\phi_{\Lambda})\over\int d\mu(\phi_{\Lambda})e^{-\beta H}}

[formula]

the parameter
\beta^{-1}
performs the link with thermodynamics and
it is interpreted as the temperature of the system. The constant

Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)~=~\int d\mu(\phi_{\Lambda})e^{-\beta H}
in (6.31) is the
partition function of the system

Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)~=~\int d\mu(\phi_{\Lambda})\exp\left[-\beta\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}\Psi(\{x,y\},\phi_{x},\phi_{y})\right]

[formula]

The partition function (6.32) is the key quantity in order
to study thermodynamic properties of the system. E.g. the
thermodynamic free energy of the system is defined as

f(\beta)~=~\lim_{\Lambda\to\infty}{1\over|\Lambda|}\log Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)

[formula]

where of course the limit
\Lambda\to\infty
has to be interpreted in
the sense of Fisher or Van Hove or even in a more restrictive
fashion, e.g. cubic boxes with size
N\to\infty
.

In particular, the question about the analyticity of

f(\beta)
as a function of
\beta
is very important. Accordingly to
the physics belief: No phase transition can occur in the
system wherever
f(\beta)
analytic. Concerning this question, we
could say that there is a sort of “meta-theorem” stating that
any reasonable spin system has free energy analytic if
\beta
is
sufficiently small. Note that
\beta
sufficiently small means
substantially that the system is weakly interacting or in other
words spin are nearly independent.

Now we will now show that
\log Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)
can be written always in term of
a formal series expansion which converges absolutely if
\beta
is
sufficiently small. Such an expansion is called the high
temperature expansion of the lattice system. We will also study
directly (via cluster expansion methods) this series expansion in
the next section.

6.2.2

Polymer representation of
Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)
for the pair potential case

Again, in order to make things easier we will
consider just free boundary condition. This means that the
partition function of our spin system is given by

Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)~=~\int d\mu(\phi_{\Lambda})\exp\left[-\beta\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}\Psi(\{x,y\},\phi_{x},\phi_{y})\right]

[formula]

Let us now use the
“Mayer expansion trick” and rewrite our partition function in the following way

Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)~=~\int d\mu(\phi_{\Lambda})\exp\left[-\beta\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}\Psi(\{x,y\},\phi_{x},\phi_{y})\right]~=~

~=~\int d\mu(\phi_{\Lambda})\prod_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}[(e^{-\beta\Psi(\{x,y\},\phi_{x},\phi_{y})})-1)+1]~=~

~=~1+\int d\mu(\phi_{\Lambda})\sum_{g\in{\cal G}_{\Lambda}}\prod_{\{x,y\}\in E_{g}}(e^{-\beta\Psi(\{x,y\},\phi_{x},\phi_{y})}-1)~=~

where recall that
{\cal G}_{\Lambda}
denotes the set of all graphs

g
with vertex set
\Lambda
(either connected or not connected). Note also that the graph
with empty edge set has been separated and yields the contribution
1 in equation above, since by definition
\int d\mu(\phi_{\Lambda})1~=~1
.
As we did in Section
3.1.2
, we can group together connected components
in
\sum_{g\in{\cal G}_{\Lambda}}
namely

\sum_{g\in{\cal G}_{\Lambda}}~=~\sum_{\{R_{1},\dots R_{k}\}\in\Lambda\atop R_{i}\cap R_{j}~=~\emptyset,~|R_{i}|\geq 2}\prod_{i~=~1}^{k}\sum_{g\in{{G}_{R_{i}}}}

where
\{R_{1},\dots R_{k}\}
is a non ordered
k
-ple of mutually non
intersecting subsets of
\Lambda
with cardinality greater than 2.
Note in fact that the sets with cardinality 1 yields contribution
equal to 1. Now, recalling that
d\mu(\phi_{\Lambda})
is a product
measure, we get

\int d\mu(\phi_{\Lambda})\sum_{g\in{\cal G}_{\Lambda}}\prod_{X\in g}(e^{-\Psi(\{x,y\},\phi_{x},\phi_{y})}-1)~=~\sum_{\{R_{1},\dots R_{k}\}\in\Lambda\atop R_{i}\cap R_{j}~=~\emptyset,~|R_{i}|\geq 2}\rho_{\beta}(R_{1})\dots\rho_{\beta}(R_{k})

with

\rho_{\beta}(R)~=~\int\prod_{x\in R}d\mu(\phi_{x})\sum_{g\in{G}_{R}}\prod_{X\in g}(e^{-\beta\Psi(\{x,y\},\phi_{x},\phi_{y})}-1)

and evidently

\sum_{{\{R_{1},\dots R_{k}\}\in\Lambda\atop R_{i}\cap R_{j}=\emptyset}\atop|R_{i}|\geq 2}~~\rho_{\beta}(R_{1})\dots\rho_{\beta}(R_{k})=~~~~\sum_{k\geq 1}{1\over k!}\sum_{{R_{1},\dots,R_{k}\subset\Lambda\atop R_{i}\cap R_{j}=\emptyset}\atop|R_{i}|\geq 2}~~~~\rho_{\beta}(R_{1})\dots\rho_{\beta}(R_{k})

Namely, if we define for any pair of
sets
R_{i},R_{j}

V(R_{i},R_{j})~=~\begin{cases}0&{\rm if}~R_{i}\cap R_{j}~=~\emptyset\\
+\infty&{\rm if}~R_{i}\cap R_{j}\neq\emptyset\end{cases}

[formula]

We can rewrite the
grand-canonical partition function (3.16) as

Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)~=~1+\sum_{k\geq 1}{1\over k!}\!\sum_{R_{1},\dots,R_{k}\atop|R_{i}|\geq 2}\!\rho_{\beta}(R_{1})\dots\rho_{\beta}(R_{k})e^{-\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}V(R_{i},R_{j})}

[formula]

where

\rho_{\beta}(R)~=~\int\prod_{x\in R}d\mu(\phi_{x})\sum_{g\in{G}_{R}}\prod_{X\in g}(e^{-\beta\Psi(\{x,y\},\phi_{x},\phi_{y})}-1)

[formula]

So we have shown that the
partition function
Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)
defined in (6.21) can always
be written as the grand canonical partition function of a
gas of “polymers” in which polymers are subsets
R
of

\Lambda
with cardinality greater or equal 2, with activity
\rho_{\beta}(R)
given by (6.25),
and with pair interaction given by (6.23), i.e., they cannot overlap.

Threfore,
according to Theorem
4.2
, whenever the condition

\sup_{x\in Z^{d}}\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\mathbb{P}\atop x\in R}|\rho_{\beta}(R)|~e^{a|R|}\leq e^{a}-1

[formula]

is satisfied,
the free energy is analytic in
\beta
.

Note that the information about the original system is contained in the structure of the
activity
\rho_{\beta}(R)
of a given polymer
R
. Note also that
\rho_{\beta}(R)\to 0
as
\beta\to 0
. Hence the activity of
polymers is small when
\beta
is small. So it is reasonable to
expect for convergence of this polymer gas when the parameter

\beta
is sufficiently small. This is the reason why such an
expansion is called the high temperature expansion of the spin
system.

6.2.3

The bounded spin system case

Let us suppose that our spin sysytem is bounded, namely it holds that

\|\phi_{x}\|\leq c<+\infty

[formula]

then we can state the following theorem.

Theorem 6.2

Consider a spin system at inverse temperature
\beta

with partition function
Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)
given by (6.32), interacting via a pair potential satisfying
(6.26) and (6.27). Suppose that (6.39) holds and let
\beta
such that

\beta\leq{0,058\over c^{2}J}

Then

|\Lambda|^{-1}\log Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)
is an
absolutely convergent series uniformly in
\Lambda
.

Remark.
(6.26) and (6.27) are satisfied for pratically all
studied bounded spin system (e.g. Ising-type models, Potts model,
Heisenberg model).

Proof
. All we need to do in order to prove the theorem is to check
condition (6.38). First note that (6.38) is satisfied if

\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}e^{an}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum\limits_{R:\in\mathbb{P}\atop|R|=n,\;x\in R}|\rho_{\beta}(R)|\leq e^{a}-1

[formula]

For that we need need an upper bound on the modulous of the activity

\rho_{\beta}(R)
given in (6.25) when
|R|=n
. To bound
|\rho_{\beta}(R)|
we use Theorem
3.7
and in particular the tree graph inequality
(3.69). Supposing that
R
with cardinality
|R|=n
is the unordered set
\{x_{i},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and setting, for
\{x_{i},x_{j}\}\subset R
,

\Psi(\{x_{i},x_{y}\},\phi_{x_{i}},\phi_{x_{j}})=\Psi_{ij}

we have

\displaystyle|\rho_{\beta}(R)|

\displaystyle=\Bigg|\int\prod_{x\in R}d\mu(\phi_{x})\sum_{g\in{G}_{R}}\prod_{X\in g}(e^{-\beta\Psi(\{x,y\},\phi_{x},\phi_{y})}-1)\Bigg|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}d\mu(\phi_{x_{i}})\sum_{g\in G_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}(e^{-\beta\Psi_{ij}}-1)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}d\mu(\phi_{x_{i}})\bigg|\sum_{g\in G_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}(e^{-\beta\Psi_{ij}}-1)\bigg|

Now observe that in force of (6.28) and (6.39) we have that

\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset R}\Psi(\{x,y\},\phi_{x},\phi_{y})\geq-{c^{2}J\over 2}|R|

i.e., if we pose
R=\{x_{i},\dots,x_{n}\}
and
\Psi(\{x_{i},x_{y}\},\phi_{x_{i}},\phi_{x_{j}})=\Psi_{ij}
, we have that the real numbers
\{\Psi_{ij}\}_{\{i,j\}\in E_{n}}
are such that

|\Psi_{ij}|\leq c^{2}J_{x_{i}x_{j}}

and

\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\Psi_{ij}\geq-{c^{2}J\over 2}n

Therefore, according to Theorem
3.7

\bigg|\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}(e^{-\beta\Psi_{ij}}-1)\bigg|\leq e^{{c^{2}\beta J\over 2}n}\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}(1-e^{-\beta|\Psi_{ij}|})

Therefore, since
\int d\mu(\phi_{x_{i}})=1
, we have

\displaystyle|\rho_{\beta}(R)|

\displaystyle\leq e^{{c^{2}\beta J\over 2}n}\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}(1-e^{-\beta c^{2}J_{x_{i}x_{j}}})

\displaystyle\leq e^{{c^{2}\beta J\over 2}n}(\beta c^{2})^{n-1}\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}J_{x_{i}x_{j}}

and thus

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum\limits_{R:\in\mathbb{P}\atop|R|=n,\;x\in R}|\rho_{\beta}(R)|

\displaystyle\leq e^{{c^{2}\beta J\over 2}n}(\beta c^{2})^{n-1}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum\limits_{\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}\atop x_{1}=x}\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}J_{x_{i}x_{j}}

\displaystyle=e^{{c^{2}\beta J\over 2}n}(\beta c^{2})^{n-1}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}{1\over(n-1)!}\sum_{(x_{i},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{dn}\atop x_{1}=x,\,x_{i}\neq x_{j}}\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}J_{x_{i}x_{j}}

\displaystyle\leq e^{{c^{2}\beta J\over 2}n}{(\beta c^{2})^{n-1}\over(n-1)!}\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{(x_{i},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{dn}\atop x_{1}=x,\,x_{i}\neq x_{j}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}J_{x_{i}x_{j}}

and since (recall Proposition
3.1
and (6.27)), for any
\tau\in T_{n}

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{(x_{i},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{dn}\atop x_{1}=x,\,x_{i}\neq x_{j}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}J_{x_{i}x_{j}}\leq J^{n-1}

[formula]

we get in the end that (recall:
\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}1=n^{n-2}
)

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum\limits_{R:\in\mathbb{P}\atop|R|=n,\;x\in R}|\rho_{\beta}(R)|\leq e^{{c^{2}\beta J\over 2}n}(c^{2}\beta J)^{n-1}{n^{n-2}\over(n-1)!}

Therefore condition (6.40) is satisfied if (as we did earlier, we choose for simplicity
a=\ln 2
and we bound
{n^{n-2}\over(n-1)!}\leq e^{n-1}
)

\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\Big(2e^{{c^{2}\beta J\over 2}}\Big)^{n}(c^{2}e\beta J)^{n-1}\leq 1

i.e., setting
x=c^{2}\beta J

\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\Big(2e^{{x\over 2}}\Big)^{n}(ex)^{n-1}\leq 1

i.e.

2e^{{x\over 2}}\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\Big(2e^{{x\over 2}+1}x\Big)^{n-1}\leq 1

i.e

{4e^{{x}+1}x\over 1-2e^{{x\over 2}+1}x}\leq 1

which is satisfied if

x\leq 0,058
.

In conclusion (6.40) is satisfied for all
\beta
such that

\beta J\leq{0,058\over c^{2}}

which ends the proof of Theorem
6.2
.
\Box

6.2.4

The unbounded spin system case

Concerning unbounded spin system we will prove the following theorem

Theorem 6.3

Consider a spin system at inverse temperature
\beta

with partition function
Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)
given by (6.32), interacting via a pair potential satisfying
(6.26) and (6.27). Suppose that
the norm
\|\cdot\|:\Omega\to[0,+\infty)
is a function such that, for
n\in\mathbb{N}

\int e^{+{\beta J\over 2}\|\phi\|^{2}}\|\phi\|^{n}d\mu(\phi)\leq n![C(\beta J)]^{n}

[formula]

and let
\beta
such that

\beta JC^{2}(\beta J)\leq{e-1\over 4e^{2}}

[formula]

Then
|\Lambda|^{-1}\log Z_{\Lambda}(\beta)
is an absolutely convergent series uniformly in

\Lambda
.

Proof
.

Again, we just need to check the condition (6.38). Thus, given
R\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
such that
|R|\geq 2
, let us first estimate
the modulus of the polymer activity is given by (6.25). We have as before

|\rho_{\beta}(R)|~\leq~\int\prod_{x\in R}d\mu(\phi_{x})\Big|\sum_{g\in G_{R}}\prod_{\{x,y\}\in g}(e^{-\beta\Psi(\{x,y\},\phi_{x},\phi_{y})}-1)\Big|

As before let
R
such that
|R|=n
; so
R
is the unordered set
\{x_{i},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and let set, for
\{x_{i},x_{j}\}\subset R
,

\Psi(\{x_{i},x_{y}\},\phi_{x_{i}},\phi_{x_{j}})=\Psi_{ij}

Thus, as before, we have

|\rho_{\beta}(R)|\leq\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}d\mu(\phi_{x_{i}})\bigg|\sum_{g\in G_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}(e^{-\beta\Psi_{ij}}-1)\bigg|

Now, by (6.26) and (6.28), with

R=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
and
\Psi(\{x_{i},x_{y}\},\phi_{x_{i}},\phi_{x_{j}})=\Psi_{ij}
, we have that the real numbers
\{\Psi_{ij}\}_{\{i,j\}\in E_{n}}
are such that

|\Psi_{ij}|\leq J_{x_{i}x_{j}}\|\phi_{x_{i}}\|\|\phi_{x_{j}}\|

and, for any
S\subset[n]
,

\sum_{\{i,j\}\subset S}\Psi_{ij}\geq-{J\over 2}\sum_{i\in S}\|\phi_{x_{i}}\|^{2}

Therefore, according to Theorem
3.7

\bigg|\sum_{g\in G_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{g}}(e^{-\beta\Psi_{ij}}-1)\bigg|\leq e^{{\beta J\over 2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\phi_{x_{i}}\|^{2}}\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}(1-e^{-\beta J_{x_{i}x_{j}}\|\phi_{x_{i}}\|\|\phi_{x_{j}}\|})

Now we get

\displaystyle|\rho_{\beta}(R)|

\displaystyle\leq\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}d\mu(\phi_{x_{i}})e^{{\beta J\over 2}\|\phi_{x_{i}}\|^{2}}\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}(1-e^{-\beta J_{x_{i}x_{j}}\|\phi_{x_{i}}\|\|\phi_{x_{j}}\|})

\displaystyle\leq\beta^{n-1}\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}}\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}d\mu(\phi_{x_{i}})e^{{\beta J\over 2}\|\phi_{x_{i}}\|^{2}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}J_{x_{i}x_{j}}\|\phi_{x_{i}}\|\|\phi_{x_{j}}\|

Then observe that if
\tau\in T_{n}
has degrees
d_{1},\dots d_{n}
at vertices
1,2,\dots,n
respectively, then

\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}\|\phi_{x_{i}}\|\|\phi_{x_{j}}\|=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\|\phi_{i}\|^{d_{i}}

and thus

|\rho_{\beta}(R)|\leq b^{n-1}\!\!\!\!\sum_{d_{1},\dots,d_{n}:\;d_{i}\geq 1\atop d_{1}+\dots+d_{n}=2n-2}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\int d\mu(\phi_{x_{i}})e^{{\beta J\over 2}\|\phi_{x_{i}}\|^{2}}\|\phi_{x_{i}}\|^{d_{i}}\!\!\!\!\sum_{\tau\in T_{n}\atop d_{1},\dots,d_{n}~{\rm fixed}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{\tau}}J_{x_{i}x_{j}}

whence, using again (6.41), and recalling also Cayley formula (3.5), we get

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum\limits_{R:\in\mathbb{P}\atop|R|=n,\;x\in R}|\rho_{\beta}(R)|

\displaystyle\leq{(\beta J)^{n-1}\over n-1}\sum_{d_{1},\dots,d_{n}:\;d_{i}\geq 1\atop d_{1}+\dots+d_{n}=2n-2}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\int d\mu(\phi_{x_{i}}){\|\phi_{x_{i}}\|^{d_{i}}e^{{\beta J\over 2}\|\phi_{x_{i}}\|^{2}}\over(d_{1}-1)!}

\displaystyle\leq{(\beta J)^{n-1}\over n-1}\sum_{d_{1},\dots,d_{n}:\;d_{i}\geq 1\atop d_{1}+\dots+d_{n}=2n-2}\prod_{i=1}^{n}d_{i}C(\beta J)^{d_{i}}

\displaystyle\leq{(\beta J)^{n-1}\over n-1}[C(\beta J)]^{2n-2}\sum_{d_{1},\dots,d_{n}:\;d_{i}\geq 1\atop d_{1}+\dots+d_{n}=2n-2}\prod_{i=1}^{n}d_{i}

where in the second inequality we have used hypothesis (6.42). Observe now that

\displaystyle\sum_{d_{1},\dots,d_{n}:\;d_{i}\geq 1\atop d_{1}+\dots+d_{n}=2n-2}\prod_{i=1}^{n}d_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s_{1},\dots,s_{n}:\;s_{i}\geq 0\atop s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}=n-2}\prod_{i=1}^{n}(1+s_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s_{1},\dots,s_{n}:\;s_{i}\geq 0\atop s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}=n-2}\prod_{i=1}^{n}e^{s_{i}}

\displaystyle=e^{n-2}\sum_{d_{1},\dots,d_{n}:\;d_{i}\geq 1\atop d_{1}+\dots+d_{n}=2n-2}1

\displaystyle=e^{n-2}{2n-3\choose n-1}

\displaystyle\leq(4e)^{n-1}

Therefore we get

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum\limits_{R:\in\mathbb{P}\atop|R|=n,\;x\in R}|\rho_{\beta}(R)|\leq{(4e\beta J)^{n-1}\over n-1}[C(\beta J)]^{2n-2}

In conclusion we have that condition (6.38) is satisfied if (as we did earlier, we choose for simplicity
a=\ln 2
)

\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}{(4e\beta JC^{2}(\beta J))^{n-1}\over n-1}\leq 1

i.e. if

-\ln[1-4e\beta JC^{2}(\beta J)]\leq 1

i.e. if

\beta JC^{2}(\beta J)\leq{e-1\over 4e^{2}}

This concludes the proof of Theorem
6.3
.
\Box

6.3

Discrete Spin systems with many-body interactions

We have found a general condition, the equation
(4.82), that guarantees the absolute convergence uniformly in
\Lambda

of
\Sigma^{\Lambda}_{x}
, and hence of
\log\Xi_{\Lambda}(\rho)
.
The ways we found so far to check that the polymer activities
satisfy (4.82) may be used only for pair interaction.
We have now to face a new problem: starting from
the structure of the polymer activity
\rho_{\gamma}

in the case of general many body interactions, which is given by

\rho_{\gamma}~=~\int\prod_{x\in\gamma}d\mu(\phi_{x})\sum_{g\in{G}_{\gamma}}\prod_{X\in g}(e^{-\beta\Psi(X,\phi_{X})}-1)

[formula]

we want to give general criteria in order to satisfy the condition (4.82),
that in this case take the form

\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{V}}\atop x\in\gamma}|\rho_{\gamma}|~e^{a|\gamma|}\leq e^{a}-1

[formula]

Here we want to present a simple technical device,
introduced in 1981 by M. Cassandro and E. Olivieri,
which exploits the fact that for bounded spin systems on the
lattice the sum over the Mayer graphs can be directly bounded,
for
N
body potentials
absolutely summable in an appropriate sense.
This method has two main advantages: it is relatively simple,
since it avoids the combinatorial complexity arising in the tree
identities presented above,
and it may be applied to a large class of discrete systems.
In particular, for systems with many-body interactions it is difficult
to obtain better estimates of the interactions
\beta\Psi(X,\phi_{X})
for which
(6.45) is satisfied.

The method can be also applied to systems with two body interactions but
the results are obviously far from optimal, as we shall see below.

Theorem 6.4
(Cassandro, Olivieri)

Consider the polymer activity (6.44).
If it is possible to give for any connected graph

g
such that
{\rm supp}\ g=n
the following bound

\int\prod_{x\in{\rm supp}\ g}d\mu(\phi_{x})\prod_{X\in g}\left|e^{-\beta\Psi(X,\phi)}-1\right|\leq{\bar{\sigma}}^{n}\prod_{X\in g}W(X)

[formula]

with

\sum_{X\ni x}W(X)\leq K,\qquad\qquad{\bar{\sigma}}<1

[formula]

and the following relation between
{\bar{\sigma}}
and
K
holds

K<{|\log\bar{\sigma}|\over 4}

[formula]

then the condition (6.45) is satisfied for some
a>0
.

Remark

It is important, in order to be able to apply this theorem to concrete models,
to understand the structure of the estimate needed in order to satisfy (6.45).
Given a graph, the contribution of that graph to the polymer activity have to be
bounded by the product of a vertex contribution
\bar{\sigma}<1
, meaning that
the bigger
{\rm supp}\ g
the smaller its contribution, and an edge contribution

J(X)
, whose estimate has to be suitably related to
\bar{\sigma}
.

Proof
.

We will bound directly l.h.s. of (6.45)

\sum_{\gamma\ni x}|\rho_{\gamma}|e^{a|\gamma|}=\sum_{\gamma\ni x}e^{a|\gamma|}\left|\int\prod_{x\in\gamma}d\mu(\phi_{x})\sum_{g\in{\cal G}_{\gamma}}\prod_{X\in g}[e^{-\beta\Psi(X,\phi)}-1]\right|\leq

\leq\sum_{\gamma\ni x}\sum_{g\in{\cal G}_{\gamma}}{\bar{\sigma}}^{|\gamma|}e^{a|\gamma|}\prod_{X\in g}W(X)\leq\sum_{\gamma\ni x}\sum_{g\in{\cal G}_{\gamma}}{\sigma}^{|\gamma|}\prod_{X\in g}W(X)

[formula]

Hence the theorem follows if we are able to show
the inequality

F(\sigma)=\sum_{\gamma\ni x}\sum_{g\in{\cal G}_{\gamma}}{\sigma}^{|\gamma|}\prod_{X\in g}W(X)<e^{a}-1

[formula]

where

\sigma={\bar{\sigma}}e^{a}

We now rewrite the sum over connected graphs
g

passing through the lattice point
x
using Cassandro-
Olivieri hierarchy.
The latter is constructed observing that, given a connected graph

g
, its links can be always ordered, for some
positive integer
t
, in the following way

g=\{C_{0},C^{1}_{1},\dots,C^{1}_{k_{1}},\dots,C^{t}_{1},\dots,C^{t}_{k_{t}}\}

where
C_{0}
is a link of
g
such that
x\in C_{0}
.
For
1\leq s\leq t
,
the links
C^{s}_{1},\dots,C^{s}_{k_{s}}
represent the

s^{\rm th}
hierarchy and we have the following relations
between different hierarchies:

C^{s}_{i}\cap\left[\cup_{i=1}^{k_{s-1}}C^{s-1}_{i}\right]\neq\emptyset

C^{s}_{i}\cap\left[\cup_{l=0}^{s-2}\cup_{i=1}^{k_{l}}C^{l}_{i}\right]=\emptyset

[formula]

Thus the first hierarchy

C^{1}_{1},\dots,C^{1}_{k_{1}}

of links of
g
is the collection of
all links of
g
which have a non empty intersection with
C_{0}
;
the second hierarchy
C^{2}_{1},\dots,C^{2}_{k_{2}}
is the collection
of links of
g
which have a non empty intersection with

\cup_{i}C^{1}_{i}
, but has an empty intersection with the set
C_{0}
,
and so on.

We will also denote, for
s\geq 1

\Delta_{s}=\cup_{i=1}^{k_{s}}C^{s}_{i}\backslash\left[\left(\cup_{i=1}^{k_{s}}C^{s}_{i}\right)\cap\left(\cup_{i=1}^{k_{s-1}}C^{s-1}_{i}\right)\right]

[formula]

while
\Delta_{0}=C_{0}
.
The set
\Delta_{s}
represents therefore the set of the new
points reached by the
s
-th hierarchy. By definition

|\Delta_{s}|>0
for
s<t
, because the bonds of the
(s+1)^{\rm th}

hierarchy have intersection only with the set
\Delta_{s}
,
else they would belong to an earlier hierarchy.
Note that
\{\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1},\dots,\Delta_{t}\}
is a
partition of the set
{\rm supp}\ g
and thus

|\Delta_{0}|+|\Delta_{1}|+\cdots+|\Delta_{t}|=|{\rm supp}\ g|

[formula]

thus, for a given
g=\{C_{0},C^{1}_{1},\dots,C^{1}_{k_{1}},\dots,C^{t}_{1},\dots,C^{t}_{k_{t}}\}
, we can always write

\sigma^{|{\rm supp}\ g|}\leq\sigma^{|\Delta_{0}|}\sigma^{|\Delta_{1}|}\cdots\sigma^{|\Delta_{t-1}|}

Hence we can get the estimate

F(\sigma)\leq\sum_{C_{0}\ni 0}W(C_{0})\sigma^{|C_{0}|}+\sum_{C_{0}\ni 0}W(C_{0})\sigma^{|\Delta_{0}|}\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{\infty}\sum_{C^{1}_{1},\dots C^{1}_{k_{1}}}\prod_{j=1}^{k_{1}}W(C^{1}_{j})\sigma^{|\Delta_{1}|}\cdots

\cdots\sum_{k_{t-1}=1}^{\infty}\sum_{C^{t-1}_{1},\dots C^{t-1}_{k_{t-1}}}\prod_{j=1}^{k_{t-1}}W(C^{t-1}_{j})\sigma^{|\Delta_{t-1}|}\sum_{k_{t}=1}^{\infty}\sum_{C^{t}_{1},\dots C^{t}_{k_{t}}}\prod_{j=1}^{k_{t}}W(C^{t}_{j})

[formula]

Let us start to perform the last sum in r.h.s. of (6.54),
i.e. the sum over the
t^{\rm th}
hierarchy, supposing to keep
fixed all sets concerning the previous hierarchies.
We obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{k_{t}=1}^{\infty}\sum_{C^{t}_{1},\dots C^{t}_{k_{t}}}\prod_{j=1}^{k_{t}}W(C^{t}_{j})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k_{t}=1}^{\infty}{1\over k_{t}!}\left(\sum_{C:\,C\cap\Delta_{t-1}\neq\emptyset}W(C)\right)^{k_{t}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k_{t}=1}^{\infty}{1\over k_{t}!}\left(|\Delta_{t-1}|K\right)^{k_{t}}

\displaystyle\leq e^{K|\Delta_{t-1}|}-1

where the sum
\sum_{C:\,C\cap\Delta_{t-1}\neq\emptyset}W(C)
is bounded by fixing one point in
\Delta_{t-1}

(and this gives the factor
|\Delta_{t-1}|
)
and then by summing over all
C
passing for such point.
Hence

F(\sigma)\leq\sum_{C_{0}\ni 0}W(C_{0})\sigma^{|C_{0}|}+\sum_{C_{0}\ni 0}W(C_{0})\sigma^{|\Delta_{0}|}\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{\infty}\sum_{C^{1}_{1},\dots C^{1}_{k_{1}}}\prod_{j=1}^{k_{1}}W(C^{1}_{j})\sigma^{|\Delta_{1}|}\cdots

\cdots\sum_{k_{t-1}=1}^{\infty}\sum_{C^{t-1}_{1},\dots C^{t-1}_{k_{t-1}}}\prod_{j=1}^{k_{t-1}}W(C^{t-1}_{j})\sigma^{|\Delta_{t-1}|}(e^{K|\Delta_{t-1}|}-1)

Note now that
e^{K}\sigma<1

certainly holds if (6.48) does and
a
is choosen small enough,
namely
a<(3/4)|\log{\bar{\sigma}}|
.
To find a bound of the maximum of
the factor
\sigma^{|\Delta_{t-1}|}(e^{K|\Delta_{t-1}|}-1)

it is convenient to write

\sigma^{|\Delta_{t-1}|}(e^{K|\Delta_{t-1}|}-1)=e^{(-|\log\sigma|+K)|\Delta_{t-1}|)}(1-e^{-K|\Delta_{t-1}|})

The maximum in
|\Delta_{t-1}|
of this function is assumed for
|\Delta_{t-1}|=x
such that

e^{-Kx}={|\log\sigma|-k\over|\log\sigma|}
, and using
e^{K}\sigma<1
and hence

e^{(-|\log\sigma|+K)|\Delta_{t-1}|)}<1
, this gives easily

\sigma^{|\Delta_{t-1}|}(e^{K|\Delta_{t-1}|}-1)\leq{K\over|\log\sigma|}

We sum now the next hierarchy
t-1
as before keeping fixed
everything concerning the previous hierarchies and obtain

\sum_{k_{t-1}=1}^{\infty}\sum_{C^{t-1}_{1},\dots C^{t-1}_{k_{t}}}\prod_{j=1}^{k_{t-1}}W(C^{t-1}_{j})\sigma^{|\Delta_{t-1}|}(e^{K|\Delta_{t-1}|}-1)

\leq{K\over|\log\sigma|}\sum_{k_{t-1}=1}^{\infty}{1\over k_{t-1}!}\left(\sum_{C:\,C\cap\Delta_{t-2}\neq\emptyset}W(C)\right)^{k_{t}}\leq{K\over|\log\sigma|}(e^{K|\Delta_{t-2}|}-1)

Iterating until
t=1
, we obtain

F(\sigma)\leq\sum_{C_{0}\ni 0}W(C_{0})\sigma^{|C_{0}|}+\sum_{C_{0}\ni 0}W(C_{0})\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\left({K\over|\log\sigma|}\right)^{t}\leq K\left[\sigma+{K\over|\log\sigma|-K}\right]

hence we get (6.45), and then the proof of the theorem,
if the following condition is satisfied

K\left[\sigma+{K\over|\log\sigma|-K}\right]<e^{a}-1

[formula]

The relation (6.55) give a condition between
K
,
a
and
\bar{\sigma}
.
If for instance one suppose to know the value of
\bar{\sigma}
, the problem is
to find the value of
a
which maximizes the constant
K
. This problem
can be solved numerically. A rough estimate, once the value of
\bar{\sigma}

is fixed, is to
choose e.g.
a=(1/2)|\log{\bar{\sigma}}|
, which corresponds to
the choice

\sigma=\sqrt{\bar{\sigma}}
(i.e.
|\log\sigma|={1\over 2}|\log{\bar{\sigma}}|
),
obtaining easily

K<{|\log\bar{\sigma}|\over 4}

[formula]

\Box

6.4

Some applications of theorem
6.4

6.4.1

The Ising model

A rough but fast estimate of the analyticity region in
\beta
(high temperature regime)
of the partition function
of the Ising model can be obtained using theorem
6.4
.

In order to apply theorem
6.4
to the partition function (6.7) we have to
find
\bar{\sigma}
and
J
such that

\int\prod_{x\in{\rm supp}\ g}d\mu(\sigma_{x})\prod_{\{x,y\}\in g\atop x,y~{\rm near.~neigh.}}\left|e^{\beta J\sigma_{x}\sigma_{y}}-1\right|\leq{\bar{\sigma}}^{n}\prod_{\{x,y\}\in g}W

[formula]

This can be done observing that, for
\beta<1
,
\left|e^{\beta J\sigma_{x}\sigma_{y}}-1\right|<2\beta J
.
The l.h.s. of (6.57) can be estimated then as
(2\beta J)^{|E_{g}|}
. Since
|E_{g}|\geq n-1
and
n\geq 2
,
we can choose
\bar{\sigma}=(\beta J)^{1/3}
,
J=2(\beta J)^{1/3}
and then, having each site
2d
nearest neighbors,

K=4d(\beta J)^{1/3}
. The condition (6.48) becomes then

4d(\beta J)^{1/3}<{|\log(\beta J)|\over 12}\Rightarrow{(\beta J)^{1/3}\over|\log(\beta J)|}<{1\over 48d}

[formula]

which is satisfied for
(\beta J)<1
small enough.
By a rapid computation
(6.58) is satisfied by
\beta\approx 4\times 10^{-5}
.
However, we will see in the next chapter that this estimate is far from optimal.

6.4.2

Direct proof of the Israel theorem

Discrete spin systems with many body interactions have been studied since the early
’70. After the pioneering work by Gallavotti and Miracle-Sole, which proved analiticity
of the logarithm of the partition function when
the spin space state has only a finite number of values, Israel proved in [I] a general
